## Page 7


Abstract
This thesis is devoted to the study of hyperbolic differential operators on globally hyper-
bolic Lorentzian manifolds, linear field-theoretic models exhibiting a gauge symmetry, and their
quantisation.
In the first part, we treat the Cauchy problem for symmetric hyperbolic systems and nor-
mally hyperbolic operators on globally hyperbolic manifolds from first principles, complemented
by many examples and a discussion of Green hyperbolicity. Although hyperbolic equations are
usually studied in the context of local interactions, there are strong motivations from several
areas of mathematical physics to consider also nonlocal interactions.
As an intermezzo, we
therefore take a small deviation from the classical local theory and prove well-posedness of
the Cauchy problem for symmetric hyperbolic systems coupled to a broad class of nonlocal
potentials, with applications, for instance, to the Maxwell equations in linear dispersive media.
The subsequent part presents a detailed exposition of linear gauge theories in globally hy-
perbolic spacetimes. Linear gauge theories are yet another deviation from the concept of hyper-
bolicity: the corresponding equations of motion are generically non-hyperbolic, however, can
always be reduced to a constrained hyperbolic dynamics once an appropriate gauge fixing pro-
cedure has been applied. In particular, we give a thorough analysis of their Cauchy problems
and construct the corresponding classical phase space. A central part of this section is the pre-
sentation of several examples of direct physical interest, some of which have not appeared yet
in the literature in this context. Moreover, we explain how linear gauge theories are quantised
following the algebraic approach to quantum field theory, which offers a mathematically rigorous
quantisation scheme, ideally suited for field theories defined on Lorentzian backgrounds. In par-
ticular, we introduce the notion of Hadamard states, which are physically distinguished states
whose two-point function has a specific singularity structure as a bidistribution, and provide a
detailed bibliographic review of existence results for such states.
The final chapter of this thesis is devoted to the quantisation of Maxwell’s theory on glob-
ally hyperbolic spacetimes.
After a detailed discussion of the Cauchy and gauge problems
for Maxwell’s theory on Lorentzian manifolds, the central goal is to prove the existence of
Hadamard states for Maxwell’s theory on any globally hyperbolic spacetime. The novelty of
our approach lies in a new gauge-fixing procedure at the level of initial data, which allows us to
suppress all the unphysical degrees of freedom. This gauge is achieved by means of a new Hodge
decomposition for differential k-forms in Sobolev spaces on complete (possibly non-compact)
Riemannian manifolds. Using tools from microlocal analysis, we explicitly construct Hadamard
states on ultrastatic spacetimes for the completely gauge-fixed theory and define states obtained
as the pull-back thereof along the gauge-fixing projector, while ensuring that this construction
preserves the Hadamard property. For general spacetimes, we employ a deformation argument.
This thesis is complemented by an appendix that, alongside other supplementary materials,
provides a self-contained introduction to microlocal analysis and pseudodifferential calculus.

## Page 9


Acknowledgements
Much like any good proof in mathematics that is based on lemmata and proposition, this
thesis was not built in isolation. It relies on many essential steps, both scientifically and per-
sonally, each contributed by the people I was, and still am, fortunate to learn from.
First and foremost, I would like to express my deepest gratitude to my supervisors Simone
Murro and Matteo Capoferri for all their teachings, their patience and their unwavering support
and guidance throughout this journey. Simone was my main supervisor in Genoa and I could
not have asked for a better mentor and Sensei. He invested an incredible amount of time, always
finding a moment to discuss any doubts or questions, often leaving everything aside, no matter
how busy he was. He supported me in every single step of my academic journey and gave me
countless many opportunities. I am deeply grateful not only for his guidance and support, but
also for his deep care that went far beyond what I could have expected. Whether through his
broad interests and knowledge, or through his originality in approaching problems and many
mathematical ideas, I learned an incredible amount from him, both in mathematics and about
research in general. Grazie mille Simo!
I met Matteo during my three-month visit to the Heriot-Watt University in Edinburgh,
and from the very first second he made me feel welcome and supported. With his gentle and
genuinely kind soul, he always created an atmosphere in which I felt comfortable, encouraged,
and inspired.
He was constantly ready to help, whether with research questions, ideas, or
simply with reassuring words when needed. His clarity, rigorous approach to science and the
way in which he tackled every problem taught me a lot, both academically and personally. I
am sincerely grateful for all his support, his patience, and last but certainly not least, all his
academic advice and feedback on applications and related topics towards the end of my PhD.
Grazie mille anche a te Matteo!
I am not sure how these past three years in Genoa would have been without my best
peers, fellow PhD students, and friends, Luca Fiorindo and Stefano Galanda. I was already
virtually in touch with Stefano shortly before the start of our PhD. On the first day, the 2nd of
November 2022, we decided to meet in front of the department. However, it was raining, so I
let him know that I would rather wait inside. With his response, “Only a man with a degree in
mathematical physics could have come up with such a clever decision”, a wonderful friendship
began. We met Luca on that very same day, and with his genuinely kind soul, unshakable
positivity, and occasional singing in the office that echoed through the hallways, he would not
only become a vital part of the department, but – despite his algebras being commutative – also
a wonderful friend for both of us. It was clear that nothing could separate the three of us, the
very latest after our first hike on the 6th of November to the forti around Genoa. Throughout
the years, the (Superior-)Tirol, Südtirol, Veneto-trio shared countless adventures, movie and
gaming nights, holidays to each of our home towns, as well as constant and invaluable support
both on an academic and personal level though up and downs during this journey, making even
the toughest days memorable. Their friendship, humour, and unwavering encouragement were
invaluable, both in my academic life and beyond. I will leave Genoa not only with research
experience gained, but also with many memories and with two friends for life. I could definitely
not have asked for better companions for this journey and I am looking forward to the many
years to come.
The community of PhD students in Genoa felt like a little family, bringing life to the depart-
ment through many initiatives, such as the PhD seminars or informal welcoming meetings for
new PhD students each year, but also through their friendship and positive energy. I was truly
happy to be part of it. I want to especially mention Ludovica Buelli, Laura Carini, Edoardo
D’Angelo, Emilien Ouvrard and Andrea Poggio, with whom I shared countless memorable mo-
ments filled with laughter, warmth, and kindness. I will always treasure them and they continue
to brighten my life whenever I think back.

## Page 10


When I first arrived in Genoa in November 2022, I immediately felt at home and warmly
welcomed by the wonderful members of the Mathematical Physics research group, who, at
various times over the past three years, included, Marco Benini, Edoardo D’Angelo, Nicolò
Drago, Pietro Falzoni, Stefano Galanda, Roberta Iseppi, Pierre Martinetti, Simone Murro,
Giorgio Musante, Gaston Nieuviarts, Emilien Ouvrard, Nicola Pinamonti, Andrea Pistolesi,
Stefano Rosarin and Peem Ubonsri. I am deeply grateful to everyone for making these past
three years such a memorable and joyful experience, through scientific discussions, our daily
lunch and coffee breaks, and all the social dinners and other occasions we shared. Thank you
for your kindness, support, and for creating such an inspiring environment. Moreover, I would
like to extend my acknowledgements to the other department members I had the opportunity
to interact with. In particular, I would like to mention Tommaso Bruno and Andrea Carbonaro
for discussions and support.
During my time as a PhD student, I was very glad to have had the chance to travel exten-
sively, both for conferences and research visits. In my second year, I spent three months at the
Heriot-Watt University in Edinburgh, visiting Matteo Capoferri. I want to take this opportu-
nity to thank the whole analysis group for their warm and kind hospitality. In this regards, I
want especially express my gratitude to Lyonell Boulton for inspiring discussions at numerous
occasions. In my final year, I had the opportunity to visit Felix Finster and his group in Regens-
burg. I felt immediately very welcome in his group, which at the time consisted of Marco van
den Beld-Serrano, Patrick Fischer, Shane Farnsworth, and Claudio Paganini. I want to take this
opportunity to thank Felix for his support and all the inspiring discussions, as well as his group
for their warm hospitality and for the new friendships that made the visit truly unforgettable.
Directly after Regensburg, I spent one month in Vienna working on a research-in-teams project
together with Lars Andersson, Benjamin Moser, Marius Oancea, and Claudio Paganini. My
time in Vienna was equally filled with countless enjoyable moments and stimulating discussions
and I want to express my gratitude for the research team that made this possible.
In addition, I undertook several shorter research visits, namely to Jan Sbierski in Edinburgh,
as well as to the mathematical physics groups in Trento, in particular, to Nicolò Drago, Paolo
Meda, Valter Moretti, Lorenzo Pettinari and Carmine De Rosa, and to Pavia, visiting Claudio
Dappiaggi and his group consisting of Alberto Bonicelli, Beatrice Costeri, Paolo Rinaldi, Raman
Deep Singh, and Luca Sinibaldi, among others. Thank you all for your hospitality, countless
discussions and the personal kindness that made these visits truly memorable. Additionally, I
had the chance to discuss with many other researchers, either at conferences or during their visits
to Genoa, and I especially want to thank Christian Gérard and Nicolas Ginoux for inspiring
discussions at several occasions.
Having attended many conferences, workshops, and summer schools over the past years, I
have encountered many people I cannot mention individually. Nevertheless, I am deeply grateful
to all of them for their support, inspiring conversations, and friendship throughout my PhD. I
have made many friends along the way, and their encouragement and camaraderie have meant
a great deal to me. I am always happy to meet them again at conferences, where seeing familiar
faces over the years has made this community feel like a second family.
More broadly, I would also like to express my deep gratitude to the many people who sup-
ported my academic journey even before I began my PhD in Genoa. I would specifically like to
mention my Bachelor thesis supervisor, Anita Reimer, as well as my Master thesis supervisors,
Daniele Oriti and Christophe Goeller. Under their guidance, I gained my first research expe-
rience and all of them supported me wholeheartedly in my applications and academic career,
ensuring that I could continue pursuing the path I wished to follow. At this stage, I would like
to extend my gratitude to all the professors I have had the chance to learn from, and to the
countless scientists who came before me, whose work I was able to study and build upon. We
are truly standing on the shoulders of giants, and none of this work would have been possible

Conventions and Notations
Basic Conventions:
• The end of a proof is denoted by the tombstone (or Halmos symbol) ■. The end of an
example or a remark is denoted by a black triangle ▲.
• The terms function and map will be used synonymously, as opposed to some authors,
which use map for specific types of functions (e.g. linear maps,. . .).
Set Theory:
• Usually, we shall use the symbol K (from German Körper for (algebraic) field) when we
specifically work with either R or C, if not explicitly stated otherwise.
• N := {1, 2, . . . } and N0 := N ∪{0}.
• If X is an arbitrary set, then we denote the identity function by idX : X →X, x 7→x or
simply id if it is clear from the context which set is used.
• X ⊂Y :⇔∀x ∈X : x ∈Y, i.e. X ⊂Y does also include the case X = Y. In other words, we
do not distinguish between ⊆and ⊂unlike other authors. Instead, if we explicitly want
to exclude the case X = Y, we use the notation X ⊊Y.
• We will sometimes employ the multi-index notation, i.e. for α = (α1, . . . , αd) ∈Nd and
x = (x1, . . . , xd) ∈Rd, d ∈N, we write
|α| :=

Differential and pseudo-Riemannian Geometry:
• A manifold is a second-countable locally Euclidean Hausdorff space and hence in particular
regular, normal, paracompact and metrisable. Furthermore, all our manifolds are usually
assumed to be smooth and without boundary if not explicitly stated otherwise.
• If M and N are two smooth manifolds and f ∈C∞(M, N) a smooth map, we denote its
(total) differential at a point p ∈M by dpf : TpM →Tf(p)N.

we will use the following special notation for the modules of vector and covector fields:

## Page 21


Introduction
Hyperbolic partial differential equations play a fundamental role in mathematical physics,
as they model many physical phenomena, particularly those involving wave propagation, causal-
ity, and finite propagation speed of information.4 Their mathematical structure allows for the
formulation of a well-posed initial value problem, meaning that suitable initial data assigned at
a fixed moment in time uniquely determine the evolution of the system in a stable manner. At
the same time, hyperbolicity permits the construction of so-called advanced and retarded Green
operators, which are fundamental solutions encoding the causal propagation of disturbances.
These features, in turn, are indispensable for the formulation of relativistic field theories, where
the causal structure of the underlying Lorentzian spacetime imposes strict constraints on how
information may propagate. As a result, hyperbolic equations arise naturally in a wide range
of classical and quantum field theories, such as the Dirac and Klein–Gordon equations.
Besides these basic examples, there is another fundamental class of field-theoretic models,
which are mathematically different in nature, namely gauge theories, characterised by a dy-
namics that is constrained by the invariance under a local symmetry transformation. Being
responsible for mediating the fundamental forces of nature via interactions with charged par-
ticles, those kind of theories play a major role in our modern understanding of the universe.
Examples range from Maxwell’s theory of electromagnetism, the celebrated Yang-Mills theory,
with applications both to elementary particle physics and pure mathematics, from the Einstein
equations of general relativity, to Chern-Simons theory with applications, for instance, in con-
densed matter physics. In particular, gauge theories are of indispensable importance in both
of the two cornerstones of modern mathematical physics, namely Einstein’s theory of general
relativity, the basic theory underlying the standard model of cosmology, as well as the standard
model of particle physics, which lies within the realm of (relativistic) quantum field theory.
The presence of a gauge symmetry, however, implies that the associated equations of motion
are generically non-hyperbolic in nature and hence fail to yield a well-posed Cauchy problem. In
particular, without further modifications, one cannot construct advanced and retarded Green
operators, objects that, as mentioned above, are indispensable for the analytic treatment of
wave equations and for the construction of quantum field theories on curved Lorentzian space-
times. This difficulty is typically resolved by imposing a suitable gauge-fixing condition, such
as the Lorenz gauge in Maxwell’s theory or the harmonic/de Donder gauge in general relativity
and linearised gravity. Upon fixing a suitable gauge, the equations of motion acquire a hy-
perbolic character, thereby restoring well-posedness and enabling the definition of the relevant
Green operators. At the same time, the physical content of the classical theory is encoded in
gauge-invariant observables, ensuring that the resulting description remains consistent with the
underlying symmetry principles and gauge redundancies.
4 For the sake of readability, this introductory chapter, kept at a general level, contains only minimal citations.
A detailed bibliography with pointers to the literature is provided in the following chapters.

Introduction
In the setting of linear field-theoretic models, an abstract and axiomatic formalism for gauge
theories on globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifolds, containing essentially all the examples of
interest for physical applications, has been initially developed by Hack-Schenkel [231] and further
refined by Gérard-Wrochna [198]. Within this formalism, the dynamics of a linear gauge theory
is described by two linear differential operators
P: Γ∞(E2) →Γ∞(E2) ,
K: Γ∞(E1) →Γ∞(E2) ,
acting on smooth sections of the Hermitian vector bundles (E1, ⟨·, ·⟩E1) and (E2, ⟨·, ·⟩E2) defined
over a globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifold (M, g), with the property that P ◦K = 0. The
operator P, assumed to be formally self-adjoint, parametrises the dynamical equations of motion
of the gauge theory in question, while the operator K encodes the gauge transformations via
Γ∞(E2) ∋ψ 7→ψ+Kω for ω ∈Γ∞(E1). By definition, these transformations leave the equations
of motion invariant, i.e. Pψ = P(ψ + Kω). One further assumes that the two operators
D1 := K∗K: Γ∞(E1) →Γ∞(E1) ,
D2 := P + KK∗: Γ∞(E2) →Γ∞(E2)
are hyperbolic.
The operators D2 is the gauge-fixed operator with respect to the canonical
gauge condition K∗ψ = 0 for ψ ∈Γ∞(E2), where K∗denotes the formal adjoint with respect to
(·, ·)Ei =
R
M⟨·, ·⟩Ei dµg, while the role of D1 is to parametrise the remaining gauge freedom in the
sense that a gauge transformation ψ 7→ψ+Kω with ω ∈Γ∞(E1) preserves the condition K∗ψ = 0
if and only if ω ∈ker(D1). Hyperbolicity of D1 ensures that the space of remaining gauge freedom
is well-behaved and, moreover, guarantees that the canonical gauge condition can always be
achieved. Indeed, for every ψ ∈Γ∞(E2), finding a gauge transformation ω ∈Γ∞(E1) such that
K∗(ψ + Kω) = 0 is equivalent to finding a solution to the hyperbolic equation D1ω = −K∗ω.
Now, as already mentioned above, the operator P of a (non-trivial, i.e. K ̸= 0) linear gauge
theory is generically non-hyperbolic. Indeed, the presence of a gauge symmetry implies that
solutions to the equation Pψ = ϕ for a given source ϕ and initial data can never be unique,
if they exist, as one can always perform a gauge transformation to obtain another, distinct,
solution.
Nevertheless, thanks to the gauge symmetry present, one can always reduce the
discussion to the analysis of the hyperbolic operator D2. Indeed, from a physics perspective,
we are interested in the quotient space in which we identify two solutions of Pψ = 0 whenever
they differ by a gauge transformation and, continuing the above discussion, this space of gauge
equivalent classes can be identified with a suitable quotient space of solutions to the hyperbolic
problem D2ψ = 0 with the gauge constraints K∗ψ = 0. Schematically, we have

gauge condition K∗ψ = 0 and by defining a suitable notion of gauge transformation on the level
of initial data to account for the remaining gauge freedom.
From a physical perspective, we are interested in studying the classical phase space, that
is, the space of linear, on-shell, gauge-invariant observables. In the context of the axiomatic
formalism presented above, this is the space

which is gauge-invariant in the sense that O[ψ](ϕ + Kω) = O[ψ](ϕ) for all ω ∈Γ∞(E1). The

Having understood the classical theory, the next step consists of quantisation. Within this
context, we employ the algebraic approach to quantum field theory, which offers a mathe-
matically rigorous quantisation scheme, ideally suited for describing field theories on curved
Lorentzian backgrounds. In this framework, quantisation consists of a two-step procedure:
(i) First, one assigns a unital ∗-algebra CCR(Vc, σ) of observables to the classical phase space
(Vc, σ), which encodes structural properties such as causality, dynamics, and the canonical
commutation relations [Φ(v), Φ∗(w)] = σ(v, w)1. Here the quantum fields Φ(v), Φ∗(v) with
v ∈Vc are the generators of the algebra, and 1 is the algebra unit.
(ii) Next, one identifies a physical state ω, namely a positive, linear and normalised functional
on the algebra CCR(Vc, σ). Among the plethora of mathematically well-defined states,
not all of them can be considered physical and hence, one usually considers only those
satisfying the so-called Hadamard condition. This condition ensures the correct short-
distance behaviour of the n-point functions, mimicking the ultraviolet singularity structure
of the Minkowski vacuum, and plays a key role in the perturbative approach to quantum
field theory. Indeed, it implies the finiteness of the quantum fluctuations of the expectation
value of every observable, and it allows one to construct Wick polynomials and other
nonlinear observables.
The Hadamard condition can be best understood by employing tools from microlocal anal-
ysis. Indeed, already since the appearance of the seminal paper by Duistermaat-Hörmander in
the 1970s on the study of partial differential equations via the wavefront set [126], it has become
increasingly clear that microlocal analysis is an indispensable tool for formulating quantum field
theories in a mathematically rigorous way. On the one hand, microlocal analysis provides power-
ful techniques for solving the partial differential equations governing the dynamics of quantum
field theory.
On the other hand, it offers practical criteria for the existence of products of
quantum observables, viewed as Hilbert space-valued distributions, thus enabling a rigorous
formulation of Wick polynomials. In the 1990s, the seminal work of Radzikowski [364, 365]
brought microlocal analysis to the center stage of quantum field theory in curved spacetime
by showing that the Hadamard condition can be reformulated using the wavefront set.
In
particular, in a modern formulation, the Hadamard condition is equivalent to the requirement
WF′(λ±) ⊂N ± × N ± ,
where N ± are the positive/negative energy cones, i.e. the two connected components of the light
cone in T∗M, and where λ± : Γ∞
c (E2) →Γ∞(E2) are the distributional kernels of the 2-point
functions, i.e.
(ψ, λ+ϕ)E2 = ω(Φ([ϕ])Φ∗([ψ])) ,
(ψ, λ−ϕ)E2 = ω(Φ([ψ])Φ∗([ϕ])) .
The wavefront set WF(T) ∈T∗M\{0} of a distribution T is a refinement of the singular support
of T that includes additional informations on the singular directions in Fourier space. In par-
ticular, the above definition makes precise the idea of “states with the same singular ultraviolet
structure of the Minkowski vacuum”.
Remarkably, following earlier works of Junker-Schrohe [269–271], Gérard-Wrochna demon-
strated that Hadamard states can be constructed using pseudodifferential techniques on general


## Page 24


4
Introduction
curved spacetimes for Klein-Gordon fields [197]. The construction of Hadamard states for lin-
ear field theories that possess a gauge symmetry, such as Maxwell’s theory or linearised gravity,
however, presents much more subtle challenges, mainly due to the following reasons:
(i) First, as already indicated above, the presence of gauge symmetry implies that the differ-
ential operator governing the dynamics of a gauge theory is generically non-hyperbolic.
(ii) Secondly, while the fibre metric on the space of solutions is typically non-degenerate, it
is not, in general, positive-definite. Indeed, in essentially all the linear gauge theories
coming from physical applications, such as Maxwell’s theory, linearised Yang-Mills theory
and linearised gravity, the fibre metric ⟨·, ·⟩E2 is usually constructed out of the Lorentzian
metric g itself and hence Lorentzian in nature.
(iii) Linear gauge theories often suffer from infrared problems. On ultrastatic spacetimes, the
construction of Hadamard states is usually reduced to the construction of projections
onto the subspace of positive frequency solutions. For massive theories, this, in turn, is
achieved as soon as the operator
√
∆+ m2 and its inverse are well-defined. For massless
theories (m = 0), however, there are a priori obstructions to the construction of these
projector, since the Laplacian ∆is in general strictly non-positive. For ordinary field
theories, such as massless Klein-Gordon theory, this problem is usually overcome by em-
ploying techniques from pseudodifferential calculus. More precisely, one aims to construct
a suitable smoothing operator r−∞for which
p∆+ r−∞consistently can be defined. Be-
ing a smoothing operator, this does not spoil the Hadamard property, however, in the case
of gauge theories, it is in conflict with gauge invariance, which requires the corresponding
projections to commute with the operator K that parametrises the gauge transformations.
How to tackle the first issue is well-understood, namely by means of a suitable gauge-fixing
procedure, as explained above. The second issue, namely the non-positivity of the fibre metric,
however, is much more intricate and leads to a conflict between the Hadamard condition and
the positivity of states, while infrared problem provide further complications to be taken into
account.
While it is well-known how to construct bidistributions satisfying the Hadamard
condition, ensuring the positivity and gauge-invariance of states in linear gauge theories hence
remains a highly non-trivial task at the forefront of current research.
This thesis is devoted to the study of hyperbolic operators on Lorentzian manifolds as well
as of linear gauge theories and their quantisation. We now provide a detailed overview of the
main results as well as the overall content and structure of this doctoral thesis.
Content, Main Results and Organisation of this Thesis
The thesis is organised into three chapters, devoted to the study of hyperbolic differential
operators on Lorentzian manifolds and their Cauchy problem, a detailed analysis of linear gauge
theories and their quantisation, as well as to the explicit quantisation of Maxwell’s theory on
general globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifolds. Each chapter is divided into three sections
and is supported by the necessary preliminary and expositional material, ensuring that the
presentation is entirely self-contained. Moreover, the thesis is supplemented by an appendix
containing, besides some additional technical results and auxiliary material, a self-contained
exposition on microlocal analysis and pseudodifferential calculus.
Chapter 1. The first chapter of this thesis is devoted to the study of (linear) hyperbolic
partial differential operators on globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifolds, thereby providing the
essential analytic foundations for the investigation of relativistic field theories. Section 1.1 begins
with a brief review of the basic notions of Lorentzian geometry. In particular, we discuss the


## Page 25


Introduction
5
notion of globally hyperbolic spacetimes, complemented by several physically relevant examples,
which constitute a particularly well-suited class of backgrounds for formulating and analysing
the Cauchy problem for hyperbolic partial differential equations. In particular, we review the
theorem of Bernal-Sánchez (Theorem 1.8), which states that any such manifold takes the form
M = R × Σ ,
g = −β2dt ⊗dt + ht ,
where Σ is a smooth and spacelike Cauchy hypersurface, β ∈C∞(M, (0, ∞)) the lapse function
and (ht)t∈R a one-parameter family of Riemannian metrics on R.
In the subsequent section, Section 1.2, we recall the definition of linear differential operators
on smooth manifolds, emphasising their characterisation through the concept of locality. We
then provide a detailed treatment of symmetric hyperbolic systems, which are linear first-order
differential operators S: Γ∞(E) →Γ∞(E) acting on the smooth sections of a Hermitian vector
bundle (E, ⟨·, ·⟩E) over a globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifold (M, g), whose principal symbol
σS : T∗M →End(E) is Hermitian with respect to the bundle metric ⟨·, ·⟩E and which is hyper-
bolic in the sense that ⟨σS(τ)·, ·⟩E is positive-definite for all future-directed timelike τ ∈T∗M.
Symmetric hyperbolic systems are of fundamental importance in mathematical physics, as they
include a wide range of hyperbolic operators that arise naturally in applications. In particular,
the Dirac operator of a globally hyperbolic spin manifold falls into this class. Moreover, every
normally hyperbolic operator, that is, wave-type operator N = −□∇E + N0, where □∇E denotes
the connection d’Alembertian and N0 a zero-order operator, can be rewritten as a symmetric
hyperbolic system, as we will explain in some detail.
A central aim of this chapter is to establish the well-posedness of the Cauchy problem for
symmetric hyperbolic systems from first principles, without assuming any prior familiarity with
the subject. More precisely, the aim is to show that the Cauchy problem
(

= ϕ

has a unique smooth solution ψ ∈Γ∞(E) for arbitrary Cauchy data (f, ϕ) ∈Γ∞(E|Σ0) × Γ∞(E)
that propagates at most with the speed of light, i.e. supp(ψ) ⊂J (supp(ϕ) ∪supp(f)). This is
the content of Theorem 1.22. To this end, we provide a novel proof which has not previously
appeared in the literature in the context of hyperbolic systems on manifolds. The strategy is as
follows: we first restrict our attention to spatially compact manifolds, an admissible reduction in
the curved setting, since one can always employ a compactification procedure to achieve spatial
compactness. We then establish the existence of weak solutions on a finite time strip by suitably
adapting the classical Fréchet–Riesz argument to the setting of manifolds. Finally, we prove
regularity of solutions using a new approach based on formulating an extended system that
acts simultaneously on the unknown field and its derivatives. As mentioned above, we obtain
the well-posedness of the Cauchy problem for normally hyperbolic operators as a byproduct,
summarised in Theorem 1.39. We conclude this section with a discussion of Green hyperbolicity.
In the final part of this chapter, Section 1.3, we investigate a small but conceptually signifi-
cant departure from the classical theory. As noted above, differential operators are intrinsically
linked to the notion of locality, in the sense that their action is determined entirely by data in
an arbitrarily small neighbourhood. Recent developments in mathematical physics and related
areas, however, have highlighted the importance of incorporating also nonlocal potentials or
interactions into hyperbolic equations, where effects at one point can depend on events occur-
ring far away in space and time. Examples range from Maxwell’s equations in linear dispersive
media, semiclassical models such as the semiclassical Einstein equations to relativistic field equa-
tions on noncommutative spacetimes. Following [158], we investigate the initial value problem
for symmetric hyperbolic systems on globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifolds coupled to a

Chapter 2. The second chapter of this thesis provides a detailed exposition on linear gauge
theories on globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifolds and their quantisation. In the first part,
Section 2.1, we discuss the mathematical structure of classical linear gauge theories employing
the formalism initially described by Hack-Schenkel and further developed by Gérard-Wrochna,
as outlined above. In particular, we provide a detailed discussion of the Cauchy problem for
linear gauge theories as well as of the structure of the classical theory by studying several
equivalent characterisations of the corresponding phase space.
Having established an abstract axiomatic framework for linear gauge theories and the well-
posedness of the corresponding Cauchy problems, we come to the main part of the second
chapter, namely Section 2.2. Within this section, we will study several examples of linear gauge
theories of direct physical interest in the context of the Hack-Schenkel formalism, some of which
have not been discussed yet in the literature. To start with, we will discuss the following gauge
theories on general globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifolds:
,→Maxwell’s theory of electromagnetism (see Proposition 2.22).
,→Yang-Mills theory on arbitrary, possibly non-trivial, smooth principal fibre bundles and
with possibly non-compact structure groups, linearised around general, possibly non-flat,
background solutions to the non-linear Yang-Mills equations (see Proposition 2.30).
,→Einstein gravity, linearised around arbitrary (globally hyperbolic) solutions to the non-
linear vacuum Einstein equations (see Proposition 2.44). Moreover, we will consider the
equivalent theory by taking trace-reversed vacuum Einstein equations as background equa-
tions instead, which leads to a slightly different description of linearised gravity in the
Hack-Schenkel formalism (see Proposition 2.45).

Introduction
7
Through the prism of the quest of studying more realistic models from a physical perspective,
we will further study the following coupled systems:
,→Linearised Yang-Mills theory coupled to a (real-valued) Klein-Gordon (or “Higgs”) field
taking values in an associated vector bundle and transforming under an arbitrary (finite-
dimensional) Lie group representations of the structure group (see Proposition 2.37).
,→Linearised Einstein gravity coupled to a real-valued scalar field (see Proposition 2.50).
In particular, the main task is to show that all the aforementioned theories fit within the Hack-
Schenkel formalism of linear gauge theories. As one can see by looking at the list of theories,
we restrict ourselves to bosonic theories. This, however, is purely done for convenience and it is
strongly suggested that also other and more complicated coupled theories, such as a linearised
Yang-Mills-Dirac-Higgs-Yukawa theory eventually fits within this formalism.
In the final part of this chapter, Section 2.3, we will turn to the quantisation of linear gauge
theories. In particular, we will give a detailed exposition of the Gérard-Wrochna formalism for
quantisation of linear gauge theories. To start with, we provide the reader with a short review
of the algebraic approach to quantum field theory. As mentioned above, quantisation in this
framework consists of two steps, namely the assignment of a ∗-algebra of observables and the
construction of a suitable state satisfying the Hadamard condition. We will explain both these
ingredients in detail. We conclude this chapter by providing a detailed bibliography of existence
results for Hadamard states for various different field theories.

as a linear gauge theory in the formalism of Hack-Schenkel is described by the two operators

In the first section of this chapter, Section 3.1, we provide a more detailed analysis of
Maxwell’s theory on globally hyperbolic manifolds.
On the one hand, this section aims to
prepare the ground for the construction of Hadamard states of Maxwell’s theory to be per-
formed in the subsequent sections. On the other hand, it provides a detailed and self-contained
treatment of both the Cauchy problem for Maxwell’s equations on general globally hyperbolic
spacetimes as well as the corresponding gauge problem by describing several gauge conditions,
their achievability and their relations among each other.
Afterwards, we will turn to the construction of Hadamard states for Maxwell’s theory,
following [340]. As mentioned above, the construction of Hadamard states for field-theoretic
models admitting a gauge theory is mathematically more complicated compared to the ordinary
field theories. To overcome the difficulties mentioned above, the main idea of our approach is
to fix completely all the gauge degrees of freedom. This will be achieved by working in the
so-called Cauchy radiation gauge, a novel gauge condition. More precisely, we consider those
solutions of the Maxwell equation that satisfy the conditions
δA = 0
AT|t=0 = ∂tAT|t=0 = 0 ,


## Page 28


8
Introduction
where we decomposed A = ATη + AΣ with η := βdt and AT := η⌟A. On globally hyperbolic
ultrastatic spacetimes, such as Minkowski spacetime, this gauge is equivalent to the so-called
radiation gauge, i.e. δA = AT = 0 and, if Σ is in addition non-compact, it is (on-shell the equa-
tions of motion) equivalent to the Coulomb gauge δΣAΣ = 0, under suitable decay assumptions
on the involved fields. The gauge condition is chosen in such a way that it removes the negative
contributions in the bundle metric at the initial-data stage, thereby enabling the construction
of positive Cauchy covariances for the fully gauge-fixed theory.
In order to show that the Cauchy radiation gauge can always be achieved, we will need
to solve the Poisson equation ∆f = δΣω for a given source ω ∈Ω1(Σ), where ∆= δΣdΣ
denotes the de Rham-Hodge Laplacian acting on functions, on general complete Riemannian
manifolds. Now, for compact Σ, this can easily be seen to be equivalent to the celebrated Hodge
decomposition theorem. Indeed, if a solution f exists to the above equation, then δΣ(dΣf −
ω) = 0 and hence, ω = dΣf + η, where η ∈ker(δΣ). Therefore, the idea is to generalise the
Hodge decomposition to non-compact, complete Riemannian manifolds, for differential forms
taking values in suitable Sobolev spaces. This will be achieved in Section 3.2, in particular in
Theorem 3.40 and in Theorem 3.44.
In the last section of this chapter, Section 3.3, we will prove the existence of Hadamard
states for Maxwell theory on arbitrary globally hyperbolic manifolds. It is the first proof on
general globally hyperbolic backgrounds, with earlier works imposing several technical analytic
or topological restrictions. Building upon the results of the previous discussion, we will show
that the Cauchy radiation gauge can always be achieved in suitable spacetimes and in suitable
Sobolev spaces. Moreover, we will show that the Cauchy problem for differential forms is well-
posed when working with the differential forms that are smooth in time and H∞in space, see
Theorem 3.53. The construction of Hadamard states in the Cauchy radiation gauge will be first
performed explicitly in ultrastatic globally hyperbolic manifolds. As already emphasised above,
the main benefit of working in the Cauchy radiation gauge is that the fibre metric becomes
manifestly positive definite. States for Maxwell’s theory are will therefore be defined first on
the completely gauge-fixed theory and then defined on the original phase space via the pull-back
along the gauge-fixing projector. It will be a central part to show that this procedure preserves
the Hadamard property.
The existence of Hadamard states on general globally hyperbolic
manifolds is then deduced by a deformation argument, leading us the main theorem of this
section, Theorem 3.66.
Appendix A. This thesis is supplemented with an appendix consisting of the following
parts: the first two sections, Section A.1 and Section A.2, provide a detailed and self-contained
introduction into microlocal analysis, including a brief review of distribution theory, both on Rd
and on manifolds, the celebrated kernel theorem of Schwartz, the notion of the wavefront set
of distributions, pseudodifferential operators on Rd and on manifolds, with a particular focus
on Riemannian manifolds of bounded geometry, as well as into the celebrated propagation of
singularities theorem. The aim of this section is two-fold: on the one hand, it provides the basic
technical tools needed in the formulation and construction of Hadamard states. On the other
hand, it can be seen as an independent introduction to microlocal analysis on its own without
assuming any background knowledge.
Section A.3 supplements the thesis with a short discussion of basic notions of functional
analysis.
In particular, we provide a basic dictionary for unbounded operators on Hilbert
spaces and review the spectral theorem, which we will need in our discussion of the Hodge
decomposition in Section 3.2.
Last but not least, the final section of the appendix, Section A.4, includes a technical result
on isomorphisms between quotients of vector spaces that will be useful when discussing various
equivalent characterisations of the phase space for linear gauge theories in Section 2.1.

Chapter1
Hyperbolic Operators on Lorentzian
Manifolds
In this first chapter, we lay the essential geometric and analytic foundations for the study of
linear field theories on Lorentzian manifolds. We begin by introducing the notion of globally hy-
perbolic spacetimes, which constitute a particularly well-suited class of Lorentzian backgrounds
for the formulation and analysis of the Cauchy problem for hyperbolic partial differential equa-
tions. Subsequently, we give a concise overview of linear differential operators on manifolds
acting on sections of vector bundles. Building up on this, we proceed to discuss various notions
of hyperbolicity in the context of globally hyperbolic manifolds. In particular, we will prove the
well-posedness of the Cauchy problem for symmetric hyperbolic systems and normally hyper-
bolic operators starting from first principles, along with the concept of Green operators, which
will play a central role in the subsequent development of the theory.
Last but not least, we study a small deviation from the classical local theory, namely the
Cauchy problem of symmetric hyperbolic systems coupled to potentials that are nonlocal both
in time and space. In particular, we show that the Cauchy problem is well-posed for a large
class of nonlocal potentials, which is the first main result of this thesis.
1.1
Globally Hyperbolic Lorentzian Manifolds
In this preliminary section we shall discuss some basic concepts of Lorentzian geometry. In
particular, we introduce the notion of globally hyperbolic spacetimes that will provide a suitable
background for analysing the Cauchy problem for hyperbolic equations.
1.1.1
Preliminaries on Lorentzian Geometry
Throughout this section, let (M, g) denote a smooth Lorentzian manifold of dimension d =
n + 1 with n ∈N and signature convention (−, +, . . . , +). It is well known that topological
obstructions can prevent the existence of a Lorentzian metric: if M is non-compact, then a
Lorentzian metric always exists, while in the case in which M is compact, a Lorentzian metric
exists on M if and only if its Euler characteristic vanishes; see, e.g., [348, Chap. 5, Prop. 37].
As usual, the Lorentzian metric g allows us to classify tangent vectors into three different
types: a tangent vector v ∈TpM at some point p ∈M is called
(i)
spacelike
if
gp(v, v) > 0
or
v = 0 ;
(ii)
lightlike
or
null
if
gp(v, v) = 0
and
v ̸= 0 ;
(iii)
timelike
if
gp(v, v) < 0 .

A vector that is either timelike or lightlike is also called causal. The classification into spacelike,
lightlike, timelike and causal objects extends to vector fields when applied pointwise and to
curves when applied to their tangent vectors. Moreover, the classification extends to hypersur-
face when applied to their normal vector fields suitably, i.e. a timelike normal defines a spacelike
hypersurface and vice versa.
In order to introduce some notions of causality, we assume that (M, g) is time-orientable,
that it, we assume that there exists a global continuous timelike vector field X ∈Γ0(TM) on M.
The choice of such a vector field is called a time orientation of M and allows to decompose the
light cone at p ∈M, i.e. the linear subspace
Vp := {v ∈TpM | gp(v, v) < 0} ⊂TpM ,

directed (or past-pointing), respectively.
This classification extends to all causal vectors by
considering the closed lightcones. Furthermore, the classification extends to causal vector fields
when applied pointwise and to causal curves when applied to their tangent vector fields.
Remark 1.1. The existence of a time-orientable Lorentzian metric is again a purely topological
question: the existence of a Lorentzian metric on a manifold M is equivalent to the existence
of a nowhere vanishing vector field. Given such a vector field, it can always be chosen to be
timelike with respect to some Lorentzian metric. Hence, the existence of a Lorentzian metric is
equivalent to the existence of a time-orientable Lorentzian metric, see [348, Chap. 5, Prop. 37].
On the other hand, not every Lorentzian manifold is time-orientable.
Nevertheless, ev-
ery Lorentzian manifold admits a time-orientable Lorentzian double cover and if M is simply
connected, then every Lorentzian metric on M is time-orientable, see [348, Chap. 7, Lemma 17].
Lastly, we stress that time-orientability and orientability are independent condition. For
instance, the cylinder S1×R is an orientable manifold, for which one can easily construct both a
time-orientable and non-time-orientable Lorentzian metric. Similarly, one can find both a time-
orientable and non-time-orientable Lorentzian metric on the non-orientable Möbius strip.

## Page 31


1.1. Globally Hyperbolic Lorentzian Manifolds
11
1.1.2
Global Hyperbolicity
After this preliminary discussion on Lorentzian geometry, we will now define and study
a special class of time-orientable Lorentzian manifolds, namely those for which the Cauchy
problem of hyperbolic operators is well-posed. To this end, we make the following definition.
Definition 1.2. (Spacetimes and Globally Hyperbolic Manifolds)
A spacetime is a connected, oriented, time-oriented and smooth Lorentzian manifold. Fur-
thermore, a spacetime (M, g) is called globally hyperbolic if the following holds true:
(i) (M, g) is causal, i.e. there are no closed causal curves;
(ii) For every p, q ∈M, the set J +(p) ∩J −(q) is compact.
Remarks 1.3. (Historical Remarks and Equivalent Definitions)
(i) In earlier literature, one usually encounters Definition 1.2 with condition (i) replaced by
the requirement that (M, g) is strongly causal, i.e. there are no almost causal curves, that
is, for each point p ∈M and every open neighbourhood U ⊂M of p, there exists an open
neighbourhood V ⊂U of p such that any causal curve starting and ending in V remains
within U. However, it has been shown by Bernal-Sánchez [57, Thm. 3.2] that causality
and strong causality are equivalent, provided the compactness condition (ii) holds.
In fact, it has recently been shown by Hounnonkpe-Minguzzi in [255] that for Lorentzian
manifolds with dim(M) ≥3, the causality assumption (i) can entirely be omitted, provided
that M is additionally assumed to be non-compact.
(ii) Globally hyperbolic manifolds were originally introduced by Leray [305] in the context of
hyperbolic differential equations. However, Leray did not use the modern definition given
above. Instead, he considered for each p, q ∈M the set
C(p, q) := {C0-curves from p to q future-directed causal}/∼,
where two curves are equivalent if they differ by a strictly monotonic reparametrisation,
endowed with the C0-topology.5 Then, (M, g) is globally hyperbolic if it is causal and if
C(p, q) is compact for all p, q ∈M. The equivalence of this definition with Definition 1.2
has been shown by Hawking-Ellis [236, Prop. 6.6.2], based on some technical results of
Geroch [203] and Seifert [410]. A modern proof can also be found in [327, Thm. 3.79].
▲
As a first observation, we note that global hyperbolicity imposes a topological restriction:
any globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifold must necessarily be non-compact.
Lemma 1.4. A globally hyperbolic spacetime is non-compact.
Proof. The collection {I+(p)}p∈M defines an open cover of M and if M is compact, we can find
a finite subcover {I+(p1), . . . , I+(pn)} for some n ∈N and points p1, . . . , pn ∈M. Without loss
of generality, we assume that none of the sets I(pi) is entirely contained in another one. But
now, note that pi ∈I+(pi) for every i, which means that there is timelike curve starting and
ending at pi. Hence, (M, g) violates the causality condition.
■
It turns out that any globally hyperbolic manifold possesses a particularly simple topological
structure: it is diffeomorphic to a product R × Σ, where Σ is some (compact or non-compact)
spacelike hypersurface. To discuss this fact in more detail, we start with the following definition:
5 For a given γ ∈C(p, q), the set of curves in C(p, q) contained in ran(γ) ⊂M is a neighbourhood basis.

Chapter 1. Hyperbolic Operators on Lorentzian Manifolds
Definition 1.5. (Cauchy Surfaces)
Let (M, g) be a spacetime.
A subset Σ ⊂M is called a Cauchy (hyper)surface if it is
intersected exactly once by any inextensible timelike curve.
It is not too hard to show that any Cauchy surface Σ is in fact an embedded topological
submanifold of codimension one that is intersected exactly once by every inextendible causal
curve, see e.g. [348, Chap. 14, Lemma 29].
Theorem 1.6. (Geroch [203])
A spacetime is globally hyperbolic if and only if it admits a Cauchy surface.
As a by-product of Geroch’s theorem, it follows that a globally hyperbolic spacetime (M, g)
admits a continuous foliation by Cauchy surfaces, i.e. M is homeomorphic to R × Σ for some
Cauchy surface Σ. This can be established by finding a Cauchy time function, i.e. a continu-
ous function t: M →R which is strictly increasing on any future-directed timelike curve and
whose level sets t−1(τ), τ ∈R, are Cauchy surfaces homeomorphic to Σ. Geroch’s splitting
is topological in nature and the fact that it can be done in a smooth way has been part of
the mathematical folklore (see e.g. the proof of Prop. 6.6.8 in Hawking-Ellis [236]), whilst re-
maining an open problem for many years (see also the introduction of [54]). Only recently
Bernal-Sánchez obtained a smooth version of Geroch’s theorem by introducing the notion of a
Cauchy temporal function.
Definition 1.7. (Cauchy Temporal Functions)
For a spacetime (M, g), we call t ∈C∞(M, R) a Cauchy temporal function if t−1(τ) for all
τ ∈R are smooth Cauchy hypersurfaces and gradg(t) = (dt)♯is past-directed timelike.
With this definition, the central result of Bernal-Sánchez is as follows.

τ ∈C∞(M). Furthermore, there exists a smooth diffeomorphism ψ: M′ →M such that
M′ := R × Σ ,

where t := ψ∗τ : M′ →R acts as the natural projection, β ∈C∞(M′, (0, ∞)), Σ is a smooth,
spacelike Cauchy surface and h ∈Γ∞(M′, pr∗
2(TΣ⊗s2)) with pr2 : M′ →Σ being the natural
projection, is such that hτ := h|Στ is a Riemannian metric on Στ := {τ} × Σ for all τ ∈R.
Moreover, if Σ ⊂M is any smooth and spacelike Cauchy hypersurface, there exists a
Cauchy temporal function t ∈C∞(M, R) such that Σ ∼= t−1(τ) for some τ ∈R.
The function β is usually called the lapse function and the tensor h can be thought of as
a one-parameter family (ht)t∈R of Riemannian metrics on Σ, depending smoothly on t. If we
choose local coordinates (xi)i=1,...,k of Σ on some open subset U ⊂Σ, then (x0 := t, x1, . . . , xk)
are local coordinates of M on R × U, and
(ψ∗g)00(t, xi) = −β2(t, xi) ,
(ψ∗g)i0(t, xi) = 0
and
(ψ∗g)ij(t, xi) = (ht)ij(xi) .
Note that there are no “cross-terms”, i.e. non-trivial metric components g0i, as for instance in
ADM-type decompositions of the form
ψ∗g = (−β2 + αiαi) dt ⊗dt + 2αi dt ⊗dxi + gij dxi ⊗dxj

Remark 1.9. Global hyperbolicity can also be extended to include timelike boundaries. A

The class of globally hyperbolic spacetimes is non-empty and contains most of the spacetimes
relevant for physical applications. We briefly mention some important examples.

The spacetime (MI, g) describes the interior region of a black hole and (MII, g) the gravi-
tational field of a star or black hole in the exterior. Both of them are globally hyperbolic.
The metric singularity at r = 2m is a coordinate singularity and can be overcome by
considering a different set of coordinates, such as Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates (af-
ter [131, 152]; see [354]). In this way, one can show that also MI∪II := MI ∪MII ∪Hev,
where Hev denotes their common boundary, the event horizon, is globally hyperbolic. A
proof can be found in [374], where a Cauchy surface for MI∪II is constructed. In fact,
also the Kruskal-Szekeres spacetime (see e.g. [442, Sec. 6.4], [294, 424]), the maximal ex-
tension of Schwarzschild, is globally hyperbolic. Similar statements can be made for the
Kerr spacetime (after [277]; see [349] for details on Kerr geometry), that describes rotating
black holes (or stars), outside the Cauchy horizon, see [193, Prop. 5.1, 5.3, 5.4].

Examples of spacetimes that are not globally hyperbolic are the anti de Sitter-spacetime,
see e.g. [36, Sec. 3.5], and the Gödel universe, see e.g. [209] and [236, Sec. 5.7]. Note that both
of there examples are solutions of the vacuum Einstein equations.
Last but not least, we introduce some further terminology of causality. Let (M, g) be a
globally hyperbolic spacetime and A ⊂M be closed. Then,
• A is called past compact, if A ∩J −(p) is compact for all p ∈M.
• A is called past compact, if A ∩J +(p) is compact for all p ∈M.
• A is called temporally compact, if it is both past and future compact.
• A is called spatially compact, if there exists a compact set K ⊂M such that A ⊂J (M).
It is easy to see that A is past (resp. future) compact if and only if there exists a Cauchy surface
Σ ⊂M such that A ∈J +(Σ) (resp. A ⊂J −(Σ)), see e.g. [33, Lemma 1.2] or [390, Thm. 3.1].
Furthermore, as proven in [390, Thm. 2.2], A is spatially compact if and only if A∩Σ is compact
for every spacelike Cauchy surface Σ ⊂M. Last but not least, if E π−→M is some real or complex
vector bundle over M, we denote by
Γ∞

Γ∞(E)
the linear subspaces of Γ∞(E) consisting of sections whose support is a past compact, future
compact, temporally compact and spatially compact set, respectively.
1.2
Hyperbolic Operators and Cauchy Problem
Having established a suitable geometric background for the study of hyperbolic equations
on manifolds, we now turn our attention to differential operators. We begin with a brief review
of linear partial differential operators on manifolds, following the approach of Peetre in which
those kind of operators are purely characterised by their locality property.
Afterwards, we
discuss two important classes of hyperbolic operators, namely symmetric hyperbolic systems
and normally hyperbolic operators. In particular, we demonstrate that the Cauchy problem for
each of these classes is well-posed. Last but not least, we discuss retarded/advanced Green’s
operators and the notion of Green hyperbolicity.

1.2.1

The goal of the first section is to briefly review the concept of linear differential operators
on manifolds.
To start with, we recall some basic definitions of the local theory, i.e. linear

operator on U is a K-linear map D: C∞(U, Km) →C∞(U, Kn) of the form

Now, a natural way to define differential operators on manifolds is to consider linear and
local operators whose local representations are differential operators as defined in Eq. (1.3).
Surprisingly, the second condition is actually redundant: any local operator between spaces of
smooth sections is automatically a differential operator in the local sense. This is exactly the
content of Peetre’s theorem, whose elegant proof we sketch here for completeness.

Hence, if D: Γ∞(E) →Γ∞(F) is a linear and local operator, it is locally a linear differential

p ∈M is independent of the chosen charts and trivialisations. We hence, define the order of D
to be the supremum of the order of the local representations at each point. We stress that this
number might as well be infinite if M is non-compact. To sum up:

might ask is for which types of differential operators there exists a canonical closed extension.
Furthermore, having discussed suitable closed extensions of linear partial differential operators,
the next question to answer is when a formally self-adjoint operator D can be extended to an
honest self-adjoint operator in the Hilbert space L2(E) in a unique way. Both of these questions
are addressed in the following proposition:
Proposition 1.16. (Essentially Self-Adjointness and Closed Extensions)
Let D ∈DO(E) be a formally self-adjoint linear partial differential operator, which we view
as a densely-defined operator D: dom(D) →L2(E) with dom(D) = Γ∞
c (E). Then,
D is essentially self-adjoint
⇔
Dmin = Dmax
⇔
dom(Dmax) ⊂dom(Dmin) .
Proof. By definition, D: dom(D) →L2(E) is essentially self-adjoint if and only if D is self-
adjoint, i.e. D
† = D. Now, its a general fact that the adjoint of the closure coincides with the
closure, since D
† = (D††)† = (D†)†† = (D†) = D†, where we used the fact that the adjoint
of any operator is closed.
Hence, essentially self-adjointness is equivalent to D† = D.
But
this is equivalent to Dmin = Dmax, by definition. For the second equivalence, we recall that
dom(Dmax) ⊃dom(Dmin) since Dmin ⊂Dmax, as mentioned above. Hence, Dmin = Dmax if and
only if also the other inclusion is true.
■
On a practical level, showing dom(Dmax) ⊂dom(Dmin) explicitly for a given operator be
quite complicated, however, it turns out to be true for Laplace-type and Dirac-type operators.
The concept of minimal and maximal closed extensions is standard in functional analysis and
operator theory, see, for instance, [223, Chap. 4] and [135, pp. 73ff.] for general references.
1.2.2
Symmetric Hyperbolic Systems
Having established the general framework of linear differential operators on smooth mani-
folds, we now focus on the class of hyperbolic operators defined on globally hyperbolic Lorentzian
manifolds.
Broadly speaking, the notion of hyperbolicity is fundamentally tied to the well-
posedness of the Cauchy problem for initial data prescribed on a non-characteristic hypersur-
face. We begin by examining two particularly important classes of hyperbolic equations. In
Section 1.2.4, we extend our discussion to more general formulations.
In this section, we consider a very general class of first-order hyperbolic operators, namely
symmetric hyperbolic systems. Throughout this section, we fix the following notation.
• Let (M, g) be a globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifold of dimension d = 1 + n, n ∈N.
• Let (E π−→M, ⟨·, ·⟩E) be a Hermitian K-vector bundle of rank rankK(E) = N ∈N, equipped

Chapter 1. Hyperbolic Operators on Lorentzian Manifolds
Definition 1.17. (Symmetric Hyperbolic System, [33, Def. 5.1])
A first-order operator S ∈DO1(E) is called a symmetric hyperbolic system, if its principal
symbol σS : T∗M →End(E) has the following properties.
(i) σS(ξ) is Hermitian with respect to ⟨·, ·⟩Ep for all ξ ∈T∗
pM, p ∈M.
(ii) ⟨σS(τ)·, ·⟩Ep is positive-definite for all future-directeda timelike τ ∈T∗
pM, p ∈M.
a A covector ξ ∈T∗M is future/past-directed if ξ(v) > 0 for all future/past-directed v ∈TM. In particular,
the musical isomorphism map future-directed to past-directed objects and vice versa.
The following example shows how this definition is related to the usual one found in classical
PDE literature, see e.g. the textbooks [263,426] and the classical articles [175,176].
Example 1.18. We consider (1 + n)-dimensional Minkowski spacetime (M1,n := Rn+1, η) to-
gether with the trivial vector bundle E := RM = M × RN equipped with the standard complex
inner product on each fibre. Any first-order operator on M can be written in the form
S := A0∂t + Aj∂j + S0 ,
where A0, Aj, S0 ∈C∞(M1,n, CN×N). Now, in this case, property (i) in Definition 1.17 translates
to the requirement that
(A0(t, ⃗x))† = A0(t, ⃗x) ,
(Aj(t, ⃗x))† = Aj(t, ⃗x)
∀(t, ⃗x) ∈M1,n .
Furthermore, any future-directed timelike covector on M1,n can (up to a positive function) be
written as τ = dt+αidxi for some coefficient functions αi satisfying P
j α2
i < 1. Hence, condition
(ii) is the requirement that
(A0 + αjAj)(t, ⃗x)
is positive definite
∀(t, ⃗x) ∈M1,n .
This requirement seems a bit stronger than the one found in classical PDE literature, in which
one usually just requires A0 to be positive-definite. However, since the principal symbol σS(ξ) =
Aµξµ depends smoothly on the coordinates, the positive definiteness of σS(dt) = A0 extends to
small perturbations of dt, i.e. to σS(dt + αidxi) = A0 + αiAi for sufficiently small αi.
▲
The preceding example shows that Definition 1.17 generalises the standard notion of sym-
metric hyperbolic systems to Lorentzian manifolds in a covariant manner, i.e. without the need
of specifying a time function to define hyperbolicity. In the following, we will look into some more
examples that are of direct interest for applications in theoretical and mathematical physics.
Example 1.19. (Maxwell’s Equations on Ultrastatic Manifolds)
An example of a symmetric hyperbolic system is provided by Maxwell’s equations in ultrastatic
manifolds, which in this context has been discussed in [158, Sec. 5], [34, Ex. 3.7.3] and [82,

A generalisation of the previous example can be found in [125], in which it is shown that the
Maxwell’s equations for k-forms in terms of the electric and magnetic part of the field strength
tensor define a symmetric hyperbolic system on arbitrary globally hyperbolic manifolds.
Example 1.20. (Spin Dirac Operator)
Let (M, g) be a (1 + n)-dimensional globally hyperbolic spin manifold equipped with a spin
structure, i.e. a pair (P, Λ) consisting of a Spin+(1, n)-principal bundle πP : P →M together
with a twofold covering map Λ from P to the frame bundle FrSO+(M) such that

commutes, where λ denotes the double covering at the group level, πP and πFr refer to the
respective bundle projections, and the horizontal arrows represent the right group actions on the
corresponding principal bundles. The question whether a given (pseudo-)Riemannian manifold
admits a spin stricture is a purely topological one. It can be shown that a spin structure exists
if and only if the second Stiefel-Whitney class w2(M) ∈H2(M, Z2) vanishes. Note that the first
Stiefel-Whitney class w1(M) ∈H1(M, Z2) is an obstruction for orientability of a manifold. Hence,
the existence of a spin structure can be understood as the existence of a “higher orientation” for
M. Furthermore, there is a bijection between the set of isomorphism classes of spin structures8
and H1(M, Z2). A sufficient (but not necessary) condition for the existence of a spin structure is
parallelisability of M. In particular, since any 3-dimensional orientable manifold is parallelisable,
Theorem 1.8 implies that any (1 + 3)-dimensional globally hyperbolic manifold M = R × Σ
admits a spin structure. Furthermore, since H1(M, Z2) ∼= H1(Σ, Z2) ∼= Hom(π1(Σ), Z2), the spin
structure is unique if Σ is simply connected. We refer to [298,378] for details on spin geometry
in the Riemannian setting and to [42,235] for the pseudo-Riemannian and Lorentzian case.
Using the spin representation ρ: Spin+(1, n) →Aut(CN) with N := 2⌊n+1
2 ⌋, where ⌊x⌋:=
max{k ∈Z | k ≤x} for x ∈R denotes the floor function, we construct the spinor bundle, that
is, the associated C-vector bundle
SM := P ×ρ CN .
On this bundle, we have the following additional structures:
• A natural Spin+(1, n)-invariant non-degenerate fibre metric ⟨·, ·⟩SM (defined up to a sign).

that ⟨γ(X)ψ, φ⟩SM = ⟨ψ, γ(X)φ⟩SM for all φ, ψ ∈Γ∞(SM) and X ∈X(M) and such that

Remark 1.21. (Clifford Dirac Operators)
The previous example is a special case of a more general construction: let (M, g) be a globally
hyperbolic Lorentzian manifold and (E, γ, ⟨·, ·⟩E, ∇E) be a Dirac bundle (see e.g. [378, Sec. 5.5],
[298, Chap. 5]), that is,
(i) a Clifford module bundle (E, γ), i.e. a real or complex vector bundle together with a bundle
morphism γ : TM →End(E) satisfying
γ(X)2 = −g(X, X)idE ,
∀X ∈X(M) .
The universal property of the Clifford bundle Cl(M, g) and TM ⊂Cl(M, g) imply that the
morphism γ admits a unique extension on the Clifford bundle Cl(M, g). One can hence
think of the bundle E as the vector bundle whose fibre at a point p ∈M is isomorphic to
a given left module of the Clifford algebra Cl(TpM, gp), see [27].
(ii) a bundle metric ⟨·, ·⟩E such that ⟨γ(X)ψ, φ⟩E = ⟨ψ, γ(X)φ⟩E for all X ∈X(M).
(iii) a Clifford connection ∇E, i.e. a connection on E satisfying
∇E(γ(X)ψ) = γ(∇X)ψ + γ(X)(∇Eψ) ,
or equivalently ∇End(E)
X
(γ(Y )) = γ(∇XY ) for all X, Y ∈X(M), where ∇End(E) is the
induced connection on End(E). Another equivalent characterisation is to require that γ,
viewed as a section of End(E)⊗T∗M, is parallel, i.e. ∇End(E)⊗T∗Mγ = 0, where ∇End(E)⊗T∗M
denotes connection on End(E) ⊗T∗M induced by ∇E and the Levi-Civita connection.
In this context, one can define the (Clifford) Dirac operator D ∈DO1(E) similarly as in Exam-
ple 1.20. In fact, Example 1.20 is the special case in which, for a spin manifold, the Clifford
module is chosen to be the irreducible spin representation. Furthermore, we remark that on
an even-dimensional spin manifold, every Clifford module bundle is obtained by twisting the
spinor bundle, see [53, Prop. 3.35].

Now, the goal of the following discussion is the prove that the Cauchy problem for symmetric
hyperbolic systems is well-posed. More precisely, the aim is to prove the following theorem.
Theorem 1.22. (The Cauchy Problem for Symmetric Hyperbolic Systems)
Let (M, g) be a globally hyperbolic manifold and t: M →R be a Cauchy temporal func-
tion with corresponding foliation (Σt)t∈R. Furthermore, let S ∈DO1(E) be a symmetric
hyperbolic system on a Hermitian vector bundle (E, ⟨·, ·⟩E). Then, the following holds true:
(i) For every pair (ϕ, f) ∈Γ∞(M, E) × Γ∞(Σ0, E|Σ0), there exists a unique solution ψ ∈
Γ∞(M, E) to the Cauchy problem

Remark 1.23. Extensions to Theorem 1.22 to globally hyperbolic spacetimes with timelike
boundary (see Remark 1.9) can, for instance, be found in [208].
▲
There are several ways to prove the well-posedness of the Cauchy problem for symmetric
hyperbolic systems on globally hyperbolic manifolds.
First, in [33], with an earlier sketch
in [204], the existence of solutions is obtained by gluing together local solutions obtained by
standard PDE theory. An alternative proof, based on the construction of weak solutions via a
suitable mollification procedure, is presented in the lecture notes [34]. In this thesis, we adopt a
different strategy, in which weak solutions are first constructed via the representation theorem
of Fréchet-Riesz, i.e. translating the standard approach of classical PDE theory to a global
setting, followed by proof of regularity based on the construction of an extended system acting
on sections and their first (covariant) derivatives.
To start with, we fix a globally hyperbolic spacetime (M, g) with Levi-Civita connection ∇
and a Cauchy temporal function t: M →R, which allows us to identify
M := R × Σ ,
g = −β2dt ⊗dt + ht ,
as in Theorem 1.8. We denote by (Σt := {t}×Σ)t∈R the corresponding foliation of M by Cauchy
hypersurfaces. Furthermore, let S ∈DO1(E) be a symmetric hyperbolic system on a Hermitian

■
Let us now turn to existence of solutions. We will start with a more simple situations,
namely we will show the existence of weak solution on a time strip for a spatially compact
globally hyperbolic spacetime. We then establish regularity of these solutions and conclude
with the existence of globally defined smooth solutions. The compactness assumption on the
Cauchy surfaces will be removed lateron by means of a suitable compactification procedure.

Proposition 1.29. (Existence of Weak Solutions in the Spatially Compact Case)
Let S ∈DO1(E) be a symmetric hyperbolic system on (M, g) and t ∈C∞(M) be a Cauchy
temporal function. Furthermore, assume that Σ is compact and consider a time strip MT =
t−1([0, T]). Then, there exists a weak solution ψ ∈L2(MT, E) to the Cauchy problem (1.13)
with initial datum f = 0 and source ϕ ∈Γ∞(E).
Proof. Since the formal adjoint −S∗is a symmetric hyperbolic system on its own right, by
Lemma 1.24, and since Σ is assumed to be compact, which implies that σ(dt) ∈Γ∞(End(E)) is
bounded on MT, we obtain from Lemma 1.28
∥φ∥L2(MT) ≤C(∥Stφ∥L2(MT) + ∥φT∥L2(ΣT)) = C∥Stφ∥L2(MT)
(1.14)
for all φ ∈D, where we used a time reversal argument to assign the initial data on ΣT rather
than Σ0. Now, consider the linear functional lϕ : ran(St|D) →C defined by
lϕ(ω) := ⟨ϕ, φ⟩L2(MT) ,

where φ ∈D is such that Stφ = ω. Note that this definition is independent of the choice of φ:
for any other eφ ∈D with St eφ = ω, the section θ = φ −eφ satisfies Stθ = 0 and hence θ = 0 by
the energy estimate Eq. (1.14). Moreover, the estimate (1.14) implies that lϕ is bounded, since
|lϕ(ω)| = |(⟨ϕ, φ⟩L2(MT)| ≤∥ϕ∥L2(MT) · ∥φ∥L2(MT) ≤Cϕ∥ω∥L2(MT)
for all ω ∈ran(St|D) with constant Cϕ := C∥ϕ∥L2(MT). Now, consider the Hilbert space
H := ran(St|D)
∥·∥L2(MT) ,
which naturally can be viewed as a closed subspace of L2(MT, E). Then, since lϕ : ran(St|D) →C
is bounded, it extends to a linear and continuous functional lϕ : H →C. Furthermore, the
representation theorem of Fréchet-Riesz (see e.g. [444, Thm. V.3.6]) implies that there exists a
unique ψ ∈H such that lϕ(ω) = ⟨ψ, ω⟩L2(MT) for all ω ∈H. In particular, we obtain
⟨ϕ, φ⟩L2(MT) = lϕ(ω) = ⟨ψ, ω⟩L2(MT) = ⟨ψ, Stφ⟩L2(MT)
for all test sections φ ∈D. In other words, ψ is a weak solution to the Cauchy problem (1.13)
with initial datum f = 0.
■
Note that we only obtain uniqueness of weak solutions on H, which in general is only a closed
subspace of L2(MT, E), since it is not clear a priori that St defined on D has a dense image.
Nevertheless, we will show below that the solution ψ constructed abstractly in the previous
proposition is actually smooth and hence, uniqueness will follow from Corollary 1.27.
As a next step, as already indicated above, we prove that the weak solutions constructed
above are in fact smooth. To this end, we follow the approach of [158, Sec. 4.2], inspired by the
discussion in [263, Sec. 5.3] for symmetric hyperbolic systems on Rn (see also [154, Sec. 13.3])
to the global framework of manifolds considered in this section.
Let us introduce the following notation. We consider the bundle E := E⊕(E⊗T∗M) equipped
with the bundle metric ⟨·, ·⟩E induced from ⟨·, ·⟩E and g0 = dt ⊗dt + ht and define

As discussed above, S is uniquely determined by its principal symbol and the requirement
that Sψ = ϕ if and only if SΘψ = Θϕ for all ψ, ϕ ∈Γ∞(E). Let us stress, however, that Θ• and
hence also S depends explicitly on the choice of connection ∇E. Choosing a different connection
on E results into different zero-order components R1 and R2.

i.e. the well-posedness of the Cauchy problem for symmetric hyperbolic systems.
Proof of Theorem 1.22. Claim (ii), uniqueness and finite speed of propagation, has been shown

1.2.3
Normally Hyperbolic Operators
In this section we shall study yet another important class of linear hyperbolic differential op-
erators, namely those generalising the wave operator (d’Alembertian) on Minkowski spacetime.
We start with the central definition of this section.
• Throughout this section, let (M, g) be a (1+n)-dimensional globally hyperbolic spacetime
with Levi-Civita connection ∇and E π−→M be a smooth and finite-rank K-bundle over M.
Definition 1.33 (Normally Hyperbolic Operators). A linear differential operator N ∈
DO2(E) called normally hyperbolic if its principal symbol is given by
σN(ξ) = −g♯(ξ, ξ)1E
∀ξ ∈TM .

Remark 1.36. For a normally hyperbolic operator M on E with E-compatible connection ∇E,
one may ask whether there exists a bundle metric ⟨·, ·⟩E with respect to which ∇E is metric-
compatible. In general, this need not be possible and the existence of such a metric is determined
by the holonomy group of the connection (see e.g. [398]). For normally hyperbolic operators,
this question is in fact equivalent to formal self-adjointness: we can find a bundle metric with
respect to which N is formally self-adjoint if and only if ∇E preserves this fibre metric.
▲
Besides the connection d’Alembertian, we mention two other important classes of examples
of normally hyperbolic operators. By the previous lemma, each of these can be expressed as a
connection d’Alembertian plus a zeroth-order operator.

Example 1.38. (Spin d’Alembertian)
Let (E, γ, ⟨·, ·⟩E, ∇E) be a Dirac bundle as defined in Remark. 1.21. As a special case, one can
think of the spin Dirac operator with E = SM as defined in Example 1.20. Then, the operator
N := D2 is normally hyperbolic. Indeed, σD(ξ) = γ(ξ♯) and hence
σN(ξ) = γ(ξ♯)2 = −g(ξ♯, ξ♯)idE = −g♯(ξ, ξ)idE ,
where we used the fact that γ is a Clifford multiplication. As a special case, we obtain the de
Rham-Hodge d’Alembertian from the de Rham-Hodge Dirac operator D = d + δ.
▲
We now discuss the Cauchy problem for normally hyperbolic operators.
Theorem 1.39. (The Cauchy Problem for Normally Hyperbolic Operators)
Let (M, g) be a globally hyperbolic manifold and fix a Cauchy temporal function t: M →R
with corresponding foliation (Σt)t∈R. Furthermore, let N ∈DO2(E) be a normally hyperbolic
operator with associated connection ∇E. Then, the following holds true:
(i) For every Cauchy data (ϕ, f1, f2) ∈Γ∞(M, E) × Γ∞(Σ0, E|Σ0) × Γ∞(Σ0, E|Σ0), there
exists a unique solution ψ ∈Γ∞(M, E) to the Cauchy problem

Well-posedness of the Cauchy problem for normally hyperbolic operators can be shown
in various different ways. A modern proof based on an explicit construction of fundamental
solutions via Riesz distributions can be found in [36] and [207]. Earlier proofs in the global
setting can be found in Leray’s unpublished lecture notes [305] and in [89]. Here we follow
a different strategy, which has been discussed, for example, in [34, Rem. 3.7.11.] and [208,
Sec. 6.3.3], namely by rewriting a normally hyperbolic operator as an equivalent symmetric
hyperbolic system and proving well-posedness as a corollary of Theorem 1.22.
Proof of Theorem 1.39. As explained above, the idea is to find a symmetric hyperbolic system
with equivalent Cauchy problem. We follow the procedure outlined in [34, Rem. 3.7.11.] and
[208, Sec. 6.3.3], which generalises the analogues well-known procedure from Rn to the global
setting. However, the approach here is slightly modified and made more precise by filling in
some details and by defining suitable bundles and connections.

the claims then follow from Theorem 1.22.

1.2.4
Green Hyperbolic Operators
Having established well-posedness of the Cauchy problem for symmetric hyperbolic systems
and normally hyperbolic operators, we now turn to a slightly more general notion of hyperbolic-
ity. The concept of Green hyperbolicity, introduced by Bär-Ginoux in [35] and further developed
by Bär in [33], focuses on another important property of hyperbolic operators, namely the ex-
istence of retarded and advanced Green operators, rather than the Cauchy problem itself.
Definition 1.40. (Green Hyperbolicity)
Let (E, ⟨·, ·⟩E) be a Hermitian vector bundle over a globally hyperbolic spacetime (M, g). An
operator D ∈DO(E) is called Green hyperbolic, if there exist linear operators G± : Γ∞
c (E) →
Γ∞(E), called retarded and advanced Green operators, satisfying the following conditions:
(i)
G+ ◦D|Γ∞
c = D ◦G+ = idΓ∞
c
(ii)
G−◦D|Γ∞
c = D ◦G−= idΓ∞
c
(iii)
supp(G±ψ) ⊂J ±(supp(s))
∀ψ ∈Γ∞
c (E) .

Remark 1.41. In the original articles [33,35], it is also required that the dual operator Dt ∈
DO(E∗) admits retarded/advanced Green operators. The main advantage of this is that G±
then continuously extend to distributions with the extensions satisfying similar properties as
(i)-(iii) above. In other works, such as [231], it is required that the formal adjoint D∗with
respect to some fixed bundle metric on E admits Green operators instead.
▲

shows that normally hyperbolic operators are Green hyperbolic.
▲
Example 1.43. (Pre-Normally Hyperbolic Operators)
A linear differential operator D ∈DO(E) is called pre-normally hyperbolic if there exists another
differential operator Q ∈DO(E) such that N := D ◦Q is normally hyperbolic. In this case,
it is easy to see that D is Green hyperbolic with Green operators given by G±
D := Q ◦G±
N,
see e.g. [453, Thm. 2.4.6] or [337].
An explicit example is provided by the Proca operator
P = δd + m2 : Ω1(M) →Ω1(M) for some mass parameter m > 0. In this case, we set Q :=
id + m−2dδ: Ω2(M) →Ω2(M). Then, the composition N := PQ = □dRH + m2 is normally
hyperbolic by Example 1.37.
▲

A central object in the solution theory of Green hyperbolic operators and also for applica-
tions in quantum field theory is the so-called causal propagator (or also Pauli-Jordan distribu-
tion/function [350] in the physics literature), which is defined as follows.
Definition 1.45. (Causal Propagator)
Let D ∈DO(E) be a Green hyperbolic operator. The linear operator
G := G+ −G−: Γ∞

is Green hyperbolic and denote its causal propagator by G∗. Then, G∗= −G∗.
(iv) The following sequences are exact complexes:

Following the same line of reasoning as in Example 1.42, one can see that every linear
differential operator that admits a well-posed Cauchy problem is necessarily Green hyperbolic.
As it turns out, the converse statement also holds true, at least in a suitable sense: every
differential operator D ∈DOm(E) of order m ∈N is Cauchy hyperbolic, which means that for
any smooth spacelike Cauchy hypersurface Σ ⊂M, there exists a smooth finite-rank vector

It follows that J +(K) ∩J −(K) is compact.
