1.3
Symmetric Hyperbolic Systems with Nonlocal Potentials
As we have discussed in detail in the previous sections, hyperbolic partial differential equa-
tions model many physical phenomena, particularly those involving wave propagation, causality,
and finite speed of propagation. They arise, for instance, naturally in systems governed by clas-
sical and quantum field theories. While they are traditionally studied in the context of local in-
teractions, it has become increasingly clear in many areas of mathematics that it would be highly
desirable to extend this type of equations by incorporating also nonlocal interactions, thereby
providing a more flexible framework for modelling complex phenomena, where long-range cor-
relations or memory effects play a significant role. Examples range from linear response theory,
in which a system’s response does not only depend on the instantaneous perturbation but also
on its entire history, hence leading to nonlocal temporal kernels, viscoelastic materials, in which


## Page 65


1.3. Symmetric Hyperbolic Systems with Nonlocal Potentials
45
memory-dependent stress–strain relations are naturally described by kernels of the same type
(see e.g. [274, 347]), classical and quantum fields on noncommutative spacetimes [299, 437], as
well as causal fermion systems, a non-perturbative framework unifying general relativity and
quantum field theory (see e.g. [154]), where nonlocalities arise in the causal action principle [153]
and the quantum field theoretic limit [101], with applications, e.g., to collapse models discussed
in [155, 159]. Beyond these physical applications, nonlocal PDEs have also become a vibrant
area of research in other applied areas of mathematical analysis, for instance, in biology and
life science (see e.g. the recent review [351]).
As seen above, there is a plethora of motivations arising from diverse areas of mathematical
physics and various areas of applied analysis. Before turning to the main content of this section,
let us highlight two specific examples and sources of motivation within the area of classical and
quantum field theory in more detail.
Maxwell Equations in Linear Dispersive Media.
The first example falls into the general
category of linear response theory briefly mentioned above and provides an explicit example of
the general type of nonlocal equations that we will consider in the forthcoming discussion.
Following Example 1.19, we consider an ultrastatic globally hyperbolic spacetime of the
form (M = R × Σ, g = −dt ⊗dt + h), where (Σ, h) denotes a complete Riemannian manifold.
Now, in the presence of matter, the electric and magnetic fields E , B ∈Γ∞(π∗(TΣ)), where
π: M →Σ denotes the projection, are not entirely independent of the charge and current
distributions; rather, they interact with bound charges and currents within the medium. These
interactions give rise to induced fields, making the total sources inseparable from the fields
themselves. To handle this coupling in a tractable way, the standard approach is to decompose
the total charge and current densities into external and induced parts, i.e. ρ = ρ(ext) + ρ(ind)
and j = j(ext) + j(ind), and to introduce the auxiliary fields D (electric displacement) and H
(magnetising field/magnetic field strength), in Gaußian units and with speed of light c = 1, by
D := E + 4πP ,
H := B −4πM ,
(1.24)
where P, M ∈Γ∞(π∗(TΣ)) are the polarisation and magnetisation vector fields, defined in
terms of the induced charge and current by the system14
(
ρ(ind)
= −divΣ(P)
j(ind)
= ∂tP + curlΣ(M ) .
In other words, P and M describe how the materials reacts to the electric and magnetic fields
present and how the induced charges and currents contributes to the overall electromagnetic
fields. With these definitions, the macroscopic Maxwell’s equations can be written in the form

As written, Maxwell’s equations (1.25), viewed as a system for the fields E , B, D, H in terms
of the external sources ρ(ext) and j(ext), are of course underdetermined. To resolve this issue,
one must supplement the system with a suitable matter model in which P and M are given
functions of E and B, hence determining the auxiliary fields D and H in terms of E and B
via Eq. (1.24). The precise form of these constitutive equations is determined by the physical
properties of the medium and when chosen appropriately, ensure a well-posed formulation of

Semiclassical Models.
Another important motivational example for the general theme of
hyperbolic equations with nonlocal interactions are semiclassical models, in which nonlocalities
naturally emerge due to the interaction of classical and quantum matter. An important example
in this direction is provided by semiclassical gravity. General relativity has proven remarkably
successful within its domain, yet there are situations in which quantum effects cannot be ne-
glected, such as in the vicinity of black holes. The dynamics of particles in the microscopic
domain, on the other hand, is described by quantum field theory, in which in particular the
algebraic approach (see e.g. Sec. 2.3) offers a mathematically rigorous framework, well-suited
to describe fields on curved Lorentzian backgrounds. While, despite numerous efforts, a con-
sistent theory of quantum gravity remains elusive, a major step in this direction is the study
of backreactions, i.e., the effects of quantum matter on the classical geometry, captured by the
semiclassical Einstein equations (see for instance [230, Sec. 2.6] for a review),
Ric(g) −1
2Scal(g)g + Λg = ωg(:T(g, Φ):)
(1.28)
where ωg(:T(g, Φ):) is the normal-ordered energy-momentum tensor of a quantised field Φ, Λ
a cosmological constant and ωg a suitable quantum state, we refer to Sec. 2.3 for details. The
unknowns of Eq. (1.28) are the state ωg and the Lorentzian metric g. To define the right-
hand side of Eq. (1.28) rigorously, a renormalisation procedure is required, essentially due to
the singular nature of quantum fields as operator-valued distributions, which makes nonlinear
expressions, such as Φ2 contained in T(g, Φ) ill-defined. It is well known, however, that this
obstacle can be overcome by considering a Hadamard state that mimics the singular structure
of the two-point function of the Minkowski vacuum, we refer to Sec. 2.3 for details. As a by-
product of this procedure, the right-hand side of Eq. (1.28) involves terms that are nonlocal
both in time and space, reflecting the inherent nonlocality of quantum correlation functions.
These features cannot be fully absorbed into the renormalisation freedom present [243, 244],
hence resulting in a nonlinear and nonlocal equation. While the classical Einstein equations
can be understood as a quasilinear wave equation, ensuring a well-posed Cauchy problem,


## Page 67


1.3. Symmetric Hyperbolic Systems with Nonlocal Potentials
47
the combination of nonlinearities, higher-order derivative terms, and terms that are nonlocal
both in time and space, makes the mathematical analysis of the semiclassical equations both
mathematically intriguing and significantly more challenging to analyse than their classical
counterpart. Besides some recent progress in highly symmetric spacetimes by various different
authors, e.g. [211–213, 267, 268, 322, 360, 361, 392], establishing a well-posed Cauchy problem
for the semiclassical Einstein equations on general globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifolds
remains an important open problem.
To obtain a better understanding of semiclassical models, we remark that it has recently been
shown in [321] that the linearised semiclassical Einstein equations, coupled to a Klein-Gordon
field of mass m, in a cosmological spacetime can be written in the form
N1Kψ + N2ψ = ϕ
acting on the unknown scalar degree of freedom ψ, where N1 and N2 are normally hyperbolic
operators and ϕ a source term. The operator K is a continuous operator with Schwartz kernel
K(x, y) =
Z ∞
4m2
ρ(M2)
M2 + a(□+ a)G+
M2(x, y) ,
(1.29)
where a is a (renormalisation) constant, ρ a suitable smooth function and G+
M2(x, y) the kernel
of the retarded Green’s operator of the Klein-Gordon equation with mass M. Note that the
nonlocality enters in fact at the leading order.
Another example in the context of semiclassical models are the semiclassical Maxwell equa-
tions, i.e. the electromagnetic analogue of the semiclassical Einstein equations, see e.g. [187].
Despite the diversity of hyperbolic equations with nonlocal potentials/interactions, a unify-
ing mathematical treatment based on symmetric hyperbolic systems with nonlocal potentials
that provides a coherent and powerful language to investigate their dynamics is still missing.
Notable exceptions include the recent work [145], which considers modifications of Green hy-
perbolic operators through the addition of a possibly nonlocal operator acting within a (fixed)
compact subset in spacetime, as well as [32] on semilinear hyperbolic systems with nonlocal
potentials in Minkowski spacetime. In the present section, following [158], we aim to provide a
systematic study of the Cauchy problem for a symmetric hyperbolic system coupled to a large
class of nonlocal potentials.
1.3.1
Nonlocal Potentials
As a first part in our analysis, we need to establish a general framework for nonlocal po-
tentials on globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifolds. Furthermore, we need to define a suitable
class of nonlocal potentials for which the Cauchy problem when coupled to a symmetric hy-
perbolic system can be studied. Throughout this section, let (M, g) be a (1 + n)-dimensional
globally hyperbolic spacetime and t ∈C∞(M, R) a fixed Cauchy temporal function such that
M = R × Σ ,
g = −β2dt ⊗dt + ht ,
where Σ is a smooth spacelike Cauchy hypersurface, β ∈C∞(M, (0, ∞)) a lapse function and
(ht)t∈R a one-parameter family of Riemannian metrics on Σ depending smoothly on t. The
corresponding foliation of M by spacelike Cauchy surfaces is denoted by (Σt := {t} × Σ)t∈R.

can be generalised by replacing the wavefront set with the Sobolev wavefront set.

Two Classes of nonlocal Potentials.
Let B: Γ∞
c (E) →Γ∞(E) be a nonlocal potential with
time kernel (Bt,τ)t,τ∈R as in Definition 1.48. In full generality, there is no hope of establishing
well-posedness of the Cauchy problem for a symmetric hyperbolic system on M over (E, ⟨·, ·⟩E)
coupled to the nonlocal potential B. The main obstruction, as one might expect, is the non-
locality in time. Indeed, the Cauchy problem for operators of the form ∂t −A(t) on R1+n,
where t 7→A(t) ∈Ψ1(Rn) is a time-dependent pseudodifferential operator of order one (see
Appendix A.2 for details on pseudodifferential calculus) is classical and can be treated using
techniques closely analogous to those in the fully local setting; see, for example, [240, Sec. 7.1]
and [427, Sec. 7.7]. In that situation, where the nonlocality is purely spatial, the standard
energy-estimate arguments still yield well-posedness, under suitable regularity assumptions on
A(t). When a potential is also nonlocal in time, however, this fundamentally interferes with the
energy methods and prevents a direct extension of these arguments.
Therefore, we need to impose some restrictions on the nonlocality in time. To this end, we
consider two classes of nonlocal potentials, both of which are motivated by purely mathematical
reasons, but also by application to mathematical physics.


## Page 74


54
Chapter 1. Hyperbolic Operators on Lorentzian Manifolds
Definition 1.54. Let B: Γ∞
c (E) →Γ∞(E) be a nonlocal potential with time kernel.
(R) B is called retarded, if supp(Bφ) ⊂J +(supp(φ)) for all φ ∈Γ∞
c (E) and hence also
Bt,τ = 0 for τ > t. If, in addition, there exists a t0 ∈R such that Bt,τ = 0 for τ < t0,
we say that B is past compact with switch-on time t0.
(S) B has short time range, if there exists a δ > 0 such that Bt,τ = 0 for |t −τ| > δ.
Retarded nonlocal potentials are particularly well-suited for an initial mathematical inves-
tigation of the Cauchy problem due to their inherent causal structure and compatibility with
energy methods. Moreover, many of the motivating examples presented at the beginning of this
section fall into this class, for instance, the Maxwell equations in linear dispersive media and
semiclassical models. Considering nonlocal potentials with a small time range is a natural next
step in the mathematical analysis: in this regime one can examine the effects from allowing a
controlled, localised influence from future points, restricted to a small temporal neighbourhood.
From a physical point of view, nonlocal potentials of this type appear, for instance, in the
aforementioned collapse models from causal fermion systems, in which one studies the Dirac
equation coupled to a nonlocal potential with small time range.
Remark 1.55. The two types of nonlocal potentials introduced in Definition 1.54 have the
following mapping properties, cf. [33, Lemma 2.13]:
(i) If B is a retarded nonlocal potential, then it is easy to see that
supp(kB) ⊂{(x, y) ∈M × M | y ∈J −(x)} .
In particular, B can be extended uniquely to an operator B: Γ∞
pc(E) →Γ∞
pc(E).
(ii) If B has short time range δ > 0, then
supp(kB) ⊂{(t, ⃗x; τ, ⃗y) ∈M × M | |t −τ| ≤δ} .
In particular, B can be extended uniquely to an operator B: Γ∞
sc (E) →Γ∞(E).
Fur-
thermore, B is well-defined as a linear operator of the form B: Γ∞((0, T) × Σ, E) →
Γ∞((−δ, T + δ) × Σ, E) for all T > 0.

The Cauchy Problem of Nonlocal Symmetric Hyperbolic Systems

Note that, it is important in the above definition that the approximating sequence ψk is
defined not just on the time strip MT = [0, T] × Σ, but on a bigger domain. For example, in
the retarded case, if we want to know Bψk on MT, we need to specify ψk on [−t0, T] × Σ due to
the nonlocal character of the potential. Similar considerations hold true in the case of nonlocal
potentials with short time range. Moreover, we note that the boundedness assumption in time
ensures that B is well-defined on smooth sections that are L2 on each time slice. Let us also
remark that in principle, one could also define notion of weak solutions. Also in this case one
needs to use different time strips in the definition to account for the nonlocality. Last but not
least, we define classical solutions as follows.
Definition 1.60. (Classical Solutions)
(i) Let B be a retarded and past-compact nonlocal potential with switch-on time −t0 ≥0.
A classical solution on MT is a section ψ ∈Γ∞([−t0, T] × Σ, E) such that
(S −B)ψ = ϕ
pointwise for all t ∈[0, T] ,
and
ψ|Σ0 = f .
(ii) Let B be a nonlocal potential with small time range δ > 0. A classical solution on
MT is a section ψ ∈Γ∞([−δ, T + δ] × Σ, E) such that
(S −B)ψ = ϕ
pointwise for all t ∈[0, T] ,
and
ψ|Σ0 = f .
Clearly, if a strong solution is in addition smooth, then it is also a classical solution. Indeed,
any strong solution is a weak solution and if it is also smooth then it is classical.

Having introduced the relevant definitions and terminology, we are now in the position to

The first theorem concerns the well-posedness of the Cauchy problem 1.35 for retarded
nonlocal potentials.
Theorem 1.63. (Cauchy Problem for Retarded Nonlocalities)
Let (M, g), (E, ⟨·, ·⟩E, ∇E), S and B be as in Set-up 1.58. Furthermore, consider a time strip
MT := t−1([0, T]) with T > 0, Cauchy data (ϕ, f) ∈Γ∞(MT, E) × Γ∞(Σ0, E|Σ0) and assume
the following:
(i) B is a retarded nonlocal potential, cf. Definition 1.54(R).
(ii) B is past compact with switch-on time t0 = 0.
(iii) V := βσS(η)−1B is uniformly bounded in the time strip, i.e.

∀φ ∈Γ∞

(iv) the extended nonlocal potentials Bk admit time kernels and satisfy assumptions (i)-
(iii) above for k ∈N,
then the solution is smooth, unique and propagates at most with the speed of light, i.e.
supp(ψ) ∩J +(Σ0) ⊂J +(supp(ϕ) ∪supp(f)) .
A proof of this theorem will follow below. The second theorem concerns the well-posedness
of the Cauchy problem 1.35 for nonlocal potentials with a short time range.
Theorem 1.64. (Cauchy Problem for Nonlocal Potentials with Short Time Range)
Let (M, g), (E, ⟨·, ·⟩E, ∇E), S and B be as in Set-up 1.58. Furthermore, consider a time strip
MT := t−1([0, T]) with T > 0, Cauchy data (ϕ, f) ∈Γ∞(MT, E) × Γ∞(Σ0, E|Σ0) and assume
the following:
(i) B has short time range δ > 0, cf. Definition 1.54(S).

Let us highlight the main differences in the assumptions of Theorem 1.63 and Theorem 1.64.
First of all, note that in the retarded case we only assume uniform boundedness on the time
interval [0, T], whereas in the short-time-range case we require uniform boundedness on the
entire time domain. The reason for this distinction is as follows: the idea of the proof is to
construct local sections ψ(n) ∈Γ∞(E), labelled by n ∈N0, which are inductively defined as the
solutions of the local problems
(
Sψ(0)
= ϕ
ψ(0)|t=0
= f ,
(
Sψ(n+1)
= Bψ(n)
ψ(n+1)|t=0
= 0
∀n ∈N .
The goal is then to prove that the series ψ := P∞
n=0 ψ(n) converges (absolutely) in a suitable
Hilbert space and defines a (strong) solution to the Cauchy problem 1.35.
In the case of
Theorem 1.63, the assumptions on the nonlocal potential ensure that the source term for each
of these equations remains within the future of Σ0. In other words, due to the retarded property,
if we want to solve the Cauchy problem on the time strip MT, it is sufficient to study each of
the local Cauchy problems on the same time strip MT. In contrast, in the short-time-range
case, i.e. Theorem 1.64, the support of the source increases at each inductive step by ±δ in
time. Consequently, we must have full control over the solutions ψ(n) at all times. For this
reason, also an additional uniform boundedness assumption on the zeroth-order terms is required
that in addition has to be compatible with the bounds on the nonlocal potential. The bound
C < (8eδ2)−1, which will become clear in the proof, is needed in the short-time-range case to
guarantee convergence of the series.
Before we enter into the details of the proof, let us observe that at least the scaling C ≲δ−2
is actually optimal, i.e. there exist examples of symmetric hyperbolic systems and uniformly
bounded nonlocal potentials with short time range violating this bound, for which the Cauchy
problem does not for every source admit a solution.

Having proven the well-posedness of the Cauchy problem for symmetric hyperbolic systems
with retarded nonlocal potentials, it remains to prove well-posedness in the short-time-range
case.

## Page 89


Chapter2
Linear Gauge Theories on Manifolds
We now turn to linear gauge theories, which are linear field-theoretic models admitting local
gauge symmetries, and their quantisation. First of all, we discuss the mathematical structure
of classical linear gauge theories by focusing on an axiomatic framework introduced by Hack-
Schenkel [231], which generalises and abstracts the so-called subsidiary condition formalism.
In particular, we discuss the Cauchy problem for linear gauge theories and provide various
equivalent descriptions of the their classical phase space. Afterwards, we provide the reader
with many examples of direct physical interest, namely Maxwell’s theory, linearised Yang-Mills
theory, linearised Einstein gravity, as well as the coupled linearised Yang-Mills-Klein-Gordon
and Einstein-Klein-Gordon systems.
Having studied the classical theory in some detail, we
turn to their quantisation in the last part of this chapter. For this, we will adopt the so-called
algebraic approach to quantum field theory, which offers a mathematically rigorous quantisation
scheme, ideally suited for field theories defined on globally hyperbolic spacetimes. Within this
formalism, quantisation is essentially a two-step procedure: first, one assigns a non-commutative
∗-algebra of observables to the classical phase space, which encodes structural properties such
as the canonical commutation relations. The second step consists in choosing a state on that
algebra, that is, a positive, linear and normalised functional describing the physical preparation
of the system. Among the plethora of admissible mathematical states, not every one can be
considered physical and hence, we will introduce the notion of Hadamard states, which are
physically distinguished states whose two-point function has a specific singularity structure as a
bidistribution. This class of states plays a crucial role in ensuring the physical viability of a given
quantum theory by allowing for a well-defined notion of renormalisation and the formulation of
physically relevant observables.
2.1
The Formalism of Hack-Schenkel and Cauchy Problem
In this section, we discuss linear field-theoretic models admitting local gauge symmetries
on general globally hyperbolic spacetimes. As mentioned above, we follow the framework de-
veloped by Hack-Schenkel in [231] (see also [230, Sec. 2.2.2] for a textbook treatment), which
axiomatises the subsidiary condition formalism and generalises earlier treatments of Maxwell’s
theory [118] and linearised gravity [146] on curved backgrounds to more general linear gauge
theories. Related ideas have also been explored by Khavkine [279] in the context of the covariant
phase space formalism. Within this formalism, the classical theory underlying quantisation is
both well-understood and rigorously controlled, making it perfectly suited for the quantisation
procedure to be described in the subsequent sections. We stress that our focus is restricted
to linear gauge theories and a simple class of observables. In particular, there is no need to
introduce auxiliary fields (e.g. (anti)ghosts, Nakanishi–Lautrup fields, antifields, etc.) as in the
69


## Page 90


70
Chapter 2. Linear Gauge Theories on Manifolds
widely applied and highly successful Faddeev-Popov [144] (see [378, Chap. 9] for a mathemat-
ically oriented treatment), BRST [45–47, 432] or BV formalisms [39–41] (see also the reviews
in [239] and [367, Chap. 7]) for gauge theories. In fact, the equivalence of the formalism of Hack-
Schenkel and the BRST approach in the linear setting can straightforwardly be established and
has been studied in some detail by Wrochna-Zahn in [454].
2.1.1
The Axiomatic Framework
To begin with, we introduce the general definition of linear gauge theories within the Hack-
Schenkel formalism [231].
However, we shall adopt a slightly more refined version thereof
introduced and discussed in detail by Gérard-Wrochna in [198] and subsequently discussed and
applied in [51,192,194,202,340,454] in various different contexts.
Definition 2.1. (Linear Gauge Theory)
Let (M, g) be a globally hyperbolic spacetime and consider a quadruple (E1, E2, P, K) con-
sisting of the following data:
• Two Hermitian vector bundles (E1
π1
−→M, ⟨·, ·⟩E1) and (E2
π2
−→M, ⟨·, ·⟩E2).
• An operators K ∈DO(E1, E2) and a formally self-adjoint operator P ∈DO(E2).
We call (E1, E2, P, K) a linear gauge theory, if it satisfies the following two properties:
(i) P ◦K = 0
(ii) D1 := K∗◦K ∈DO(E1) and D2 := P + K ◦K∗∈DO(E2) are Green-hyperbolic.
Let (E1, E2, P, K) be a linear gauge theory on a globally hyperbolic spacetime (M, g). Then,
the various quantities involved in this setup admit the following interpretation:
• The bundle E2 is the bundle whose sections correspond to the gauge fields on M, while
sections of E1 encode the gauge transformation parameters.
• The operator P determines the equations of motion and hence the dynamics of the gauge
fields, while K describes the linear gauge transformations via
Γ∞(E2) ∋ψ 7→ψ + Kω
ω ∈Γ∞(E1) .
Condition (i) in Definition 2.1 then encodes gauge invariance of P in the sense that
Pψ = φ
⇔
P(ψ + Kω) = φ
∀ψ, φ ∈Γ∞(E2) , ω ∈Γ∞(E1) .
• The operator D2 is the gauge-fixed operator with respect to the canonical gauge condition
(or subsidiary condition) K∗u = 0. Green hyperbolicity of D2 ensures that the Cauchy
problem of the gauge-fixed operator is well-posed.
• Green hyperbolicity of D1 ensures that the canonical gauge condition can always be
achieved: let ψ ∈Γ∞(E2). Finding a gauge transformation ω ∈Γ∞(E1) such that ψ′ :=
ψ + Kω satisfies K∗ψ′ = 0 is equivalent to solving the differential equations D1ω = −K∗ψ,
which can always be done if D1 is hyperbolic.
Remark 2.2. In the original formulation of Hack-Schenkel, one considers three operators,
P ∈DO(E2) and K, T ∈DO(E1, E2). Condition (ii) in Definition 2.1 is then replaced by the
requirements that eD2 := P + TK∗, D1 := K∗K and eD1 := K∗T are Green hyperbolic. However,

2.1. The Formalism of Hack-Schenkel and Cauchy Problem
71
in virtually all examples of interest from a physical perspective, it turns out that the operators
T and K are equivalent up to a constant and by choosing the bundle metrics on E1 and E2
appropriately, one can arrange T = K. To the best of my knowledge, the only example of some
physical interest in which T and K are distinct and not proportional to each other is provided
by the fermionic Rarita-Schwinger gauge theory, in which case P and K are operators of order
one, while T an operator of order zero, see [231, Ex. 3.10].

2.1.2
The Cauchy Problem for Linear Gauge Theories
Let (M, g) be a globally hyperbolic spacetime and (E1, E2, P, K) be a linear gauge theory
as per Definition 2.1. The case K = 0 corresponds to the special case in which there is no
gauge symmetry present. In this case, the equations of motion described by the operator P are
themselves hyperbolic. Examples are provided, for instance, by Klein-Gordon, Dirac or Proca
fields. When K ̸= 0, however, it is not too hard to see that P is generically non-hyperbolic.
Intuitively, this can be easily seen as follows. Suppose ψ is a solution to the equation Pψ = 0
for some initial datum ψ|Σ = f on some spacelike Cauchy hypersurface Σ. Now, choose any
ω ∈Γ∞(E1) whose support lies sufficiently far in the future of Σ. Then, ψ′ := ψ + Kω is also a
solution of Pψ′ = 0 with the same initial datum. Hence, the Cauchy problem is not well-posed.
As it turns out, the operator P is in fact not even Green hyperbolic.
Lemma 2.4. Let (E1, E2, P, K) be a linear gauge theory. Then
K = 0
⇔
P is Green hyperbolic .

For “⇐”, assume that K ̸= 0.

account for the gauge redundancy: rather than considering all solutions to the equations of
motion, we are interested in gauge equivalent classes of solutions. More precisely the space we
want to work with is the quotient space

2.1.3
Classical Phase Space of a Linear Gauge Theory
Following the mathematical treatment of the Cauchy problem for linear gauge theories, we
now turn to the description of their classical phase space, namely the space of observables. For
pedagogical reasons, we first discuss the case of ordinary field theories, i.e. the special case
K = 0 in Definition 2.1. For more details in the context of Klein-Gordon, Proca and Dirac
fields, we refer to [50]. Let (M, g) be a globally hyperbolic spacetime and D ∈DO(E) be a

Remark 2.11. While the class of observables we consider has a relatively simple structure at
first sight, it is rich enough to distinguish between different configurations: for two off-shell
configurations, i.e. sections ψ, φ ∈Γ∞(E), we can always find an observable Of labelled by
some f ∈Γ∞
c (E) such that Of(ψ) ̸= Of(φ). A similar statement is true for linear on-shell
observables, since taking the quotient does not alter this property. From a physical perspective,
this means that there is no need to consider a larger class of observables than {Of}f∈Γ∞
c (E).
▲
In summary, the classical phase space Vc is in one-to-one correspondence with the space of
linear on-shell observables of the type (2.2). Before moving on to gauge theories, we conclude
this preliminary discussion with the observation that the causal propagator equips the phase
space Vc naturally with the structure of a Hermitian vector space.

To sum up, we define the classical phase space of a linear gauge theory as follows:
Definition 2.15. (Classical Phase Space of a Linear Gauge Theory)
Let (E1, E2, P, K) be a linear gauge theory. The pair (Vc, σ) defined by
Vc := ker(K∗|Γ∞
c )
ran(P|Γ∞
c ) ,
σ: Vc × Vc →C ,
([ψ], [φ]) 7→σ([ψ], [φ]) := (ψ, iGφ)E2
is called the classical phase space of (E1, E2, P, K).
Remark 2.16. (Degeneracy of σ)
We stress that σ is in general degenerate, as opposed to ordinary field theories. This point has
been discussed in some detailed by Hack-Schenkel in [231, Sec. 5]. In fact, it has been shown
therein that σ is degenerate whenever ran(K) ∩Γ∞
sc (E2) ⊂ran(K|Γsc) is a proper subspace.
However, let us emphasise that this is not an if-and-only-if statement. For Maxwell’s theory, it
has been shown in [109, Prop. 3.4] (generalising [100, Prop. 2.1]) that σ is weakly non-degenerate
if either H1(M) = 0 or H2(M) = 0. More generally, a similar statement for linearised Yang-
Mills theory with trivial principal bundles has been established in [231, Ex. 5.7]. For linearised
gravity, σ is weakly non-degenerate on spatially compact globally hyperbolic manifolds, as
shown in [146, Thm. 4.3]. The case of spatially non-compact spacetimes remains open.
Let us also remark, however, that the degeneracy of σ is of physical significance. In fact,
it has been argued that the degeneracy of σ for Maxwell’s theory in general is related to the
celebrated Aharonov-Bohm effect, see [393].
▲
Remark 2.17. (Gauge-Invariant Observables)
The interpretation of the space Vc follows the same lines of arguments as in the case of or-
dinary field theories without a gauge symmetry.
First of all, note that a linear observable
Of : Γ∞(E2) →C is gauge-invariant in the sense that
Of(ψ + Kω) = Of(ψ)
∀ψ ∈Γ∞(E2), ω ∈Γ∞(E1)
if and only if the label f is contained in the subspace ker(K∗|Γ∞
c ) ⊂Γ∞
c (E2), by the fact that
Of(Kω) = (f, Kω)E2 = (K∗f, ω)E1 and non-degeneracy of (·, ·)E1. Hence, for linear gauge theo-
ries, it makes sense to consider the linear subspace ker(K∗|Γ∞
c )/ran(P|Γ∞
c ) of Γ∞(E2)/ran(P|Γ∞
c )

cohomology theory. For other gauge theories, this remains an important open question.

2.2
Examples of Linear Gauge Theories
Having established an abstract axiomatic framework for linear gauge theories, we now turn
to a detailed discussion of some concrete examples of physical relevance. Specifically, we will
study Maxwell’s theory of electromagnetism, linearised Yang-Mills theory on arbitrary principal
bundles with possibly non-compact Lie groups as well as Einstein gravity in vacuum linearised
around general Einstein manifolds. Additionally, we will consider the linearised Yang-Mills-
Klein-Gordon and linearised Einstein-Klein-Gordon theories as examples of coupled systems. In
principle, one could of course also study fermionic theories, such as the linearised Yang-Mills-
Dirac system relevant to quantum electro- and chromodynamics, or at the very end, a linearised
version of the classical standard model of particle physics. Furthermore, one could also consider
pure fermionic gauge theories such as Rarita-Schwinger theory (see [231, Exp. 3.10]). However,
we will restrict our analysis to bosonic fields in the subsequent analysis.
2.2.1
Maxwell’s Theory
One of the most basic examples of a linear gauge theory and in fact one of the main moti-
vational examples for the Hack-Schenkel formalism following Dimock’s work [118] is Maxwell’s
theory of electrodynamics, which provides a unified geometric description of classical electromag-

Proposition 2.22. (Maxwell’s Theory as a Linear Gauge Theory)
Let (M, g) be a globally hyperbolic spacetime. Then, (A0, A1, P, K) with
P := δd ∈DO2(A1) ,
K := d ∈DO1(A0, A1)
is a linear gauge theory in the sense of Definition 2.1. The operators D1 := K∗K ∈DO2(A0)
and D2 := P + KK∗∈DO2(A1) are the de Rham Hodge d’Alembertians
D1 = □dRH = δd ,
D2 = □dRH = dδ + δd .
Proof. The operator P is clearly formally self-adjoint and clearly PK = 0 on account of dd = 0.
Moreover, since K∗= δ, it immediately follows that Di = □dRH : Ωi−1(M) →Ωi−1(M).
■
Note that P is more generally invariant under A 7→A + ω, where ω ∈ker(d|Ω1). On general
manifolds, not every closed 1-form is (globally) exact, with the obstructions classified by the de
Rham cohomology group H1(M) = ker(d|Ω1)/ran(d|Ω0). Nevertheless, in the context of gauge
theories, one usually restricts to transformations of the form ω = df, in which case Maxwell’s
theory emerges as a special case of Yang–Mills theory with Abelian gauge group U(1).
Remark 2.23. Maxwell’s equations were first introduced by Maxwell, scattered throughout
a series of papers published17 in 1861–1862 [314–317], building upon the works of Ampère,
Coulomb, Faraday, Lenz, Neumann, Ørsted and others. Notably, these papers already contained
a derivation of the speed of light. The unified form of Maxwell’s equations appeared in 1865 [318]
and were later summarised in a book from 1873 [319]. Maxwell originally wrote the equations
in component form.
Only later they were recast in the language of vector analysis (as in
Example 1.19) by Heaviside [237] with independent work by Gibbs and Hertz. The formulation
in terms of differential forms emerged at the beginning of the 20th century, through the work
of Cartan (e.g. [84]), Weyl (e.g. [445]) and others.
▲
17 One of Maxwell’s main contributions was the addition of the term ∂tE to Ampére’s law rotΣ(B) = 0, which is
crucial for the derivation of the electromagnetic wave equation and the unification of electricity and magnetism.

2.2.2
Linearised Yang-Mills Theory
One of the most foundational examples of a gauge theory in physics is Yang-Mills theory.
It was initially formulated by Yang and Mills in 1954 [458] in order to generalise gauge theories
with abelian gauge groups, which were well understood at the time, to the non-abelian case. In
particular, they were interested in the case of SU(2) in an attempt to explain isospin conservation
in strong interaction processes.18 The modern mathematical formulation using connections and
principal fibre bundles goes back to Wu-Yang [455] (see [456] for a historical account of the
same authors) and Atiyah-Hitchin-Singer [28,29], which also marks the beginning of a time of
intense mathematical interest and work on gauge theories. From the physical point of view,
Yang-Mills theory is a pure gauge theory and serves as the foundation of the celebrated standard
model of particle physics (see e.g. [43,235,378] for a mathematically oriented introduction to the
classical theory), which is essentially a Yang-Mills theory with gauge group SU(3)×SU(2)×U(1),
complemented by a suitable matter sector containing the quarks, leptons and the Higgs boson.
Besides its importance in theoretical and mathematical physics, it also has profound applications
in pure mathematics, as already indicated above. For instance, the study of instantons and
the moduli space of solutions to the Yang-Mills equations has led to deep new insights into
the topology of smooth 4-manifolds (e.g. [120, 121], see also the monographs of Donaldson-
Kronheimer [122], Freed-Uhlenbeck [170] and Scorpan [405] on that subject and related topics).
While a fully non-perturbative quantum Yang-Mills theory remains out of reach (see, e.g.,
the Millennium Prize problem statement of Jaffe-Witten on the mass gap problem [262]), one
can study the linearised theory as a simpler toy model to capture low-energy effects of the
expected quantum theory. Linearised Yang-Mills theory as a linear gauge theory in the sense of
Definition 2.1 has been considered in the original article of Hack-Schenkel [231, Ex. 3.7] as well
as in the work of Gérard-Wrochna on Hadamard states for linearised Yang-Mills theory [198],
both in the case of trivial principal bundles and with semisimple compact Lie groups. The
non-trivial case was briefly sketched in Wrochna-Zahn [454, Sec. 4.2].
In this section, we explore linearised Yang-Mills theory as a linear gauge theory within the
Hack-Schenkel formalism, working with non-trivial principal bundles and (possibly non-compact
and not necessarily semisimple) Lie groups whose Lie algebra admits an invariant bilinear form.
Set-up 2.24. We consider the following general setting:
• A d-dimensional globally hyperbolic spacetime (M, g).
• A (finite-dimensional, real) Lie group G with Lie algebra (g, [·, ·]g) and a choice of
Ad-invarianta non-degenerate symmetric bilinear form ⟨·, ·⟩g on g.
• A principal G-bundle P π−→M with corresponding right action rg : P →G, p·g := rg(p).

Remark 2.25. Requiring the existence of an Ad-invariant bilinear form ⟨·, ·⟩g imposes a re-
striction on the choice of Lie group G. Nevertheless, the class of admissible Lie groups is fairly
general and G does not need to be a matrix Lie group. First, every abelian Lie group trivially
satisfies this assumption. Next, if G is semisimple, then such a bilinear form always exists, since
the Killing form κ: g×g →R, defined by κ(X, Y ) = tr(adX ◦adY ), provides an example in this
case. Moreover, also compact Lie groups are contained in this class, since in this case, one can
use the Haar measure on G to average any inner product on g and obtain an Ad-invariant one.
The examples above also cover the most important class of Lie groups for applications in
physics: if G is compact and simple (such as SU(n), n ∈N), and if ⟨·, ·⟩g is positive-definite,
then ⟨·, ·⟩g is in fact necessarily a (negative) multiple of the Killing form of g. In particular, if G
is a simple, compact matrix Lie group, the bilinear form takes the form ⟨X, Y ⟩g ∝tr(XY ). The
constant of proportionality corresponds to the coupling constant in physics terminology.
▲
Before stating the Yang-Mills equations, we need to briefly review the relevant definitions of
mathematical gauge theory, that is, the differential geometry of principal fibre bundles. We refer
to the textbooks [43,235,378] for details and proofs for the following well-known constructions.

The Yang-Mills equation is the non-linear equation

been studied by Segal [409], Choquet-Bruhat-Christodoulou [94], Eardley-Moncrief [128, 129]

The Yang-Mills equations are gauge-invariant in the following sense: a (global) gauge trans-
formation is a principal bundle automorphism, i.e. a diffeomorphism f ∈Diff(P, P) that pre-
serves the fibres, i.e. π ◦f = π, and that is G-equivariant, i.e. f(p · g) = f(p) · g for all p ∈P and
g ∈G. The group of (global) gauge transformations, denoted by G(P), is naturally isomorphic
to the group of G-equivariant smooth maps from P to G, i.e.
G(G) ∼= C∞(P, G)G := {σ ∈C∞(P, G) | ∀p ∈P, g ∈G : σ(p · g) = g−1σ(p)g},
f 7→σf ,
where σf(p) ∈G is uniquely defined by f(p) = p · σf(p) for all p ∈P. A gauge transformation
f acts on a connection 1-form A ∈C(P) via pull-back, i.e.
A 7→f∗A = Adσ−1

where µG ∈Ω1(G, g) denotes the Maurer-Cartan form23 of G. In the associate bundle E = P×ρW,
gauge transformations act as
G(P) × Ex →Ex ,
(f, [p, v]) 7→f · [p, v] := [f(p), v] = [p · σf(p), v] = [p, ρ(σf(p)−1)v] ,
which is clearly well-defined by G-equivariancy of σf. On sections of E, or more generally forms
in Ωk(M, E), gauge transformations are defined pointwise.24 Now, it is easy to see that

2.2.3
Linearised Yang-Mills-Klein-Gordon System
So far, we have only considered pure gauge theories. As a first step towards more real-
istic models, we now study the coupled linearised Yang–Mills–Klein–Gordon system. At the
nonlinear level, this system is of direct physical interest, for example in the celebrated Higgs
mechanism within the standard model of particle physics, see e.g. [235, Chap. 8], [378, Sec. 7.3]
and [59, Sec.10.3] for a mathematical discussion.
As in the previous section on Yang-Mills theory, we will consider the following general set-up.


## Page 110


90
Chapter 2. Linear Gauge Theories on Manifolds
Set-up 2.31. We consider the following general setting:
• A d-dimensional globally hyperbolic spacetime (M, g).
• A (real, finite-dimensional) Lie group G with Lie algebra (g, [·, ·]g) and a choice of
Ad-invariant and non-degenerate symmetric bilinear form ⟨·, ·⟩g on g.
• A principal G-bundle P π−→M.
• A Lie group representation ρ: G →Aut(W) of G on some finite-dimensional R-vector
space W equipped with an ρ-invariant non-degenerate symmetric bilinear form ⟨·, ·⟩W
and the corresponding associated vector bundle E := P ×ρ W.
• A potential V ∈C∞(R).
The natural bundle metrics on Ad(P) and E induced by ⟨·, ·⟩g and ⟨·, ·⟩W, cf. Eq. (2.9),
are denoted by ⟨·, ·⟩Ad(P) and ⟨·, ·⟩E, as before. Now, for a given potential V, we will use the
suggestive notation V(ϕ) := V ◦∥ϕ∥2
E ∈C∞(M) for ϕ ∈Γ∞(E). An important example relevant
for the Higgs mechanism is the Mexican hat potential
V(ϕ) = −µ2∥ϕ∥2
E + λ∥ϕ∥4
E
for parameters µ2, λ ≥0, however, for our purpose it is enough to assume that V is smooth.
With this notation, the Yang-Mills-Klein-Gordon equations are the coupled system
0 = YMKGV(A, ϕ) :=

for (A, ϕ) ∈C(P) × Γ∞(E), where J(A, ϕ) ∈Ω1(M, Ad(P)) denotes the current describing the
interaction of ϕ with the gauge field A.
The operator □∇A
dRH := δAdA : Γ∞(E) →Γ∞(E) is
the de Rham-Hodge d’Alembertian of the connection ∇A and hence in particular normally
hyperbolic. Existence and uniqueness of solutions to Eq. (2.19) has for example been studied
by Ginibre-Velo [205,206], Eardley-Moncrief [128,129], Choquet-Bruhat-Christodoulou [94] and
Goganov-Kapitanskii [210] in Minkowski spacetime.
Now, before linearising the theory, we need an explicit expression of the current J(A, ϕ). The
derivation in a set-up that is similarly general than the one considered in this section is briefly
sketched in [235, Exercise 7.9.10] and [378, Sec. 7.2]. However, an explicit expression of J(A, ϕ)
in full generality is rarely found in the literature. Let us introduce the following notation: fix
v, w ∈W. Then, by non-degeneracy of ⟨·, ·⟩g, there exists a unique J(v, w) ∈g such that
⟨X, J(v, w)⟩g = ⟨v, ρ∗(X)w⟩W
(2.20)
for all X ∈g. This defines a map J: W × W →g with the following properties.
Lemma 2.32. The map J: W × W →g defined by (2.20) has the following properties.
(i) J is antisymmetric and bilinear.
(ii) J(ρ(g)v, ρ(g)w) = Adg(J(v, w)) for all v, w ∈W and g ∈G, i.e. J is G-equivariant.
(iii) J(ρ∗(X)v, w) + J(v, ρ∗(X)w) = [X, J(v, w)]g for all v, w ∈W and X ∈g.

2.2.4
Linearised Einstein Gravity
General relativity is one of the cornerstones of modern physics. Since it was firstly published
by Einstein around 1915 [136–140], many experiments have shown the great success of this
theory in many of its aspects, two of the most recent ones being the detection of gravitational
waves in September 2015 [1] as well as the first in situ observations of black holes, with the
central black hole of the elliptic galaxy M87 announced in 2019 [5–10] and the Milky Way’s
central black hole Sagittarius A∗following in 2023 [11–16].
In Einstein’s theory of general relativity, spacetime and gravity is modelled by a Lorentzian
manifold (M, g) and the dynamics is governed by Einstein’s field equations
EinΛ(g) = κT
with
EinΛ(g) := Ric(g) −1
2Scal(g)g + Λg
(2.23)
for a cosmological constant Λ ∈R, where T ∈Γ∞(T∗M⊗s2) is a divergence-free tensor, called
the energy-momentum tensor, modelling the matter content of the theory and where κ :=
8πG/c4. Exact solutions to (2.23) include Minkowski spacetime, black hole spacetimes such as
Schwarzschild and Kerr solutions for T = 0, as well as Reissner-Nordström and Kerr-Newman
spacetimes for T ̸= 0. Another family of solutions are the FLRW-metrics relevant for cosmology,
see Example 1.10(ii). From a mathematical perspective, Equation (2.23) can be formulated as
a hyperbolic problem subject to constraint equations whenever (M, g) is globally hyperbolic,
ensuring a well-posed Cauchy problem. This result is originally due to Choquet-Bruhat and
Geroch [95,168], building up on the observation of Lanczos that the Ricci tensor can be written
as a nonlinear wave operator acting on g in suitable coordinates [295]. For a more in-depth
treatment on this subject, see the monographs of Choquet-Bruhat [93] and Ringström [369],
the latter being based on a coordinate-free approach developed by DeTurck [114, 115]; for a
comparison, see [214]. An alternative prove, in which the Einstein equations are equivalently
viewed as a first-order quasilinear hyperbolic system, is due to Fischer-Marsden [164].
Besides its clear mathematical structure, it is very challenging in practise to find exact
solutions to (2.23). Consequently, numerical methods are often employed, or some form of ap-
proximation is required. In particular, when the gravitational field is weak in a certain sense,
linearising Einstein’s field equations provides an effective approach for modelling gravitational
effects. Historically, this approach has been used by Einstein himself to predict the existence
of gravitational waves in vacuum [141, 142] and has since then found many applications. In
particular, in the absence of a fully satisfactory theory of quantum gravity, the linearised the-
ory serves as a useful framework for describing low-energy effects of quantum gravity, where
linearised gravity is treated as a quantum field theory in a fixed background spacetime. In this
section, we will see that linearised gravity provides in fact an example of a linear gauge theory in
the sense of Definition 2.1 and hence can be quantised in a similar fashion as Maxwell’s theory.
The structure of the classical theory of linearised gravity needed for quantisation is nowadays
relatively well understood, see e.g. [70, 146, 281]. It what follows, we will provide the reader
with a systematic treatment of the theory starting from Einstein’s equation (2.23). In order
to derive the linearised Einstein equations, we will consider a one-parameter family of metrics
ˆg(λ) depending smoothly on a parameter λ ∈R, which then allows us to formally expand
ˆg(λ) = g + λh + O(λ2) ,

sufficient to work on a formal level.
We remark, however, that there are various ways to
make this precise. For instance, Stewart-Walker [421] considered a family of Lorentzian mani-
folds (Mλ, gλ) embedded in a higher-dimensional ambient manifold eM as a set-up for rigorous
perturbation theory. Alternatively, one might give the space of Lorentzian metrics L(M) on
a given manifold M the structure of an ∞-dimensional Fréchet manifold with tangent space
TgL(M) ∼= Γ∞(T∗M⊗s2) (see e.g. the classical article [130]), in order to consider ˆg(λ) as a curve
in this infinite-dimensional space. This approach is for example taken in Fischer-Marsden’s work
on the ADM formalism of gravity and linearised stability [165]; see also the reviews [166,167].
To obtain the linearised Einstein equations, we first have to linearise the Ricci tensor and the
scalar curvature. While this computation is straightforward and the obtained results are widely
applied and well-documented in the literature (see, e.g., [93, Sec. I.11]), explicit derivations
are surprisingly rare.
Notable exceptions include [58, Sec. 1.K] and the classical article of
Lichnerowicz [309]. This warrants the detailed proof of the following Lemma:

For the following discussion, it will be useful to establish some notation for symmetric tensor
fields, which will allow us to perform computations in a coordinate-free manner. First of all, in
what follows, (M, g) will be a globally hyperbolic spacetime of dimension d = 1 + 3. On this
manifold, we denote the bundles whose sections of symmetric (0, k)-tensor fields equipped with
their natural bundle metrics by
Sk := T∗M⊗sk,

So far, we have linearised the Einstein equations for general background metrics g. As a next
step, we explore the gauge-invariance of the obtained linearised equation. The gauge symmetry
of the full, non-linear, Einstein equations (2.23) is given by diffeomorphism invariance, i.e.
Φ∗EinΛ(g) = EinΛ(Φ∗g)
for an arbitrary diffeomorphism Φ ∈Diff∞(M). In other words, the gauge group of the Einstein
equations is Diff∞(M) and parametrised by g 7→Φ∗g. The linearised gauge transformations
should hence correspond to transformations of the form h 7→h + LXg, where LX denotes the
Lie derivative with respect to a vector field X ∈X(M).

and
R ◦ds = 1
2L·♯RicΛ(g) .
Hence, L ◦ds = 0 and R ◦ds = 0 if and only if the background g is a Λ-Einstein metric.

Proposition 2.44. (Linearised Gravity as a Linear Gauge Theory)
Let (M, g) be a globally hyperbolic Λ-Einstein manifold. Then, (S1, S2,I, P, K) with
P := −□+ 2Riemg −Idsδs ∈DO2(S2,I) ,
K := Ids ∈DO1(S1, S2,I)
is a linear gauge theory in the sense of Definition 2.1. The corresponding operators D1 :=
K†K and D2 := P + KK† are the normally hyperbolic operators
D1 = −□−Λ ,
D2 = −□+ 2Riemg .
Proof. By definition, P := 2LI and hence, by Proposition 2.41, PK = 2LI2ds = 2Lds = 0, since
the background metric is assumed to be Λ-Einstein. Furthermore, L is formally self-adjoint with
respect to (·, ·)S2 by Proposition 2.43 and hence, it is clear that P = 2LI is formally self-adjoint
with respect to (·, ·)S2,I, since
P† = 2(LI)† = 2I†L† = 2I2L∗I = 2ILI = P .
The operator D2 = P + KK† = −□+ 2Riemg is clearly normally hyperbolic and hence in
particular Green hyperbolic. Furthermore, by Lemma 2.39(iiic), we obtain
D1 = K†K = δsIds = −□−Λ ,
which is a normally hyperbolic operator as well.
■
The example above has been considered in a modified form in the original article of Hack-
Schenkel [231, Example 3.8]. Linearised gravity in the sense above and in the context of the
Hack-Schenkel formalism has also been considered in the works related to the construction of
Hadamard states for linearised gravity by Benini-Murro-Dappiaggi [51], Gérard-Murro-Wrochna
[194], Gérard [192] and Gérard-Wrochna [202].
There is yet another possibility of fitting linearised gravity in the Hack-Schenkel framework
for linear gauge theories, which has been not yet discussed in this context. It is well known that
the four-dimensional Einstein equations (2.23) are equivalent to its trace-reversed form, i.e.
EinΛ(g) = 0
⇔
Ric(g) −Λg = 0 .
Hence, instead of considering the linearised Einstein operator, we may consider the linearisation
of Ric(g) −Λg instead, i.e. the operator R −Λ, where R is the linearised Ricci operator as
introduced in Proposition 2.40. If the background metric is Λ-Einstein, it holds that
L = I ◦(R −Λ) ,
which corresponds to the same relation on the non-linear level. In other words, the diagram

In other words, energy-momentum conservation enforces the scalar field ϕ to satisfy the Klein-

0 = EKGΛ,V(g, ϕ) :=

known as the Einstein-Klein-Gordon equations. For later convenience, we record that the first
line, Einstein’s equation EinΛ(g) = TV(g, ϕ), is equivalent to the trace-reversed equation
Ric(g) = dsϕ ⊗dsϕ + (Λ + V(ϕ))g .
(2.30)
Well-posedness of Eq. (2.29) can be studied similarly as in the vacuum case, see for instance
the monograph [369]. The corresponding linearised system, viewed as a linear gauge theory,
has been studied by Hack in [229] (see also [230, Sec. 3.3]). Our exposition differs from that
of [229] in two respects. First, while [229] employs a more general framework for linear gauge
theories, as briefly recalled in Remark 2.2, we show that the system under consideration also fits
naturally into the refined framework of Definition 2.1. Second, our presentation is formulated
in a global and coordinate-free language, in contrast to the local, coordinate-based approach
in [229], which allows us to derive more geometrically transparent expressions.
The linearised Einstein-Klein-Gordon equations and their quantisation play a crucial role
in cosmology, especially in the study of cosmological perturbations. These perturbations are
fundamental to modern theoretical cosmology, providing the framework for understanding the
origin and evolution of structure in the universe. One of the central questions in this field is
how primordial inhomogeneities, which later evolve into the anisotropies observed in the cosmic
microwave background, eventually lead to the large-scale distribution of galaxies we observe
today. The inflationary paradigm offers an explanation for these perturbations. According to
this theory, quantum fluctuations in a scalar field, the inflaton, serve as the seeds for cosmological
structure. Initially, these fluctuations exist on Planckian scales, but during the rapid inflationary
expansion of space postulated in the very early universe, they are “stretched” to macroscopic
distances. This process hence creates a direct link between high-energy microphysics and the
large-scale structure of the universe, making inflation a key testbed for fundamental physics.
We refer to [338,339] for more details on the physics side; see also [68] for a discussion from the
angle of algebraic quantum field theory.

2.3
Quantisation of Linear Gauge Theories
Having discussed the Cauchy problem and the phase space of classical linear field theories
with a gauge symmetry in depth, together with a detailed analysis of various examples with
direct relevance to physical applications, we now turn our attention to the next crucial step,
their quantisation. We will follow the algebraic approach to quantum field theory, which offers
a mathematically rigorous and conceptually compelling quantisation scheme, ideally suited for
field theories defined on curved Lorentzian backgrounds. After establishing the foundational
features of algebraic quantum field theory, we proceed by discussing the concept of Hadamard
states, which play a central role in ensuring the physical viability of quantum field theories on
curved spacetimes. In this context, we review the Gérard-Wrochna formalism of quantising
linear gauge theories and Hadamard states developed in [198] (see also [192, 202, 340]). We
conclude this section by providing a detailed bibliography of various existence results and explicit
constructions of Hadamard states for a wide range of different field theories.
2.3.1
A Glance of Algebraic Quantum Field Theory
As mentioned above, we will follow the so-called algebraic approach to quantum field theory
(short: AQFT). AQFT roots back to the pioneering works of Haag, Kastler [225, 228] and
Araki [22, 23] in the early 1960s (see also the foundational monographs [24, 227] as well as
early applications to scattering theory in [226, 379]), with earlier works on algebraic quantum
mechanics by Jordan-von Neumann-Wigner [265] and Segal [407,408], and is nowadays a well-
established and rigorous formalism for quantum field theory. Instead of working with quantum
fields in the sense of operator-valued distributions, as in the pioneering program on mathematical
and axiomatic quantum field theory initiated by Gårding-Wightman [188,422] in the 1950s (see


## Page 132


112
Chapter 2. Linear Gauge Theories on Manifolds
also the detailed treatment in [366, Chap. IX, Sec. 8]), the point of view in AQFT is shifted to
local observables and their algebraic relations. Quantum fields are then merely tools that emerge
as a way of labelling observables. In this framework, the quantisation is interpreted as a two
step procedure. First, one assigns a ∗-algebra of observables, which encodes structural properties
such as causality, dynamics and canonical commutation relations, to a physical bosonic system
described by a classical phase space. The second step calls for the identification of a physical
state, i.e. a positive, linear and normalised functional on the algebra of observables.
To start with, we recall some basic notions of linear algebra:
• A C-algebra is a pair (A, ·) consisting of a C-vector space A and a C-bilinear product
·: A × A →A. It is called unital, if there exists a two-sided multiplicative unit, i.e. an
element 1A such that 1A · a = a · 1A = a for all a ∈A. It is easy to see that the unit is
unique whenever it exists. Last but not least, a ∗-algebra is a C-algebra (A, ·) together
with an antilinear involution ∗: A →A satisfying (a · b)∗= b∗· a∗.
• If (A, ·A) and (B, ·B) are two C-algebra, an algebra homomorphism is a C-linear map
φ: A →B preserving the products, i.e. φ(a ·A b) = φ(a) ·B φ(b) for all a, b ∈A. If φ
is bijective, it is called an algebra isomorphism. If both A and B are unital, an algebra
homomorphism is called unital if it preserves the unit, i.e. φ(1A) = 1B. If both A and
B are ∗-algebras, an algebra homomorphism is called a ∗-homomorphism if preserves the
involutions, i.e. φ(a∗A) = φ(a)∗B for all a ∈A.
• Given a C-algebra (A, ·), a linear subspace I ⊂A is called a two-sided ideal, if a·b, b·a ∈I
for all a ∈I and b ∈A. If A is a ∗-algebra, then a two-sided ∗-ideal is a two-sided ideal
that is in addition closed under the ∗-involution, i.e. a∗∈I for all a ∈I.
Now, suppose we are given an arbitrary set G. Then, we wish to define the “smallest” unital
∗-algebra AG such that G ⊂AG. As usual, those kind of constructions can abstractly be defined
by means of a universal property: given an arbitrary (not necessarily countable) set G, a free
unital ∗-algebra generated by G is a unital ∗-algebra AG together with a map α: G →AG such
that for every other pair (B, β) consisting of a unital ∗-algebra and map β : G →B, there exists
a unique unital ∗-homomorphism φ: AG →B such that the following diagram commutes:
G
AG
B
β
α
φ
As usual, the universal property implies that the pair (AG, α) is unique up to canonical iso-
morphism and hence it makes sense to speak of the free unital ∗-algebra AG generated by G.
Existence can be shown by an explicit construction: let S be the set of finite, ordered tuples
of elements in G, i.e. S := {(g1, . . . , gk) | k ∈N0 , gi ∈G} and consider a C-vector space AG
spanned by a basis labelled by S, i.e. (eS)S∈S. We set 1 := e(), where () denotes the empty
tuple, and define a multiplication ·: AG × AG →AG by 1 · eS = eS · 1 = eS and eS · eT := eS·T ,
where S · T := (g1, . . . , gkh1, . . . , hl) for S = (g1, . . . , gk) and T = (h1, . . . , hl). Furthermore,
let ∗: G →G be any involution and define ∗: AG →AG by 1∗= 1 and (eS)∗= eS∗, where
S∗= (∗gk, . . . , ∗g1) for S = (g1, . . . , gk). With this definition, it is easy to see that AG together
with the map γ(g) := e(g) satisfies the above universal property.
In many applications, one often seeks to construct the smallest algebra that contains a
certain given sets while also satisfying specific relations among its generators. For instance,
given a set of generators G, we asks for the smallest unital ∗-algebra containing G such that
g∗= g for all g ∈G. To construct this algebra, we consider the two-sided ∗-ideal spanned by
the relation we want to impose, for instance, g−g∗for all g ∈G in the provided example. Then,


## Page 133


2.3. Quantisation of Linear Gauge Theories
113
we set AG,I := AG/I. If there is more than one relation one want to impose, one considers the
corresponding intersection of ideals. We remark that the so-constructed algebra AG,I can again
be characterised by means of a universal property, see e.g. [282, Sec. 5.1.2].
After this preliminary discussion, we return to algebraic quantisation. As mentioned above,
the first step consists of assigning a unital ∗-algebra to a given classical phase space.
Definition 2.51. (CCR-Algebra)
Let (V, σ) be a pair consisting of a C-vector space together with a (possibly degenerate)
Hermitian sesquilinear form σ (for instance, the classical phase space of a linear gauge
theory). Then, the CCR-algebra CCR(V, σ) is the unital ∗-algebra generated by an identity
1 and elements {Φ(v), Φ∗(v)}v∈V subject to the following relations:
(i)
Φ(v)∗= Φ∗(v)
(ii)
v 7→Φ(v) is C-antilinear and v 7→Φ∗(v) is C-linear
(iii)
[Φ(v), Φ(w)] = [Φ∗(v), Φ∗(w)] = 0
and
[Φ(v), Φ∗(w)] = σ(v, w)1
The relation [Φ(v), Φ∗(w)] = σ(v, w)1 are called canonical commutation relations.
Remark 2.52. We restrict our analysis to bosonic field theories.
For fermionic theories,
such as the Dirac or Rarita-Schwinger field theories, one needs to consider the canonical anti-
computation relations, i.e. replacing the commutators in (iii) above by anti-commutators.
▲
Having defined a non-commutative algebra of observables, the second step consists of the
choice of a state. From a physical perspective, an observable is a quantity which we want to
measure. Now, it is essential to assume that experiments can be reliably repeated, enabling a
consistent measurement of a given observable under identical conditions, which is a fundamen-
tal requirement for an empirical science. Such conditions, the preparation of the system, are
described by the choice of a state, that assigns to a given observable the expectation value of
the relevant measurement in the system. To this end, we make the following definition.
Definition 2.53. (Algebraic States)
Let (A, ·, 1, ∗) be a complex unital ∗-algebra. A C-linear functional ω: A →C is called a
state if the following two conditions hold:
(i) ω(1) = 1
(“normalisation”)
(ii) ω(a∗a) ≥0 for all a ∈A.
(“positivity”)
Remark 2.54. Note that the definition implies ω(a∗) = ω(a) for all a ∈A. Indeed,
0 ≤ω((a + λ1)∗(a + λ1)) = ω(a∗a) + |λ|2 + λω(a) + λω(a∗) ≤λω(a) + λω(a∗)
for all λ ∈C and a ∈A, which implies λω(a) + λω(a∗) ∈R. Choosing λ = i, this implies
Re(ω(a)) = Re(ω(a∗)) and choosing λ = 1, we obtain Im(ω(a)) = −Im(ω(a∗)).
▲
Remark 2.55. (The GNS-Construction)
Let us briefly explain how algebraic quantum mechanics and field theory are related to the
traditional Hilbert space approach. Let A be a unital ∗-algebra and ω: A →C be a state.
Then, there exists a quadruple (Hω, Dω, Ωω, πω) consisting of a Hilbert space Hω, a dense
subspace Dω ⊂Hω, a vector Ωω ∈Hω and a unital ∗-representation27 πω : Aω →L(Dω, Hω),
27 A representation of a unital ∗-algebra A on a fixed dense subspace D of some Hilbert space H is an algebra
homomorphism π: A →L(D, H), where L(D, H) is the algebra of linear operators T : D →H that are closable
and that satisfy ran(T) ⊂D. It is a unital ∗-representation, if π(1) = idD, if D ⊂D(π(a)∗) for all a ∈A,
where D(π(a)∗) ⊂H denotes the domain of π(a)∗, and if π(a∗) = π(a)∗|D for all a ∈A, see e.g. [399, Chap. 8].

computation shows that (Hω, Dω, Ωω, πω) with Ωω := [1] and πω(a)[b] := [ab] provides the
required quadruple. For more details, we refer to [282, Thm. 5.1.13] or [148, Thm. 10].

2.3.2
Hadamard States
The definition of states given in Definition 2.53 is fairly general and just includes the min-
imal physical requirements. Among the plethora of all possible states, however, most of them
have pathological physical behaviour and we need to find a selecting criterion to distinguish
those which are physical “acceptable”. While in Minkowski spacetime there is always a pre-
ferred state28, namely the vacuum, characterised by its invariance and stability properties with
respect to the Poincaré symmetry group, it turns out this is a highly non-trivial task on general
Lorentzian backgrounds.
Let CCR(V, σ) be a CCR-algebra and ω a state on it. By definition, every a ∈CCR(V, σ)
can be written as a finite linear combination of products of the generators 1, Φ(vi), Φ∗(wj).
Hence, the state ω is completely determined by its correlation functions,
ωm,n : Vm+n

→C ,
ωm,n(v1, . . . , vm, w1, . . . , wn) := ω(Φ(v1) . . . Φ(vn)Φ∗(w1) . . . Φ∗(wn)) .
With this notation, we will only consider states contained in the following class.
Definition 2.56. (Quasifree States)
A state ω on CCR(V, σ) is called quasi-free or Gaussian, if its purely determined by its
2-point function ω1,1(·, ·) = ω(Φ(·)Φ∗(·)) in the sense that
ωm,n(v1, . . . , vm, w1, . . . , wn) = δmn

In other words, for a quasi-free state, only the correlations functions with the same number
of Φ- and Φ∗-fields are non-zero and in this case, they are given by the the sum over all pairwise
28 More generally, there always exists a preferred state on a stationary spacetime, that is, on a globally hyperbolic
spacetime for which there exists a complete and future-directed timelike Killing vector field ξ, namely the
ground state that is invariant under the isometries induced by ξ and that has minimal energy in a suitable
sense, see [192, Chap. 9] and [282, Sec. 5.2] for details.

free quantum scalar field and the vacuum state in the standard approach to quantum field theory
in Minkowski spacetime (after [448]; see [356, Sec. 4.3] for a textbook treatment from a physics
perspective). This is also related to the Isserlis theorem [258,259] of probability theory, which
allows to express higher-order momenta of (zero-mean) random Gaussian variables in terms
of their covariances in a similar manner.
In other words, quasi-free states mimic Gaussian
probability distributions, which is the origin of the name Gaussian.
Now, if ω is a quasifree state on CCR(V, σ), we define its 2-point functions to be the
sesquilinear forms Λ± : V × V →C defined by
Λ+(v, w) := ω1,1(v, w) := ω(Φ(v)Φ∗(w)) ,
Λ−(v, w) := ω(Φ(w)∗Φ(v)) .
Following Remark 2.54, it is easy to see that Λ± are Hermitian. Of course, Λ+ and Λ−are not
independent, but rather satisfy Λ+ −Λ−= σ, as a consequence of the canonical commutation
relation [Φ(v), Φ∗(w)] = σ(v, w)1. Furthermore, Λ± are positive in the sense that Λ±(v, v) ≥0
for all v ∈V. By definition, ω is completely determined by Λ±. On the other hand, every pair
of Hermitian sesquilinear forms Λ± on V satisfying
(i)

gives rise to a unique quasi-free state on CCR(V, σ).

Focusing on quasi-free states coming from pseudo-covariances already provides a restriction
on the class of all states on the CCR-algebra CCR(Vc, σ). However, there is still a plethora of
states and we need a further selection criterion. A main motivation, we consider yet another


## Page 137


2.3. Quantisation of Linear Gauge Theories
117
essential property of the Minkowski vacuum state. So far we have only considered linear ob-
servables. However, from a physical perspective, it is clear that also non-linear observables play
an essential role. For instance, consider the energy-momentum tensor of a massive scalar field
(see Eq. (2.28)) with potential V(ϕ) := m2ϕ2 for some m ∈R>0, which contains also quadratic
expressions of the fields such as ϕ2 and ∥dsϕ∥2
S1 = gαβ(∇αϕ)(∇βϕ).
Now, in the following, we briefly sketch how this problem can be overcome in the context
of the real Klein-Gordon field in Minkowski spacetime, without dwelling into technical details.
We refer to the extensive discussions in [282] and [191, Chap. 8]. Let us consider a state ω on
the CCR-algebra of the Klein-Gordon theory on Minkowski spacetime M := M1,k and consider
the corresponding GNS-construction (Hω, Dω, Ωω, πω). Now, let us write ˆΦω(f) := πω(Φ([f]))
for all f ∈C∞
c (M), which we view as an operator-valued distribution. Now, in general, it is not
possible to define ˆΦ2
ω directly, since the 2-point function ω2(·, ·) := ω(Φ([·])Φ([·])) ∈D′(M × M)
is usually divergent on the diagonal. This is for instance the case for the Minkowski vacuum
state. The solution to this problem is well known and consists of Wick ordering. The idea is to
fist define the Wick-ordered product
: ˆΦω(f)ˆΦω(g): := ˆΦω(f)ˆΦω(g) −⟨Ωω, ˆΦω(f)ˆΦω(g)Ωω⟩Hω1 .
As it turns out, the pull-back of this distribution on the diagonal is well-defined due to Hör-
mander’s theorem of multiplication of distributions and the singularity structure of the 2-point
function of the Minkowski vacuum. Hence, we obtain a well-defined observable : ˆΦ2
ω(f): in this
case, called a Wick polynomial. In other words, the idea of the Wick ordering procedure is to
subtract the vacuum contribution to obtain a well-defined object. We also note that the previous
discussion of Wick polynomials is equivalent in Minkowski spacetime to the popular procedure
in the physics literature based on the re-ordering of creation and annihilation operators (see for
instance [356, Sec. 4.3] for a standard treatment of the latter approach).
Hence, while on general curved (and non-static) spacetimes, there is in general no way to
define a “preferred vacuum state”, the idea is to consider those states whose 2-point function
mimics the ultraviolet structure of the Minkowski vacuum state, in order to be able to de-
fine physical important observables such as the energy-momentum tensor. The corresponding
condition can be formulated conveniently using the language of microlocal analysis.
Definition 2.61. (Hadamard State)
A quasi-free state ω on the CCR-algebra CCR(Vc, σ) of some linear gauge theory (E1, E2, P, K)
coming from a pair of pseudo-covariances λ± : Γ∞
c (E2) →Γ∞(E2) is called Hadamard, if
WF′(λ±) ⊂N ± × N ± ,
where we identify λ± with its Schwartz kernel λ± ∈D′(M × M, E ⊠E∗) and where N ± are
the positive/negative energy cones, i.e.
N ± := {ξ ∈T∗M | g♯(ξ, ξ) = 0 and ± ξ(v) > 0 for all future-directed timelike v ∈TM} .
The wavefront set WF(T) ∈T∗M\{0} of a distribution T ∈D′(M, E) is a refinement of the
singular support of T that includes additional informations on the singular direction in Fourier
space. In particular, the above definition makes precise the idea of states with the same singular
ultraviolet structure of the Minkowski vacuum. For details on distributions, the Schwartz kernel
theorem and microlocal analysis, we refer to Appendix A.1.
Remarks 2.62.
(i) With the general definition of Hadamard states on curved spacetimes, Wick polynomials
can then also be defined as abstract algebraic objects. More precisely, one can define an


## Page 138


118
Chapter 2. Linear Gauge Theories on Manifolds
extended algebra including Wick polynomials, see e.g. the work of Brunetti-Fredenhagen-
Kohler [69], Hollands-Wald [243,244] as well as the review by Khavkine-Moretti [282].
(ii) The concept of Hadamard states emerged out of the the question of how to rigorously
define a regularised quantum energy-momentum tensor. This quantity is, of course, in-
teresting on its own right, as it described the energy and momentum content of a theory,
but it is also of central relevance in semiclassical gravity, in which one studies the back-
reactions of a quantum field ϕ on a classical gravitational background g by means of the
semiclassical Einstein equations EinΛ(g) = ωg(:T(g, ϕ):), where ωg is a state on the cor-
responding algebra of observables, see the introduction to Section 1.3. In particular, it
has been observed in the 1970s by Wald [440,441], Christensen [88] and Adler-Lieberman-
Ng [2,3] that the right-hand side of the above equation can be consistently defined if ωg has
the same short-distance behaviour as the Minkowski vacuum. The notion of Hadamard
states was then further developed and clarified by Fulling-Sweeny-Wald [181], Fulling-
Narcowich-Wald [180] and Kay-Wald [276], among others, in which also the viewpoint has
emerged that only states satisfying the Hadamard condition should be considered as “phys-
ically acceptable”. In those early papers, the Hadamard condition is usually formulated
by specifying the singularities of the 2-point function explicitly in terms of the so-called
Hadamard parametrix, a local approximate solution (a “parametrix”) of the wave equa-
tion written in terms of geometric quantity developed by Hadamard [232] in the 1920s (see
also [116] and [253, Sec. 17.4]). In particular, Kay-Wald described in the aforementioned
paper [276] of 1991 a global description of the Hadamard parametrix and provided the
first definition of Hadamard states (see [333] for a recent technical correction to the origi-
nal definition). It was then later observed in the seminal works of Radzikoswki [364,365]
that the Hadamard condition written in terms of the global Hadamard parametrix can
be related to the theory of distinguished parametrices [126] and microlocal analysis. In
particular, it has been shown that the definition of Kay-Wald can equivalently be charac-
terised by a condition of the wavefront set of the 2-point function (see [191, Sec. 8.4] for
a modern proof). The condition in Definition 2.61 is slightly different to the one given by
Radzikowski and its equivalence can be found in [192, Sec. 8.4] and [453]. All the previous
sources consider scalar fields; for fields taking values in a vector bundle, the Hadamard
condition has been worked out by Sahlmann-Verch [387] (with earlier works for Dirac
fields, e.g. [241,287,436]).
(iii) It has been argued in [150] in the specific case of ultrastatic spacetimes with compact
Cauchy surfaces that the Hadamard condition is in fact necessary for the Wick squares of
all time derivatives a quantised Klein-Gordon field to have finite fluctuations.
▲
For applications it is useful to consider an equivalent condition for the Cauchy pseudo-
covariances of a quasi-free Hadamard states. The prove of the following condition is essentially
an application of the well-known propagation of singularities theorem (see Section A.2.5) and
can be found in [194, Prop. 2.11] (cf. also [192, Prop. 11.1.1])).
Proposition 2.63. Let ω be a quasi-free state on the CCR-algebra CCR(Vc, σ) of a linear
gauge theory (E1, E2, P, K) coming from a pair of Cauchy pseudo-covariances c± : Γ∞
c (E2) →
Γ∞(E2). If D2 is normally hyperbolic, then ω is a Hadamard state, if
∃U ⊂M neighbourhood of Σ : WF′(U2 ◦c±) ⊂(N ± × F) × T∗Σ over U × M ,
where F ⊂T∗M is a conic set with F ∩N ± = ∅.

2.3.3
Existence of Hadamard States and Construction: An Overview
In this final section, we provide a bibliography and literature review on results concerning
the existence and explicit construction of Hadamard states for various field theories. We stress
that this survey is by no means complete, given the vast amount of literature on Hadamard
states, but rather, reflects a selection of research directions that are particularly relevant in
the context of this thesis through some representative and influential works. Hence, the focus
naturally emphasises certain approaches and contributions, while other important developments
and articles may not be covered.
Klein-Gordon Theory:
The general existence of Hadamard states for Klein-Gordon theory
on general globally hyperbolic spacetimes has been established by Fulling-Narcowich-Wald [180]
(see [192, Thm. 8.9.1] or [190, Sec. 4.10.2] for a modern proof). In their proof, Hadamard states
are first explicitly constructed on ultrastatic spacetimes, generalising the definition of the vac-
uum state from Minkowski spacetime. We briefly review the central construction and argument
following the aforementioned references: let (Σ, h) be geodesically complete Riemannian mani-
fold with Levi-Civita connection ∇h and let M = R×Σ with g = −dt⊗dt+h be the corresponding
ultrastatic spacetime. Furthermore, consider the Klein-Gordon operator
P = −□+ m2 = ∂2
t + ε2 = (∂t + iε)(∂t −iε)

self-adjoint on L2(Σ, dµh) with spectrum σ(ε) ⊂[m2, ∞). In particular, using spectral calculus,
it follows that the square root ε of its unique self-adjoint extension is well-defined, self-adjoint
and invertible on its respective domain. Now, with this notation, it is not too hard to see that
