Now, in order to obtain the existence of Hadamard states on general globally hyperbolic
spacetimes, the idea of Fulling-Narcowich-Wald is to employ a deformation argument: consider
two globally hyperbolic metrics g1 and g2 on the same manifold M such that there exists a
Cauchy surface Σ of both (M, g1) and (M, g2) with the property that g1 = g2 on a (causally
compatible) neighbourhood U of Σ in M. Now, by construction, the causal propagators G1
and G2 of Pg1 and Pg2, respectively, agree on U × U and hence, if c±
1 are the Cauchy pseudo-
covariances of a Hadamard state on (M, g1), we see that the corresponding state defined on
(M, g2) via G2 will also be Hadamard on U × U. The propagation of singularity theorem (see
Section A.2.5) then implies that the state on (M, g2) is Hadamard everywhere. In order to apply
this fact, the idea is to choose an interpolating metric between an arbitrary globally hyperbolic
metric g on M and an ultrastatic metric gu on M and applying this argument twice.


## Page 140


120
Chapter 2. Linear Gauge Theories on Manifolds
While the construction of Fulling-Narcowich-Wald works in full generality, it has the disad-
vantage of being very indirect. A general construction of Hadamard states for Klein-Gordon
fields without a deformation argument using techniques from pseudodifferential calculus has
been achieved by Gérard-Wrochna [197] and Gérard-Oulghazi-Wrochna [195] for globally hyper-
bolic manifolds of bounded geometry30, also allowing for general smooth potentials, (a detailed
treatment of the same proof can also be found in the monograph [192, Chap. 11]), following
earlier works of Junker-Schrohe [269–271]. The proof is in fact similar in spirit of the construc-
tion for ultrastatic manifolds reviewed above, i.e., by factorising the Cauchy evolution operator
accordingly. However, due to the time-dependence of the metric h, the splitting becomes more
complicated and requires methods of pseudodifferential calculus and microlocal analysis. We
will review this method in more details lateron in this thesis.
Using these pseudodifferential techniques, one in fact constructs an entire family of states.
For concrete applications, however, the question remains as to which state should be selected. In
many cases, particularly when working with spacetimes of direct physical relevance, this choice is
often guided by suitable invariance properties with respect to the isometries of the background.
Beyond the general existence proofs and constructions, there is hence also a substantial body
of literature devoted to the explicit construction of Hadamard states on specific spacetimes,
often emphasising physical properties of direct importance for applications. An important line
of research is the construction of Hadamard state for conformally coupled scalar fields from
asymptotic data for asymptotically flat spacetimes, initiated by Dappiaggi-Pinamonti-Moretti
[104,330,331] (see also the monograph [108]). In this work, it is shown that for asymptotically
flat spacetimes at future null infinity I + (see [442, Chap. 11]) naturally supports a bosonic
quantum field theory on its boundary. Within this framework, a distinguished quasifree state
can be constructed using the celebrated BMS-group [64, 380], which encodes the asymptotic
symmetries of I +. Moreover, there exists an injective ∗-homomorphism from the algebra of
observables in the bulk to that at I +, allowing one to pull back the boundary state to the
bulk, which has been shown to satisfy the Hadamard condition and to be invariant under all
isometries of the bulk.
The previous results have also been expanded to other situations, namely for cosmological
spacetimes in [105,106] and to the construction of the so-called Unruh state on Schwarzschild
spacetime [107]. As a side comment, it should be mentioned that the latter construction is
more complicated than the construction of states for asymptotically flat spacetimes at future
null infinity, since in the case of Schwarzschild spacetime one needs suitable decay bounds of
solutions to the wave equations at i± in order to construct the aforementioned injective ∗-
algebra homomorphism. In particular, the result in [107] depends on the highly non-trivial
bounds derived by Dafermos-Rodnianski in [99]. Using asymptotic/boundary data, Hadamard
states have also been constructed for various different types of spacetimes and physical situations
using various different techniques and estimates, e.g. [67,199,200,245,284,391,435].
Dirac Theory:
An explicit construction of Hadamard states for Dirac fields on general spa-
tially compact globally hyperbolic manifolds has been performed by Capoferri-Murro in [79]
by writing the Cauchy evolution operator as a sum of to global Fourier integral operators
with complex-valued phase functions, based on the global parametrix techniques developed
in [77,78,81,297], and relating it to the Feynman propagators. An alternative construction using
methods from pseudodifferential calculus, as mentioned above, has been applied to globally hy-
perbolic spacetimes of bounded geometry by Gérard-Stoskopf [196]. The existence of Hadamard
30 Riemannian (and Lorentzian) manifolds of bounded geometry are a class of manifolds that behave “nicely” at
infinity and allow for a well-defined pseudodifferential calculus. We will define this concept in more detail
in the next chapter. For completeness, we just mention that virtually all globally hyperbolic spacetimes of
physical interest are included in that class.


## Page 141


2.3. Quantisation of Linear Gauge Theories
121
states for Dirac fields can also be proven by means of a deformation argument, as shown by
Murro-Volpe [341], using intertwining operators (with an extension to globally hyperbolic man-
ifolds with timelike boundaries in [124]).
Last but not least, an alternative construction of
Hadamard states from Feynman propagators has been discussed by Islam-Strohmaier [257] and
Fewster-Strohmaier [149], generalising the classic approach based on distinguished parametrices
by Duistermaat-Hörmander [126] to the case of normally hyperbolic operators on Hermitian
vector bundles with positive definite bundle metrics.
Earlier works by Sahlmann-Verch [386] have shown that the ground and thermal states
for Dirac fields on stationary spacetimes are Hadamard. Furthermore, employing asymptotic
techniques, Dappiaggi-Hack-Pinamonti [103] have constructed Hadamard states for Dirac fields
on a large class of FLRW-spacetimes. Based on the scattering calculus developed by Häfner-
Nicolas [234], the Unruh state for massless Dirac fields on (very slowly rotating) Kerr spacetime
has been constructed and its Hadamard property proven by Gérard-Häfner-Wrochna [193] (see
also Häfner-Klein [233] for a recent extension of this result). A different line of research is based
on the functional analytic construction of the fermionic projectors developed in [160,161], which
has been applied to construct Hadamard states for (massive) Dirac fields in Minkowski [156],
Rindler [157] and de Sitter spacetime [102].
Proca Theory:
The existence of Hadamard states for Proca fields has been established for
general globally hyperbolic spacetimes by Moretti-Murro-Volpe in [335]. Proca field theory is
somehow a special situation, because despite being not a gauge theory, it shares some difficulties
with them. The Proca equation is given by
P = δd + m2 : Ω1(M) →Ω1(M)
for some mass m > 0. It is not normally hyperbolic, but rather pre-normally hyperbolic, see
Example 1.43. The bundle metric of the theory is given by the de Rham-Hodge sequilinear
form on Ω1(M) and hence in particular not positive-definite. Furthermore, it is easy to show
that the Proca equation PA = 0 is equivalent to the hyperbolic equation (□dRH + m2)A = 0
with the additional constraint δA = 0.
Now, it has been argued that a general vector Klein-Gordon operator with a non-positive
fibre metric does not admit a Hadamard state under some mild analytic assumptions, see [198,
Sec. 6.3] and [149] as well as the comment after Prop. 5.6 in [387] for an earlier mentioning
of this fact. Nevertheless, does not affect the Proca case. Indeed, it has been shown that the
state is positive on divergence-free solutions to the Klein-Gordon equation. The construction
of states in [335] is explicitly performed on ultrastatic manifolds of bounded geometry and the
existence on general globally hyperbolic spacetimes by means of deformation argument based
on suitable Møller operators as defined in [334].
Maxwell and Linearised Yang-Mills Theory:
The existence of Hadamard states for
Maxwell’s theory was investigated under various assumptions on the Cauchy hypersurface Σ of
the spacetime: Fewster-Pfenning [147] (generalising results of Furlani [182] in the case of static
spacetimes) assumed Σ to be compact and with vanishing first cohomology group, Finster-
Strohmaier [162], extending the so-called Gupta–Bleuler formalism [60,224], considered Cauchy
surfaces Σ subjected to an “absence of zero resonance” condition for the Hodge Laplacian on
1-forms, while Dappiaggi-Siemssen [109] worked out the construction on asymptotically flat
spacetimes with similar methods mentioned above. The construction of Hadamard states for
Maxwell’s theory on general globally hyperbolic manifolds has been established by Murro-
Schmid in [340] and this paper will be the content of Chapter 3 of this thesis.
The construction of Hadamard states for linearised Yang-Mills theory was first studied by
Hollands [242] (within the BRST formalism), which considered the theory linearised around


## Page 142


122
Chapter 2. Linear Gauge Theories on Manifolds
the zero solution on globally hyperbolic spacetimes with compact Cauchy surface and vanishing
first cohomology group. More recently, Gérard-Wrochna [198] constructed Hadamard states
with techniques from pseudodifferential calculus for Yang-Mills theory linearised around non-
zero solutions on globally hyperbolic spacetimes of bounded geometry in the case in which Σ is
compact and parallelisable and the case Σ = Rn with a Riemannian metric h satisfying
c−1 ≤[hij(x)] ≤c
for c > 0
and

∀α ∈Nn

Linearised Gravity:
For linearised gravity, the existence of Hadamard states has been es-
tablished for Cauchy compact globally hyperbolic spacetimes of bounded geometry by Gérard
in [192] via an approach based on complete gauge fixing. Furthermore, Hadamard stated for
linearised gravity for asymptotically flat spacetimes, using asymptotic data as in the aforemen-
tioned works by Dappiaggi-Pinamonti-Moretti and Dappiaggi-Siemssen, have been constructed
by Benini-Dappiaggi-Murro [51], however, the quantisation turns out to be limited to a subspace
of observables due to divergences at null infinity. An open question in this direction is whether
there exists a gauge condition that propagates to I+.
An earlier result about existence of Hadamard states for linearised gravity has appeared in
the PhD thesis of Hunt [256], in which it has been shown that the Fock vacuum state of linearised
gravity in Minkowski spacetime is Hadamard. Furthermore, in the preprint of [70, App. D of v3
on arXiv], it has been shown that there exists a Hadamard state for linearised gravity for every
ultrastatic and Cauchy compact globally hyperbolic manifold, employing the BRST formalisms
and using functional analytic methods. The existence of Hadamard states for gravity linearised
around a general globally hyperbolic spacetime with non-compact Cauchy surfaces remains an
important open question. A possible approach is discussed in the outlook section of this thesis.
An approach for globally hyperbolic manifold of bounded geometry that are analytic in
Gaußian time based on Wick rotation has been discussed by Gérard-Murro-Wrochna in [194]. In
this approach, the relevant normally hyperbolic operators are transformed into elliptic operators
on the Wick rotated manifold, which, however, are only defined in some strip in imaginary
Gaussian time.
Then, one can define a quasi-free state for the Lorentzian theory from the
Calderón projectors of the elliptic theory, a well-known tool in elliptic boundary value problems.
In the context of Hadamard states, the use of Calderón projectors to construct Hadamard states
has been first discussed for Klein-Gordon fields by Gérard-Wrochna in [201]. However, for linear
gauge theories, this approach inherits a number of difficulties: the Wick rotated elliptic operators
should be invertible and for this, one needs to impose proper boundary conditions. In [194],
Dirichlet conditions for all the metric components are used, which has the advantage of easily
giving invertibility of the Wick rotated operators and a modified positivity property. However,
they are not gauge invariant. Hence, the states constructed in [194] are only gauge-invariant
(and also positive) up to a smoothing contribution. Motivated by this problem, the spectral
properties of more general gauge-invariant boundary conditions satisfying the Shapiro-Lopatinski
conditions (which guarantee a well-posed elliptic boundary value problem in suitable Sobolev
spaces [312, 412], see e.g. [253, Chap. XX] and [293] for a modern discussion) for linearised
Euclidean gravity have been discussed by Capoferri-Murro-Schmid in [80], generalising boundary
conditions considered by Anderson [18–20] (cf. also the review in [452]) in the linearised setting,
however, none of them gives invertibility of the relevant elliptic operators on all spacetimes.
Nevertheless, building up on [194], a Hadamard states for linearised gravity on de Sitter space
have been constructed by Gérard-Wrochna in [202], strongly relying on the fact that the Wick
rotation of de Sitter spacetime is, in fact, a compact Riemannian manifold.
In the specific case of Kerr spacetime, another line of research it to circumvent the difficulties
arising from the gauge freedom of linearised gravity by expressing the theory equivalently in
terms of the Teukolsky scalar fields [428, 429] and to construct Hadamard states in the scalar
setting, see e.g. the recent articles [260,285].

Looking at the overview of existence theorems and explicit constructions of Hadamard states
for various different linear field theories provided in Section 2.3.3, it becomes evident that much
less is known for theories admitting a (non-trivial) gauge symmetry. The reason for this is that
there are several additional technical difficulties present in this case. First of all, if (E1, E2, P, K)
is a linear gauge theory in the sense of Definition 2.1, then, following our discussion of the
Cauchy problem for linear gauge theories in Section 2.1.2, we know that the solution space of
the theory can be characterised as

where D2 := P + KK∗is the hyperbolic gauge-fixed operator with respect to the canonical (or
subsidiary) condition K∗ψ = 0 and where D1 := K∗K is a hyperbolic operator parametrising
the residual gauge freedom present. Now, naively, one idea would be to construct a state for
the hyperbolic theory defined by D2, subsequently restrict it to the relevant subspace, and pull
it back to the phase space of gauge-invariant observables. However, as one can see from our
discussion of Section 2.2, essentially all the examples of linear gauge theories of physical interest
admit a bundle metric that is not positive-definite, but merely non-degenerate. For example, in
the case of Maxwell’s theory of electromagnetism, the bundle metric is given by the Lorentzian
metric g♯on T∗M. But, as already mentioned previously, it is known that normally hyperbolic
operators with a non-positive fibre metric do not admit a Hadamard state, but only pseudo-
states, see e.g. the discussion in [198, Sec. 6.3]. Hence, the positivity condition of the state can
only hoped to be achievable on the level of the quotient space. In other words, the non-positivity
of the bundle metric is in tension with the positivity of the state to be constructed.
On the other hand, linear gauge theories are usually plagued by infrared divergencies: follow-
ing similar steps as explained in Section 2.3.3, states on ultrastatic spacetimes are usually con-
structed by considering projection operators onto the subspace of positive/negative-frequency
solutions.
In the case of Klein–Gordon theory, the definition of the corresponding Cauchy
covariances c± requires defining the square root of the “spatial part” of the Klein-Gordon op-
erator, i.e. the operator ε := (−∆+ m2)1/2, which is well defined provided m > 0 by means of
spectral calculus. For massless theories, including gauge theories, however, this issue becomes
more delicate. In the case of massless Klein–Gordon theory, the problem can be overcome by
using tools from pseudodifferential calculus (see e.g. [191, Chap. 11], [194, Sec. 5]): the idea
is essentially to find a suitable smoothing operator r−∞such that ε2 := −∆+ r−∞admits a
123


## Page 144


124
Chapter 3. Quantisation of Maxwell’s Theory via Complete Gauge Fixing
well-defined and invertible square root. Since r−∞is only a smoothing contribution, it does not
affect the Hadamard property and hence allows one to construct a state in a similar manner.
For gauge theories, however, one must additionally respect gauge invariance, encoded in the
condition c±KΣ = KΣec± for some suitable ec±, see Proposition 2.59. This requirement is, of
course, spoiled by the introduction of r−∞. In other words, the pseudodifferential techniques
that successfully resolve infrared issues in the scalar case turn out to be inherent conflict with
the gauge symmetry.
This tension creates a significant obstruction for the construction of
Hadamard states and calls for some more refined techniques and approaches to be developed.
Several approaches to this problem have been proposed in the literature, as discussed in the
bibliography in Section 2.3.3. The goal of this chapter, based on the article [340], is to prove the
existence of Hadamard states for Maxwell’s theory on general globally hyperbolic spacetimes.
Our strategy relies on a complete gauge fixing procedure.
Specifically, we impose a gauge
condition at the level of the initial data, which eliminates all the unphysical degrees of freedom.
Moreover, the condition is chosen in such a way that it removes the negative contributions in the
bundle metric at the initial-data stage, thereby enabling the construction of positive Cauchy
covariances for the fully gauge-fixed theory. States for Maxwell’s theory are then defined as
the pull-back along the gauge-fixing projector, and we show that this construction preserves
the Hadamard property.
A central ingredient in establishing this new gauge condition is a
novel Hodge decomposition for differential forms in Sobolev spaces on complete Riemannian
manifolds and a dedicated section is devoted to the development of this decomposition and the
needed functional analytic methods. The construction of states will then explicitly be performed
on ultrastatic spacetimes, while the existence on general globally hyperbolic spacetimes follows
from a deformation argument. We emphasise that, although our construction of states relies
on a complete gauge-fixing procedure, the underlying algebra of observables remains that of
gauge-invariant observables and the role of gauge fixing is purely auxiliary.
As it turns out, the construction of states via complete gauge fixing at the level of initial data
is both natural and admits several technical advantages. Besides Maxwell’s theory, a strategy
based on complete gauge fixing has recently also independently been worked out for linearised
gravity on spatially compact globally hyperbolic spacetimes by Gérard in [192].
3.1
Maxwell’s Theory on Globally Hyperbolic Spacetimes
We have already discussed Maxwell’s theory briefly in Section 2.2.1, where we also saw that
it naturally fits into the framework of linear gauge theories of Hack–Schenkel (Definition 2.1).
In this section, we provide a more detailed analysis of Maxwell’s theory to prepare the ground
for the subsequent chapters. First of all, we discuss differential forms on globally hyperbolic
spacetimes and their decomposition into temporal and spatial parts adapted to the geometry
of a chosen foliation by spacelike Cauchy hypersurfaces. We then provide explicit expressions
for the all the operators acting on the space of initial data introduced in Section 2.1.2 and
Section 2.1.3, such as KΣ, K†
Σ and GΣ,i. Last but not least, we examine various different gauge
conditions for Maxwell’s theory. In particular, we will define the Cauchy radiation gauge, which
will play a central role in the subsequent construction of Hadamard states.
The purpose of this section is twofold. On the one hand, it prepares the ground for the
construction of Hadamard states of Maxwell’s theory. On the other hand, it provides a detailed
and self-contained treatment of both the Cauchy problem for Maxwell’s equations on general
globally hyperbolic spacetimes and the corresponding gauge problem. Although the construction
of Hadamard states will be carried out specifically in ultrastatic spacetimes, with the existence
of such states on general spacetimes following from a deformation argument, we hence keep the
discussion here at a general level. This broader perspective is included both for its intrinsic
interest and to illustrate how the ultrastatic case simplifies within the general framework.

Last but not least, for a given time-dependent k-form ω ∈C∞(R, Ωk(Σ, C)), it makes
sense to define its (partial) time-derivative. There are several, equivalent, ways to make this
precise: first of all, we can define ∂tω ∈C∞(R, Ωk(Σ, C)) to be the time-dependent k-form,
which in local coordinates (xi)i=1,...,n on U ⊂Σ takes the form (∂tω)i1...ik = ∂tωi1...ik, where
ωi1...ik ∈C∞(R × U, C) are the local components of ω. It is clear that this independent of
the chosen coordinates on Σ and hence well-defined, however, we stress that it clearly depends
on the choice of Cauchy temporal function t ∈C∞(M, R) (and hence on the foliation chosen),
which here is assumed to be fixed from the beginning. From a differential geometric point of
view, a more natural (and equivalent) definition is provided by setting
∂tω := L∂tω ∈C∞(R, Ωk(Σ, C)) ,
where L∂t : Ωk(M, C) →Ωk(M, C) denotes the Lie-derivative along ∂t. The fact that this is
indeed equivalent follows easily from the definition of L∂t.
Remark 3.1. As explained above, we use the notation C∞(R, Ωk(Σ, C)) for smooth sections of
the bundle π∗
2(Vk T∗Σ) and we interpret elements of this module as time-dependent k-forms on
Σ, i.e. t 7→ω(t, ·) ∈Ωk(Σ, C), where smoothness has to be understood in the sense that ω is a
smooth section of π∗
2(Vk T∗Σ) on M. This is, of course, the natural differential-geometric defini-
tion. From an analytic point of view, we note that this is equivalent to define C∞(R, Ωk(Σ, C))
as the space of assignments t 7→ω(t, ·) ∈Ωk(Σ, C) that are smooth with respect to the Fréchet
space structure on Ωk(Σ, C) = Γ∞(CΣ ⊗Vk T∗Σ). In the special case k = 0, this is known as the
exponential law and states that C∞(R × Σ) = C∞(R, C∞(Σ)). More generally, the exponential
law states that there is a (topological) isomorphism C∞(M × N, F) ∼= C∞(M, C∞(N, F)) (in
fact, even a diffeomorphism in a suitable sense), where M, N are smooth manifolds and F a
Fréchet space. We refer to [291, Sec. I.3, Thm. 3.12 and Cor. 3.13] for details.

3.1.2
Maxwell’s Theory Revisited and (1+n)-Decomposition
Following Section 2.2.1, Maxwell’s theory, viewed as a linear gauge theory in the sense of
Definition 2.1, is defined as the quadruple (A0, A1, P, K), where the two operators P ∈DO2(A1)
and K ∈DO1(A0, A1) are given by
P := δd: Ω1(M, C) →Ω1(M, C) ,
K := d: C∞(M, C) →Ω1(M, C) .
By the previous discussion, we note that P is not hyperbolic and can also be be written as
P = δd = □−dδ = −gαβ∇α∇β −dδ + Ricg .
31 More precisely, this is the scalar 2nd fundamental form: if (M, g) is a pseudo-Riemannian manifold with
pseudo-Riemannian embedded submanifold (i: Σ ,→M, h := i∗g), the 2nd fundamental form is the NΣ-valued
symmetric (0, 2)-tensor field II(X, Y ) := (∇M
X′Y ′)⊥for X, Y ∈X(Σ) and extensions X′, Y ′ to M, where NΣ is
the normal bundle and ·⊥: TM|Σ →NΣ the projection. If Σ is a hypersurface (i.e. codimension 1), one defines
the scalar 2nd fundamental form (up to a sign) by k(X, Y ) := g(ν, II(X, Y )), where ν is a unit normal vector.

Clearly, it holds that P◦K = 0, which encodes the gauge-invariance of the theory. The canonical
gauge condition in this case is given by K∗A = δA = 0 for A ∈Ω1(M, C), where we denote by
“∗” the formal adjoints with respect to the Hodge sesquilinear form (3.1), and usually called
the Lorenz gauge. Furthermore, the two operators D1 := K∗K and D2 := P + KK∗are given by
Di = □: Ωi−1(M, C) →Ωi−1(M, C) .
For later convenience, we shall now derive a (1+n)-decomposition of the Maxwell equations,
i.e. a decomposition into the temporal and spatial parts AT and AΣ for a given Maxwell field
A ∈Ω1(M, C). This can be seen as an analogue to the way one derives the ADM decomposition of
general relativity (see the review [25] by the original authors and Fischer-Marsden [163,166,167]
for a mathematically oriented discussion). We start with the following technical lemma.

Note that the equations in Proposition 3.3 simplify a lot in the case β = 1. As it turns out,

we prove it here for completeness and the reader’s convenience.

■
Since conformal transformations preserve the causal structure of a Lorentzian manifold, we
note that global hyperbolicity is preserved as well. Hence, when working on a (1+3)-dimensional
globally hyperbolic spacetime, we can perform the conformal transformation g 7→β−2g =: g′,
which yields a new globally hyperbolic metric g′ with lapse function β′ = 1. By Proposition 3.5,
it follows that the solution spaces of the Maxwell operator on (M, g) and (M, g′) are equivalent.
3.1.3
Cauchy Problem for Maxwell’s Equations and Phase Space
As a next step, we discuss the Cauchy problem for Maxwell’s equations, following the ab-
stract discussion in Section 2.1.2. Moreover, we examine the various formulations of the phase
space of Maxwell’s theory, following Section 2.1.3. To start with, we need to set up the notation
for the spaces of initial data. Recall the decomposition
Γ∞(Ak) = Ωk(M, C) ∼= C∞(R, Ωk−1(Σ, C)) ⊕C∞(R, Ωk(Σ, C))
(3.5)
defined by ω = η∧ωT+ωΣ for ωT := ν⌟ω with ν = β−1∂t and η = βdt, cf. Eq. (3.2). Throughout
the following, we assign initial data on the slice Σ0 = {0}×Σ. Following the notation introduced

As shown in Proposition 2.14, σ gives rise to a well-defined Hermitian sesquilinear form on Vc,
i.e. σ: Vc × Vc →C, and as already noted in Remark 2.16, we stress that this form is in general
degenerate. The pair (Vc, σ) is the classical phase space of Maxwell’s theory and lies in the heart
of the algebraic quantisation procedure outlined in Section 2.3.
As discussed in Section 2.1.3 for general linear gauge theories, the Hermitian form σ induces
a corresponding Hermitian structure on VΣ, thereby defining the phase space at the level of

3.1.4
Gauge Conditions for Maxwell’s Theory and Complete Gauge Fixing
In the previous section, we provided a detailed description of the Cauchy problem of Maxwell’s
theory and introduced all the relevant operators. As the next step in our analysis of Maxwell’s
theory on globally hyperbolic spacetimes, we now turn to a more detailed discussion of the
gauge problem. This section is based on [340, Sec. 2].
To start with, for a given Maxwell field A = ATη + AΣ ∈Ω1(M, C) with η = βdt and
AT := β−1A(∂t), as usual, we consider the following gauge conditions.
(T)
0 = At
temporal (or Weyl) gauge
(L)
0 = δA
Lorenz gauge
(C)
0 = δΣAΣ
Coulomb (or transverse) gauge
(R) = (L) + (T)
0 = δA = AT
radiation gauge
We stress that different authors might use different names or different definitions for those kind
of conditions. Furthermore, we stress that the gauge conditions (T), (C) and (R) depend on the
choice of Cauchy temporal function t ∈C∞(M), which here is fixed from the start. Only the
Lorenz gauge condition (L) is fully covariant.
A comment is needed regarding the terminology. Consider (1 + n)-dimensional Minkowski
spacetime M1,n := (R1+n, η). Then, for a given 4-potential A = Aµdxµ ∈Ω1(R1+n), we note
that the Lorenz gauge condition decomposes as 0 = ∂µAµ = −∂tA0 + ∂iAi. Hence, we conclude
that (T)+(L) = (T)+(C). This condition is usually called the radiation gauge, owing to the fact
that it isolates the propagating, transverse degrees of freedom. Indeed, the Maxwell equations in
this case reduce to the wave equation ∂2
t Ai −∂k∂kAi = 0 for the transverse part AΣ = Aidxi. In
fact, at least on-shell, it turns out that the radiation gauge in Minkowski space is also equivalent
to the Coulomb gauge when choosing the function spaces properly: if A satisfies the Coulomb
gauge condition, i.e. ∂iAi = 0, then the Maxwell equations imply ∆AT = 0 and hence, when
considering fields that are compactly supported in space, or at least vanishing at spatial infinity,
the only solution to this elliptic equation is AT = 0. For these reasons, the terms Coulomb and
radiation gauge are often used interchangeably in the physics literature, as are the conditions
(T) + (L) and (T) + (C) (see, for instance, [261, Chap. 6]). In fact, a similar statement is true,
more generally, for ultrastatic globally hyperbolic manifolds:
Proposition 3.10. Let (M = R × Σ, g = −dt ⊗dt + h) be an ultrastatic globally hyperbolic
spacetime and let A ∈Ω1(M, C). Then:
(i) A satisfies (R) = (L) + (T) if and only if it satisfies (C) + (T).
(ii) If Σ is non-compact, then, for A ∈ker(P|Ω1sc), conditions (R) and (C) are equivalent.
In this case, the Maxwell equations reduce to the hyperbolic equation (∂2
t +∆)AΣ = 0.

■
Remark 3.11. Proposition 3.10(ii) can also be generalised: consider the case in which the
volume of (Σ, h) is infinite and A ∈ker(P) is such that AΣ|t=s ∈L2
1(Σ) for all s ∈R, where
L2

c(Σ, C) with respect
to the de Rham-Hodge inner product on (Σ, h).
Then, since (M, g) is ultrastatic, (Σ, h) is
complete (cf. Example 1.10(i)), which implies that any harmonic k-form is closed and coclosed.
In particular, the Maxwell equation ∆AT = 0 implies that AT|t=s = Cs for some constant
Cs ∈R depending on time s ∈R. But if volh(Σ) = ∞, this constant must necessarily be zero,
which shows again that AT = 0.
▲
Now, the situation is different when considering more general, non-ultrastatic, globally hy-
perbolic spacetimes: a natural generalisation of the Coulomb gauge condition in Minkowski
spacetime to arbitrary globally hyperbolic spacetimes is the transversality condition δΣAΣ = 0,
as defined above. However, in this case, it is no longer true that (T) + (L) = (T) + (C), at least
in general. Indeed, assuming AT = 0, i.e. condition (T), Lemma 3.2(ii) implies
δA = δΣAΣ −β−1h♯(dΣβ, AΣ) .
Hence, (T) + (L) = (T) + (C) only holds true if the Cauchy temporal function is chosen such
that β = const. At least in (1 + 3)-dimensional spacetimes we can always reduce ourselves to
this situation, since we can always perform a conformal transformation g 7→β−2g to normalise
g in time to g′ = −dt⊗dt+h′
t, by conformal invariance of Maxwell’s theory, cf. Proposition 3.5.
For more general spacetimes, however, this is not the case.
Furthermore, since the Maxwell’s equations in general curved spacetimes are the strongly
coupled system in Proposition 3.3, the two conditions (T) + (L) and (T) + (C) both lead to
different equations on AΣ, both of which would deserve to be called radiation gauge.
Here
we decide to use the former condition, as it contains the coordinate-independent and hence
geometrically more natural Lorenz gauge condition. In general, not much has been written about
different gauge conditions for Maxwell’s theory on general globally hyperbolic spacetimes, but
we mention that our concept of radiation gauge is somewhat close in spirit to the one considered
by Tolksdorf in [430], in which he calls radiation gauge the combination of the Lorenz gauge and
the condition ξ⌟A = 0 for some timelike Killing vector field ξ. Last but not least, we remind the
reader that both our temporal and Coulomb gauge depend on the choice of Cauchy temporal
function t: M →R. In principle, also in Minkowski spacetime one could choose a different,
non-trivial, foliation in which the same subtleties would appear.
Remark 3.12. There is a way of defining a modified Coulomb gauge condition such that
the equivalence (L) + (T) = (L) + (C) in Proposition 3.10(i) holds true on general globally
hyperbolic spacetimes and with arbitrary Cauchy temporal functions. Let p ∈R. Then, under
the conformal transformation h 7→h′ := βph, denoting the codifferential on (Σ, h′) by δ′
Σ, a
computation similar to the one in Proposition 3.5 yields

With this definition, Lemma 3.2(ii) implies (L)+(T) = (L)+(Cβ) on general globally hyperbolic
spacetimes. In the ultrastatic case, one clearly recovers (Cβ) = (C).
Now, in the case of 1+3 spacetime dimensions, we find p0 = −2. In this case, β−2 corresponds
precisely to the conformal factor normalising the globally hyperbolic metric g = −β2dt⊗dt+ht in
time to g′ = −dt⊗dt+(hβ)t, matching the discussion from above. On more general spacetimes,
however, there seems to be no clear physical motivation for considering this β-weighted condition
other than that it generalises the Coulomb gauge condition from Minkowski spacetime.
▲
Choosing the radiation gauge (R) has several noteworthy implications, which, particularly
in the context of constructing Hadamard states, make it an especially attractive choice: besides
the Lorenz gauge condition δA = 0, which allows to turn Maxwell’s equations PA = δdA = 0
into the hyperbolic problem D2A = □A = 0 and is central for all the analysis of Maxwell’s
theory considered so far, it includes in addition the condition AT = 0, which exactly sets the
negative contribution in the bundle metric ⟨A, B⟩A1 = g♯(A, B) = −ATBT + h♯(AΣ, BΣ) to zero.
As mentioned in the introduction of this chapter, the non positivity of the bundle metric is one
of the main complications for constructing Hadamard states for linear gauge theories and hence,
the radiation gauge provides a promising gauge fixing condition in this context. Unfortunately,
on general globally hyperbolic spacetimes, it is not clear whether the radiation gauge condition
can be achieved, i.e. whether it can consistently be applied.
At this stage, let us clarify the terminology: when looking at the solution space of Maxwell’s
theory, or more generally, of an arbitrary linear gauge theory, i.e. the space
Solsc = ker(P|Γ∞
sc )
ran(K|Γ∞
sc ) ,
a gauge condition (G) is achievable, if in any equivalence class [A] ∈Solsc, there is at least one
representative satisfying condition (G). Equivalently, for every Maxwell field A ∈Ω1
sc(M, C), we
can find a f ∈C∞
sc (M, C) such that A′ := A −df satisfies condition (G), which in turn typically
amounts to solving a PDE. In other words, the achievability of a certain gauge condition is
usually equivalent to the existence of solutions to some differential equations. In particular,
we stress that this concept crucially depends on the function spaces we are considering. For
instance, instead of working with forms having spatially compact support, one could work with
a bigger class, such as forms that are L2 or Hs for some s ∈R in space, in which a gauge
condition that is not achievable for spatially compact fields, becomes achievable.
In the case of the radiation gauge condition, achievability is equivalent to the question
whether the system
(

admits a solution f ∈C∞

sc(M, C). Besides some specific and
highly symmetric spacetimes (see, for instance, the discussion in [430]), this system is highly
non-trivial to solve and it is a priori not clear if solutions can be found.

Motivated from this problem, we hence look for a gauge condition that serves similar pur-
poses but turns out to be easier to achieve. More precisely, since we may equivalently work
on the level of initial data using the equivalent characterisation of the phase space in (3.11),
the idea is to define an analogues gauge condition on the level of initial data by combining
the Lorenz gauge with an additional condition that sets the negative contributions to the fibre
metric on initial data to zero. To this end, we make the following definition.
Definition 3.13. (Cauchy Radiation Gauge)
Let (M, g) be a globally hyperbolic spacetime and fix a Cauchy temporal function t ∈
C∞(M).
Then, for A ∈Ω1(M, C) decomposed as A = ATη + AΣ with η = βdt and
AT := β−1A(∂t), as usual, we define the Cauchy radiation gauge by
(RCauchy)
δA = 0
and
AT|t=0 = ∂tAT|t=0 = 0 .
We stress that the Cauchy radiation gauge (RCauchy) depends both on the choice of Cauchy
temporal gauge, as the temporal gauge (T), Coulomb gauge (C) and radiation gauge (R), but,
at least in general, also on the choice of initial slice, which here is simply chosen to be Σt=0.
Remark 3.14. The definition of the Cauchy radiation gauge is slightly different from the one
considered in the original article [340, Def. 2.1], in which the condition ∂tAT|t=0 = 0 is replaced
by (∇νA)T|t=0 = 0. In fact, by Eq. (3.8), it holds that
(∇νA)T|t=0 = 1

conditions are not equivalent.
However, for all practical purposes, this will not make any
difference, besides some minor modification in the proof of Proposition 3.17 below.
▲
To set this new gauge condition (RCauchy) into context, we first note that, at least on-shell,
it is equivalent to the spacetime radiation gauge condition (R) on ultrastatic spacetimes.
Proposition 3.15. Let (M = R × Σ, g = −dt⊗dt+ h) be an ultrastatic globally hyperbolic
spacetime and let A ∈Ω1(M, C). If A ∈ker(P), then A satisfies the radiation gauge (R) if
and only if it satisfies (RCauchy).
Proof. The direction “(R) ⇒(RCauchy)” is obvious. For the other direction, we first note that, in
the ultrastatic case, the hyperbolic equation □A decouples into two separate wave equations for
the temporal and spatial component, i.e. (□A)T = (∂t+∆)AT = □(AT) and (□A)Σ = (∂t+∆)AΣ,
where ∆denotes the de Rham-Hodge Laplacian acting on 0- and 1-forms, respectively. In other
words, the following systems of equations are equivalent:

## Page 164

Chapter 3. Quantisation of Maxwell’s Theory via Complete Gauge Fixing
Now, before addressing the achievability of the Cauchy radiation gauge (RCauchy), let us
introduce another important concept, one that is closely related to achievability of a gauge
condition: we call an (achievable) gauge condition (G) complete, if any equivalence class [A] ∈
Solsc has exactly one representative satisfying (G). This is usually equivalent to the question
whether the solution of a given differential equations is unique and again depends crucially one
the space of fields one considers. In the context of Maxwell’s theory, we consider the following
illustrating example:
Example 3.16. (The Coulomb Gauge on Spatially Compact Ultrastatic Spacetimes)
Let (M = R × Σ, g = −dt ⊗dt + h) be a globally hyperbolic ultrastatic spacetime such that Σ
is compact, i.e. Γ∞
sc (Ak) = Γ∞(Ak). Now, let A ∈ker(P|Γ∞) and consider the Coulomb gauge
condition δΣAΣ. The goal is to find a gauge transformation f ∈C∞(M, C) such that A′ := A−df
satisfies the Coulomb gauge, i.e. δΣA′
Σ = 0. A short computation shows that this is equivalent
to solve the Poisson equation
∆f = δΣAΣ ,
where ∆= δΣdΣ denotes the de Rham-Hodge Laplacian on (Σ, h), as usual. Now, for compact
manifolds, this equation can always be solved and the solution is unique up to a constant. Hence,
we conclude that the Coulomb gauge is achievable. Furthermore, it is in fact a complete gauge
fixing: if A ∈ker(P|Γ∞) satisfies δΣAΣ = 0, then any other gauge transformation A 7→A + df
leaving this condition untouched results into the Laplace equation ∆f = 0. The only solutions to
this equation are the constant functions and hence df = 0. In other words, every equivalent class
of the solution space Solsc has a unique representative satisfying the Coulomb gauge condition.
Now, this discussion is specific to the case of spatially compact spacetimes. If one considers
the more general case in which Σ is non-compact, the previous statements are no longer true:
it is not clear in general that one can solve the Poisson equation ∆f = δΣAΣ within the space
of compactly supported functions for a compactly supported source. Instead, it becomes more
natural to consider a different space of fields. For instance, in Minkowski spacetime, one usually
works with fields A and f that vanish at (spatial) infinity (see e.g. [261]).
▲
As it turns out, the Cauchy radiation gauge can always be achieved on spatially compact
spacetimes, since its achievability reduces to a Poisson-type equation for the initial data, anal-
ogous to the one discussed in the example above.

In summary, the Cauchy radiation gauge is related to gauge-invariant initial data.33
▲
The space VR can be equipped with a Hermitian sesquilinear form, defined in such way that
the isomorphism [TΣ] in (3.14) becomes unitary. The idea for constructing Hadamard states
is then to construct the states on the phase space VR, on which the bundle metric is positive
definite, and then to use the projector TΣ to obtain a state on the original phase space VΣ. One
of the key parts of this construction is, of course, to make sure that all the relevant properties
such as positivity and the Hadamard property are preserved.
Before addressing the explicit construction, we need to extend Proposition 3.17 also to
spacetimes for which Σ is non-compact. This requires solving the Poisson equation ∆f|t=0 =
δΣAΣ|t=0 also on non-compact manifolds. Clearly, working with spatially compactly supported
fields is not appropriate in the setting of elliptic equations. Therefore, we enlarge our functional
framework by employing suitable Sobolev spaces. In particular, the goal is to derive an analogue
of the Hodge decomposition theorem for Sobolev spaces of differential forms, which will enable
us to achieve the Cauchy radiation gauge in appropriate function spaces.
3.2
Hodge Decomposition in Sobolev Spaces
Motivated by the previous discussion, our goal is to derive a Hodge decomposition for
differential forms in suitable Sobolev spaces. While this is explicitly needed for our approach
of construction Hadamard states, such a decomposition is of course also interesting also on its
own right. We will start by defining suitable Sobolev spaces and the relevant functional analytic
preliminaries. Afterwards, we establish the Hodge decomposition on those spaces and discuss
the solvability of the Poisson equation in this setting. This section is based on [340, Sec. 3].
3.2.1
Sobolev Spaces of Differential Forms
There are several ways to define (bundle-valued) Sobolev spaces on manifolds, each moti-
vated by one of the equivalent definitions of Sobolev spaces in Rd, which, however, turn out
to be not equivalent in the general setting of non-compact Riemannian manifolds. Here, we
follow an approach based on spectral calculus for the de Rham-Hodge Laplacian, which is per-
fectly adapted for later applications and the derivation of a Hodge decomposition. To start
with, let (Σ, h) be an oriented, connected and complete n-dimensional Riemannian manifold.
Furthermore, we denote the L2-space of k-forms by
L2
k(Σ) := Ωkc(Σ, C)

tence of a globally hyperbolic ambient manifold. Ultimately, however, (Σ, h) should be regarded
as a Cauchy hypersurface of a globally hyperbolic spacetime, in which case the inner product
⟨·, ·⟩L2 corresponds to (·, ·)AΣ,k in the notation introduced in the previous section.
As usual, let dΣ : Ωk(Σ, C) →Ωk+1(Σ, C) and δΣ : Ωk+1(Σ, C) →Ωk(Σ, C) be the exterior
derivative and codifferential and let us view the de Rham-Hodge Laplacian ∆:= dΣδΣ + δΣdΣ

33 The derivation of the gauge invariant degrees of freedom is similar to the definition of the Bardeen potentials [38]
in cosmological perturbation theory. In the latter, one considers linearised gravity in cosmological spacetimes
and decomposes the linearised gravitational field h ∈Γ∞(T∗M⊗s2) into scalar, vectorial and tensorial degrees
of freedom. The transverse-traceless tensorial parts are already gauge-invariant, while the scalar and vectorial
degrees of freedom are replaced by linear combinations thereof that are gauge invariant, see e.g. [229,339].

Theorem 3.20. The de Rham-Hodge Laplacian ∆= dΣδΣ +δΣdΣ : dom(∆) →L2
k(Σ) with
dom(∆) := Ωk
c(Σ, C) is essentially self-adjoint on a complete Riemannian manifold (Σ, h).
The previous theorem is classical and well-documented in the literature. One of the earliest
proofs can probably be attributed to Gaffney34 [184, 185], who used mollification techniques
to show that the minimal and maximal extensions (see Section 1.2.1) of ∆coincide. Shortly
after, a different proof was obtained independently by Roelcke [373]. Since then, several alter-
native proofs have been given. Among them, notable examples are those by Chernoff [87] and
Strichartz [423]. The latter one of Strichartz is particularly simple and only requires basic tools
from functional analysis. For completeness, we sketch the main ideas of the proof:
Proof (sketch) of Theorem 3.20. Let A: dom(A) →H be a densely-defined, closed and symmet-
ric operator in a Hilbert space H that is non-negative, i.e. ⟨Aψ, ψ⟩H ≥0 for all ψ ∈dom(A).
Then, A is self-adjoint if and only if ker(A† −λid) = {0} for all λ < 0, i.e. if its adjoint A†
does not admit eigenvectors with negative eigenvalue. Indeed, “⇒” is obvious and for “⇐”, we
observe that dim(ker(λid −A†)) is constant on λ ∈C\[0, ∞) in this case (see e.g. [366, p. 137]).
In particular, combining this with the assumption, we conclude that the deficiency indices
N ± = dim(ker(i ∓A†)) are zero, which implies that A is self-adjoint.
Using this fact, self-adjointness of ∆is equivalent to show that ∆
†ω = λω implies ω = 0
for λ < 0 and ω ∈dom(∆). In fact, we can simplify this even a bit further. First, note that
∆
† = ∆† = ∆max, where ∆max denotes the maximal closed extension (see Section 1.2.1) of ∆.
Furthermore, by elliptic regularity, we know that ω is smooth whenever ∆maxω is smooth and
hence, self-adjointness of ∆is equivalent to
∆ω = λω
⇒
ω = 0
∀ω ∈Ωk(Σ, C) ∩L2

In particular, we find ∥ΦdΣω∥L2 + ∥ΦδΣω∥L2 ≤4∥dΣΦ∥∞∥ω∥L2. The idea is now to construct

With such a family, choose Φ = Φr,s in the above estimate and let s →∞for r fixed. It follows
that dΣω = 0 and δΣω = 0 and hence ω = λ−1∆ω = 0, which concludes the proof.

Remark 3.21. It is important to stress that completeness of (Σ, h) is a sufficient but clearly
not a necessary condition for essential self-adjointness of ∆: Ωk
c(Σ, C) →L2
k(Σ). In fact, the
question of essential self-adjointness of ∆is closely related to whether an L2-version of Stokes’
theorem holds. In his original paper [185], Gaffney considers manifolds for which such a theorem
holds and calls them manifolds of negligible boundary. In a subsequent work, he then shows that
complete manifolds fall into this class [186] (see also the generalisation by Yau in [459]). How-
ever, there are many more examples. For instance, following [185,186], any manifold obtained
from removing a (n −2)-dimensional surface from Rn has negligible boundary.
34 Its worth mentioning, however, that Gaffney considered a larger domain. For dΣ (resp. δΣ), he considers the
set of all C1-forms such that both α and dΣα (resp. δΣα) are in L2 and for ∆= dΣδΣ + δΣdΣ the obvious
domain induced thereof. Essentially self-adjointness for dom(∆) = Ωk
c(Σ, C) is proven, for instance, in [87,423].

There exist several alternative (and equivalent) approaches for defining the operators Es
without resorting to spectral calculus. For example, Strichartz [423] provides a construction
employing the heat kernel and semigroup techniques.
One of the main advantages of the
spectral-theoretic definition, however, is that many useful properties follow directly from the
general framework of spectral calculus:

Proposition 3.24. The self-adjoint operators Es have the following properties:
(i) σ(Es) ⊂[1, ∞) for s ≥0, while σ(Es) ⊂[0, 1] for s < 0.
(ii) The operators Es for s < 0 are bounded with dom(Es) = L2

(iii) Es : D(Es) →L2

(iv) Em = (id + ∆)m for all m ∈N, where dom((id + ∆)m) = Ωk
c(Σ, C). In other words,
taking the closure and the mth power are commuting operations for the operator id+∆

Proof. Define fs ∈C(σ(E), R) by f(λ) := λs. By definition of the spectral calculus, it holds
that Es := fs(E). Most of the claims then follow from the general properties of the functional
calculus (see Appendix A.3 for details): by continuity of fs, it holds that
σ(fs(E)) = fs(σ(E)) ⊂{λs | λ ∈[0, ∞)}
and hence σ(Es) ⊂[1, ∞) for s ≥0 as well as σ(Es) ⊂[0, 1] for s < 0. This shows (i). For (ii),
we note that fs is a bounded function with ∥fs∥∞= 1 on σ(E) ⊂[1, ∞) for s < 0 and for (iii),
since fs(λ) ̸= 0 on σ(E), we conclude that E = fs(E) is bijective with inverse (1/fs)(E) = E−s.
For (iv), we note that in fact all the powers of the de Rham-Hodge Laplacian are essentially
self-adjoint, see [87,98]. Hence, the claim follows from uniqueness of the self-adjoint extension.
For (v), let us first take r, s ∈R such that r ≤s. Then, for ω ∈dom(Es), it follows that

Remark 3.27. As already indicated at the beginning of this section, there are several non-
equivalent definitions of Sobolev spaces on complete Riemannian manifolds. As we will show
below, our functional-analytic definition is equivalent to define Hs

Ωk

{ω ∈Ωk(Σ, C) | ∥ω∥Hs < ∞} with respect to ∥· ∥Hs instead. Furthermore, one could generalise
the standard definition on Rd by defining Hm
k (Σ) for m ∈N0 using weak/distributional covariant
derivatives (see e.g. [344, Def. 10.2.33]).
It is known, however, that these three definitions agree in the special case of a Riemannian
manifold of bounded geometry (see Section A.2.4 in the appendix), which are Riemannian man-
ifolds with non-zero injectivity radius and with the property that the Riemann curvature tensor
and all its derivatives are uniformly bounded. Examples include the Euclidean space (Rd, δ)
and compact Riemannian manifolds. For a proof, we refer to [30, 134]. In the specific case of
differential forms, a short proof can also be found in [119]. For further details on Sobolev spaces
on non-compact manifolds, we refer to the monographs [31,135,238].
Furthermore, we mention that also Sobolev embedding theorems do not hold in general in
the non-compact setting, they do, however, hold in the case of bounded geometry, i.e. for a
Riemannian manifold of bounded geometry there is a continuous embedding Hs
k(Σ) ,→Ωl(Σ, C)
for k, l ∈N0 such that k > l + dim(Σ)/2.

Remark 3.42. (Strong Hodge Decomposition)
Following the terminology briefly mentioned in Remark 3.38, we note that the Hodge decom-
position in Theorem 3.40 is a weak Hodge decomposition. One might ask the question under
which assumptions on the manifold one obtains a strong Hodge decomposition, which would be


## Page 180


160
Chapter 3. Quantisation of Maxwell’s Theory via Complete Gauge Fixing
more similar in spirit to the Hodge decomposition on compact manifolds. An important result
in this direction has been established by Gromov [218], in which a strong Hodge decomposition
of the form
L2
k(Σ) = dΣH1
k−1(Σ) ⊕δΣH1
k+1(Σ) ⊕ker(∆|L2
k∩Ωk)
has been established under the (rather strong) assumption that all de Rham-Hodge Laplacians
∆have a spectral gap (see also [308, 406] for some extensions of this result to the Lp-setting).
Further results in this direction have been obtained by Cantor [76] on asymptotically Eu-
clidean manifolds and by Lockhart [311] on noncompact manifolds with finitely many ends and
asymptotically translational invariant metrics, both for suitable weighted Sobolev spaces.
A
strong Hodge decomposition in weighted Lp-Sobolev spaces on submanifolds of Rd with com-
pact boundaries has been established by Schwarz in [403]. Last but not least, let us mention
the recent preprint [86], in which a strong Hodge-decomposition of H1
k(Σ) is established for
noncompact manifolds of nonpositive constant sectional curvature.
▲

Remark 3.46. Consider the case of a compact manifold Σ. Then, Ωk
s,d(Σ) = dΣΩk−1(Σ, C)

as a special case of Theorem 3.44 in the compact case.

As an immediate consequence of Theorem 3.44, we get existence of solutions of the Poisson
equation, which is closely related to the achievability of the Cauchy radiation gauge.
Proposition 3.49. Let s ∈[0, ∞] and ω ∈Ω1
s(Σ). Then the Poisson equation
∆f = δΣω ,
has a unique solution (up to constant) in the space {f ∈C∞(Σ, C) | dΣf ∈Ω1
s,d(Σ)}.
Proof. By Theorem 3.44, ω can be written as ω = α+β, where α ∈Ω1
s,d(Σ) and β ∈ker(δΣ|Ω1s).
Furthermore, α = dΣf for some f ∈C∞(Σ, C). It follows that δΣω = δΣα = ∆f.
For uniqueness, let f ∈C∞(Σ, C) be such that dΣf ∈Ω1
s,d(Σ) and ∆f = 0. Then, the
1-form η := dΣf satisfies dΣη = 0 and δΣη = 0 and hence in particular η ∈ker(∆|Ω1s). Since
also η ∈Ω1
s,d(Σ) by assumption, we conclude that η = 0, by the fact that the decomposition in
Theorem 3.44 is a direct sum decomposition. It follows that f is closed and hence constant.

3.3
Existence of Hadamard States for Maxwell’s Theory
After this functional-analytic intermezzo, we return to the discussion of Maxwell’s theory
and the Cauchy radiation gauge, leading us to the construction of Hadamard states.
The
discussion in this section follows [340, Sec. 4-6].
As already mentioned at the beginning of this chapter, we restrict our analysis to ultrastatic
spacetimes, as this suffices for the purpose of constructing Hadamard states. We note, however,
that the first part of this section, namely the Cauchy problem for H∞-differential forms, the
achievability of the Cauchy radiation gauge as well as the definition and equivalence of various
phases spaces for Maxwell’s theory, can be extended to more general globally hyperbolic space-
times, provided suitable decay conditions are imposed on their lapse function and on the second
fundamental form of the foliation. We refer to [340, Sec. 4 and 5] for details.
Throughout the following, let (M, g) be a globally hyperbolic ultrastatic spacetime with
adapted Cauchy temporal function t ∈C∞(M) such that
M = R × Σ ,
g = −dt ⊗dt + h ,
where (Σ, h) is a complete Riemannian manifold (cf. Example 1.10(i)). Furthermore, we assume
that (Σ, h) is of bounded geometry, which means that its injectivity radius is non-zero and that its
Riemann curvature and all its covariant derivatives are uniformly bounded (see Appendix A.2.4).
The reason for this technical assumption is that those kind of manifold allow for a nice calculus
of pseudodifferential operators, which we will employ lateron in the construction of states.
Since (Σ, h) is complete, we can apply the analysis of the previous section on differential
forms defined on Σ. Let us recall the following notation:

3.3.1
The Cauchy Problem for Differential Forms in Spatial H∞
As discussed at the end of Section 3.1.4, the achievability of the Cauchy radiation gauge
is closely linked to the solvability of the Poisson equation ∆f = δΣω in a suitable function
space, while the uniqueness of solutions in an appropriate space ensures that this gauge fixing
is complete (cf. Proposition 3.17).
As already emphasised, both of these questions depend
crucially on the class of fields under consideration. In Proposition 3.49, we showed that the
desired properties hold when working within Sobolev spaces.

appropriate Sobolev spaces. In practice, it is convenient to work with a class of fields that is
closed under differential operators; therefore, we will consider the case s = ∞.

Now, having set up the notation for the relevant phase spaces, let us turn to the construction
of Hadamard states on the ultrastatic spacetime (M, g).
As a first step, we show that the
hyperbolic operator D2 = P + K∗K = □admits a microlocal factorisation, which corresponds
to the splitting into positive and negative energy solutions briefly sketched in Section 2.3.3. By
assumption, (Σ, h) is a Riemannian manifold of bounded geometry. On those class of manifolds,
there is a well-behaved class of pseudodifferential operators, which we review in Section A.2.4
of the appendix in some detail. For convenience of the reader, let us recall the basic set-up and
central ideas. We refer to Section A.2.4 for a detailed discussion.
The manifold (Σ, h) is of bounded geometry (see Definition A.46), if its injectivity radius
is non-zero and if its Riemannian curvature tensor together with all its covariant derivatives
is (uniformly) bounded with respect to the norm induced by the metric h.
An equivalent
characterisation, which is useful in practise, is to require that there exists a local chart φp : Up →
B1(0) around every p ∈M, where B1(0) ⊂Rd denotes the open ball around 0 with radius 1, such

for all p ∈M. In other words, around every point p, the metric g behaves like the Euclidean met-

Note that in Proposition 2.59 we had the slightly stronger condition that c++c−= id. However,
it is clear that c+ + c−= id modulo an operator mapping to ran(KΣ) equally gives rise to a
well-defined Hadamard state, since the property Λ+ −Λ−= σ for Λ± : Vc × Vc →C defined by
Λ±([·], [·]) := (·, λ±·)A1 is clearly not affected by this additional contribution.
■
3.3.4
A Deformation Argument
In this last section, we prove the main theorem of this chapter, namely the existence of
Hadamard states for Maxwell’s theory on general globally hyperbolic spacetimes. To achieve
this goal, the idea is to employ a deformation argument, similar in spirit to the one briefly
sketched in Section 2.3.3 for Klein-Gordon’s theory, which allows us to reduce the proof of
existence of Hadamard states to the ultrastatic setting. In the discussion of Klein-Gordon’s
theory in Section 2.3.3, we explained the deformation argument developed by Fulling-Narcowich-
Wald [180] and a similar deformation argument following those lines of reasoning for Maxwell’s


## Page 201


3.3. Existence of Hadamard States for Maxwell’s Theory
181
theory, and, more generally, linearised Yang-Mills theory, can be found in [198, Sec. 3.5]. Here
we follow a different approach based on paracausal deformations.
If M is any smooth manifold, it is easy to see that g := λg1 +(1−λ)g2 with λ ∈[0, 1] for two
Riemannian metrics g1, g2 on M is again a Riemannian metric on M, since positive-definiteness
is preserved by taking convex combinations.
The same statement, however, is not true for
pseudo-Riemannian (and hence also Lorentzian) metrics, since non-degeneracy is in general lost
by taking such combinations. This naturally raises the following question:
Question: Is there a natural operation on the space of (globally hyperbolic) Lorentzian metrics
on M that preserves both the Lorentzian signature and possibly global hyperbolicity?
A suitable operation has been proposed by Moretti-Murro-Volpe in [334], which we shall
briefly review here. Let M be a smooth manifold that admits globally hyperbolic metrics. This
does, of course, impose some restrictions on the topology of M. For instance, M has necessarily
to be non-compact (see Lemma 1.4) and, moreover, admit a product structure, i.e. M ∼= R × Σ
for some smooth hypersurface Σ, as discussed in Theorem 1.8.
To start with, let g and g′ be two time-oriented36 Lorentzian metrics on M. Then, we define
the preorder, i.e. reflexive and transitive, relation
g ⪯g′

∀p ∈M : Vg

p := {v ∈TpM | gp(v, v) < 0} denotes the (open) light cone of p ∈M, as in Eq. (1.1). If
the two time-orientations of g, g′ coincide, i.e. if they are induced by the same global continuous
timelike vector field, then we also have (Vg

for all subsets A ⊂M.
With this terminology, there is the following result:
Proposition 3.63. (following [334, Thm. 2.18(1),(5)])
Let M be a smooth manifold, g, g′ be two smooth Lorentzian metrics on M with g ⪯g′ and
χ ∈C∞(M, [0, 1]). Then, gχ := (1 −χ)g + χg′ defines a Lorentzian metric on M satisfying
g ⪯gχ ⪯g′. If, moreover, g is globally hyperbolic and g′ time-oriented, then gχ is globally
hyperbolic as well.
Now, with this notation, we define the notion of paracausal deformations, which encodes
the idea of deforming a given Lorentzian metric equipped with a time-orientation into another
Lorentzian metric with a corresponding time-orientation in a finite number of steps, each of
which is compatible with the respective causal structures.
Definition 3.64. (Paracausal Relation, [334, Def. 2.19])
Let M be a smooth manifold and g, g′ be two globally hyperbolic metrics on M. We say
that g is paracausally related to g′ and write g ∼g′, if there exists a finite sequence of
globally hyperbolic metrics (gi)i=1,...,N with g1 = g and gN = g′, such that
∀p ∈M , i ∈{1, . . . , N −1} :
either
(Vgi

In other words, for every i ∈{1, . . . , N −1}, either gi ⪯gi+1 or gi+1 ⪯gi while preserving
the respective time orientations.

metrics on M, see Remark 1.1.

Clearly, ∼defines an equivalence relation on the space of globally hyperbolic metrics on M.
Furthermore, we stress that this definition explicitly depends on the choice of time-orientations.
Now, the most important result for our purposes is the fact that any globally hyperbolic
metric can paracausally be deformed into a ultrastatic one.
Theorem 3.65. Every globally hyperbolic metric g on some smooth manifold M = R × Σ
is paracausally related to a globally hyperbolic ultrastatic metric gu = −dt ⊗dt + h such
that (Σ, h) is of bounded geometry.
Proof (sketch). The previous theorem appeared in [335, Sec. 5] and is a combination of several
results established in [334]. First of all, in [334, Prop. 2.22], it is shown that if (Vg

such that h ⪯g and h ⪯g′ and such that their time orientations agree. We omit the technical
proof, which can be found in [334], and refer to Figure 3.1 for the intuitive idea.

Having established this result, it is straightforward to show that any globally hyperbolic
metric g is paracausally related to an ultrastatic one, see [334, Cor. 2.23]. We identify M = R×Σ
and g = −dt ⊗dt + ht for some time-dependent Riemannian metric ht.
This is not a loss
of generality, since we can always normalise a globally hyperbolic in time to obtain a lapse
function β = 1, e.g. by performing a conformal transformation, where we recall that such
a transformation preserves the causal structure and hence global hyperbolicity.
Now, take
any complete Riemannian metric h on Σ and set g′ := −dt ⊗dt + h, which, as explained in
Example 1.10(i), is globally hyperbolic. Moreover, the vector field ∂t is clearly contained in the

is well-known that every smooth manifold admits a Riemannian metric of bounded geometry,
see e.g. [215], which shows that h may be chosen such that (Σ, h) is of bounded geometry.
■
We are finally in the position to prove the main theorem of this chapter, namely the existence
of Hadamard states for Maxwell’s theory on general globally hyperbolic spacetimes.
Theorem 3.66. (Existence of Hadamard States for Maxwell’s Theory)
Let (M, g) be a globally hyperbolic spacetime and consider Maxwell’s theory
(A0 = CM, A1 = CM ⊗T∗M, P = δd, K = d)
as defined in Proposition 2.22. Moreover, let CCR(Vc, σ) be the CCR-algebra as defined in
Definition 2.51 associated to the classical phase space (Vc, σ) as defined in Definition 2.15.
Then, there exists a Hadamard state on CCR(Vc, σ).

## Page 204

Chapter 3. Quantisation of Maxwell’s Theory via Complete Gauge Fixing


## Page 205


Summary and Outlook
At the beginning of this thesis, we performed a detailed analysis of the Cauchy problem
for linear hyperbolic differential operators on globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifolds.
In
particular, we showed that the Cauchy problem is well posed for both symmetric hyperbolic
systems and normally hyperbolic operators. We next studied two departures from the classical
theory of hyperbolic PDEs. First, we examined what happens when we give up on locality, at
least in the lower-order terms, by adding nonlocal potentials to an otherwise local symmetric
hyperbolic system.
In full generality, one cannot expect well-posedness, essentially due to
an inherent conflict with the classical energy methods. Nonetheless, we analysed the Cauchy
problem for two specific classes of nonlocal potentials, namely retarded potentials and potentials
with a small time range, and we established existence of solutions under reasonable assumptions.
Our second deviation concerned linear gauge theories, which form the central part of this
thesis. Such theories are inherently non-hyperbolic. However, thanks to the gauge symmetry
present, they can be transformed into hyperbolic problems supplemented by gauge constraints,
when we consider gauge equivalent classes of solution. After discussing numerous examples of
direct physical interest in many details, we moved on to the quantum theory by presenting the
general and abstract framework for quantising linear gauge theories. Finally, in the last chapter
we carried out a detailed analysis of the quantisation of a specific linear gauge theory, namely
Maxwell’s theory of electromagnetism. In particular, we proved the existence of Hadamard
states for this theory on globally hyperbolic spacetimes.
In what follows, we provide a brief discussion and outlook on the construction of Hadamard
states for yet another linear gauge theory, namely linearised gravity (see Section 2.2.4), which
remains an important open problem.
Gauge-Invariant Initial Data and Linearised Gravity
As highlighted in Section 2.3.3, the existence of Hadamard states for linearised gravity is an
important open question and so far, constructing such states has only be achieved for spatially
compact globally hyperbolic manifolds of bounded geometry [192] and, in the case of manifolds
with a non-compact Cauchy surface, for Minkowski spacetime [256], de-Sitter spacetime [202]
and, for a suitable subspace of observables, for asymptotically flat spacetimes [51].
One of the main complications in linearised gravity compared to other linear gauge theories
is the fact that a deformation argument cannot consistently be applied. As discussed in Sec-
tion 2.2.4, the linearised Einstein equations only admit a gauge symmetry if the background
metric around which we linearise is a solution to the non-linear Einstein equations (see Propo-
sition 2.41) and, as a consequence of the non-linearity of the Einstein equations, it is not clear
how to deform a given metric such that we stay within the class of Einstein metrics. Therefore,
for showing the existence of Hadamard states for linearised gravity, one needs to find an explicit
185


## Page 206


186
Summary and Outlook
construction on general globally hyperbolic spacetimes, which is a highly non-trivial task. The
approach in [192] for spatially compact spacetimes is based on a complete gauge fixing approach,
however, crucially depends on the compactness assumption.
Now, as explained in Remark 3.19, the Cauchy radiation gauge and the general idea of
complete gauge fixing on the level of initial data admits a nice interpretation in terms of gauge-
invariant (“physical”) initial data. In the following, we give a short outlook on how such ideas
can be applied in the context of linearised gravity. We only consider the case of a spatially-
compact and ultrastatic globally hyperbolic manifold of the form (M = R×Σ, g = −dt+dt+h),
where (Σ, h) denotes a compact Riemannian manifolds. We will comment below on the problems
that must be addressed in order to generalise this approach to more general spacetimes.
To start with, let us assume that the ultrastatic metric g is a solution to the vacuum Einstein
equations, i.e.
EinΛ(g) = Ric(g) −1
2Scal(g)g + Λg = 0 .
Now, let us choose local coordinates (x0 = t, xi) on M, where (xi)i=1,...,n are local coordinates
on Σ. In the ultrastatic case, we have Riem(g)αβγδ = 0 whenever at least one index is zero,
while Riem(g)ijkl = Riem(h)ijkl for spatial indices. In particular, we have Ric(g)αβ = 0 if either
α = 0 or β = 0 and Ric(g)ij = Ric(h)ij as well as Scal(g) = Scal(h). Therefore, the Einstein
equations for g = −dt ⊗dt + h decompose as
0 = EinΛ(g) =
(

2Scal(h)h + Λh
Taking the trace of the last equations gives (2 −n)Scal(h) + 2nΛ = 0 and hence, for this system
of equations to admit a solution, we necessarily need to require Λ = 0. To sum up, for an
ultrastatic metric g to be an Einstein metric, we necessarily have to require Λ = 0 and in this
case, being a solution to the Einstein equations is equivalent to require that (Σ, h) is Ricci flat.

Once again, we stress that some components are only defined up to constant and hence, to
make this precise, one either needs to fix those redundancies or work with suitable quotient
spaces. By construction, Π ◦KΣ = 0 and, similarly to the Maxwell case, it is expected that this

where the space on the right-hand side has the interpretation of the space of gauge-invariant,
i.e. “physical”, initial data.
Starting from there, one can approach to construct Hadamard
states for linearised gravity, either by following similar a strategy as presented in this thesis for
Maxwell’s theory, or by using an approach based on modified Cauchy covariances as studied
in [198].
Now, in the previous approach, we restricted our analysis to spatially compact and ultrastatic
globally hyperbolic spacetimes. To follow a similar strategy for more general globally hyperbolic
background spacetimes, the following two aspects need to be considered:
(i) First, one needs to find a suitable generalisation of the tensor field decompositions pre-
sented above to non-compact Riemannian manifolds. Achieving decompositions such as
the York decomposition on Riemannian manifolds usually requires to solve elliptic equa-
tions and one might hope that the relevant decompositions can be achieved under suitable
assumptions on the geometry and topology.
(ii) In the non-ultrastatic case, the operators KΣ and K†
Σ take a more complicated form.
However, when working with the adapted initial data maps, it is expected that one can
derive suitably simply expressions, at least on specific spacetimes such as static ones.
Tackling the previous problems and making the procedure outlined above more precise is
subject to future work.


## Page 211


AppendixA
Appendix
A.1
Microlocal Analysis I: Distributions and Wavefront Set
In the first section of the Appendix, we review some basics of the theory of distributions.
In particular, we discuss distributions on manifolds, the celebrated Schwartz kernel theorem as
well as some basic notions of microlocal analysis.
A.1.1
Basics of Distribution Theory
To begin with, we provide a short introduction to distribution theory, starting with the local
theory in Rd. Afterwards, we review the global theory on manifold. We omit citations as the
results are well known. We refer to the textbooks [127,172,252,431] for details.
Distributions, the Local Theory.
Let K ⊂{R, C}, d ∈N and consider an arbitrary open
subset U ⊂Rd. Throughout the following, we will use the notation C∞(U) := C∞(U, K). Now,
we consider the space C∞(U) as a locally convex topological vector space (short LCTVS), whose
topology is induced by the family of seminorms (i.e. the initial topology38 with respect to)

in fact Hausdorff and complete. In particular, C∞(U) is a Fréchet space. Now, consider

Fréchet spaces”, owing to the fact that notion of LF-spaces can be made more systematic using

c (U) inherited from the C∞-topology on C∞(U).
It turns out that the topology of the canonical LF-topology is very rich: first of all, C∞
c (U) is
a complete Hausdorff LCTVS. It is also nuclear, which implies that the projective and injective
tensor products of C∞
c (U) with any other LCTVS coincide. Furthermore, it is a so-called Montel
space, which in particular implies that it is reflexive, that a version of the Banach-Steinhaus
theorem holds and that it has the Heine-Borel property, i.e. that subsets are compact if and only
if they are closed and bounded. However, it is important to stress that C∞
c (U) is not metrisable
and hence in particular not a Fréchet space.
Definition A.1. (Distributions)
Let U ⊂Rd be open and consider the space C∞
c (U) equipped with its canonical LF-space

D′(U) := (C∞
c (U))′ = {φ: C∞

T ∈D′(U) is called a (Schwartz) distribution on U and we introduce the bilinear pairing
⟨·, ·⟩U : D′(U) × C∞
c (U) →K ,
⟨T, φ⟩U := T(φ) .
Remark A.2. Let (X, TX) and (Y, TY) be two topological spaces and f : X →Y be arbitrary.
Then, if f is continuous, it is also sequentially continuous, i.e. f(xn) →f(x) for any sequence
(xn)n∈N converging to x ∈X. The reverse, however, is not true in general. In fact, it is true
if (X, TX) is first-countable, however, this is only a sufficient condition.39 Now, the space of
test functions C∞
c (U) is not first-countable, nevertheless, it turns out that a linear functional
T: C∞
c (U) →K is still continuous if and only if it sequentially continuous. In fact, this is a
consequence of the limit structure of the topology on C∞
c (U): let T: C∞
c (U) →K be sequentially
continuous. Then, by definition, φ|C∞
K is continuous for every K ⊂U compact. But C∞
K (U) is
metrisable and hence in particular first countable, which shows continuity of T|C∞
K (U). Since K
was arbitrary, we conclude that T is continuous. To sum up, a linear map T: C∞
c (U) →K is in
D′(U) if and only if T(φn) →T(φ) for all (φn)n∈N in C∞
c (U) converging to φ ∈C∞
c (U).
▲
In practice, the space of distributions D′(U) is typically endowed with one of two topologies,
each of which is widely used depending on the application: consider a topological vector space
X with topological dual space X′ and consider the bilinear pairing ⟨·, ·⟩: X′ × X →R defined
by ⟨x′, x⟩:= x′(x). Furthermore, for a given bounded subset B ⊂X, consider the seminorm
pB(·) := supx∈B |⟨·, x⟩|. Then, we define the following two topologies on X′:
(i) The weak (dual) topology on X′ is the coarsest topology on X′ such that that ⟨·, x⟩: X′ →R
is continuous for all x ∈R. Equivalently, X′ equipped with the weak topology is exactly
the LCTVS whose topology is induced by the family of seminorms (pF)F∈F, where F is
the collection of all finite subsets of X.
(ii) The strong (dual) topology on X′ is the locally convex topology induced by the family
(pB)B∈B, where B is the collection of bounded subsets of X.
By definition, the strong dual topology is strictly finer than the weak dual topology. Now, we
can equip the space D′(U) both with the strong and the weak dual topology induced from the

true again, i.e. a function is continuous if any only if it is net continuous.

c (U) →R | linear and continuous } .
Now, consider the inclusion map i: C∞
c (U) →C∞(U). This map is clearly continuous with
respect to the canonical LF-topology on C∞
c (U) and the Fréchet topology on C∞(U), since, as
mentioned above, the former is strictly finer than the subspace topology induced from C∞(U).
Furthermore, the inclusion map can be shown to be an injection with dense image, which in
particular implies that the corresponding transpose map it : E′(U) →D′(U), where both spaces
are equipped with their strong dual topologies, is also a continuous injection. In other words,
E′(U) can naturally be viewed as a subspace of D′(U).
In fact, a distribution T ∈D′(U) is in E(U) exactly when it is also continuous in the coarser
subspace topology on C∞
c (U) induced from C∞(U), in which case it extends to a linear functional
on all of C∞(U), by density. Looking at a regular distribution Tψ, i.e. Example A.3(ii), this
is the case when the labelling function ψ is compactly supported. More precisely, distributions
in E′(U) can be characterised as follows: first of all, if V ⊂U ⊂Rd are two open sets, we can

c (V) trivially by zero to view it as an element of C∞
c (U). This

c (U), which is a linear and continuous injection.41
Hence, its transpose provides a linear continuous map et : D′(U) →D′(V), which is called the

supp(T) := U\{Ω⊂U open | T|Ω= 0} .
By definition, this is a closed subset of U. Now, as it turns out, the space E′(U) is exactly the
space of compactly supported distributions, i.e.
E′(U) = {T ∈D′(U) | supp(T) compact } .

S′(Rd) := (S(Rd))′ = {φ: S(Rd) →K | linear and continuous } .

A.1.2
The Kernel Theorem of Schwartz
Having set up the terminology and notation for distributions both in the local and global
setting, we now briefly discuss the celebrated kernel theorem of Schwartz, which allows us to
represent a linear and continuous operator between spaces of test sections and distributions in
terms of a bidistributional kernel. It was originally proven by Schwartz [401]; for a modern
proof, we refer to [431, Thm. 51.7] and [425, Thm. 1.5.1].
For the formulation of the kernel theorem, we recall that E ⊠F for two finite-rank smooth

Let M be a smooth manifold and E, F π−→M be two finite-rank K-bundle over M. A linear

As explained in Remark A.6, the space of distributional kernels is homeomorphic to the
tensor product D′(F)ˆ⊗D′(E∗). We consider the following important subspaces:
(i) A kernel kA is called semiregular, if kA ∈Γ∞(F)ˆ⊗D′(E∗).
(ii) A kernel kA is called continuable, if kA ∈D′(F)ˆ⊗Γ∞(E∗).
(iii) A kernel kA is called biregular if it is both semiregular and continuable.
(iv) A kernel kA is called properly supported, if the projections pr1,2 : supp(kA) →M onto the
first and second factor are proper maps (i.e. preimages of compact sets are compact).
Formally, a kernel is semiregular (resp. continuable), if kA(x, y) is smooth in the variable x
(resp. y). Let us also stress that biregularity is a weaker property than regularity. For instance,
the kernel of the identity operator id: C∞
c (R) →C∞
c (R) is given by kid(x, y) = δ(x −y), which
can easily seen to be biregular. However, kid is clearly not regular.

to a map A: E′(E) →E′(F), which in turn continuously extends to A: D′(E) →D′(F).

A.1. Microlocal Analysis I: Distributions and Wavefront Set
