A.1.3
Singular Support and Wavefront Set
As a next step, we take a closer look at the singularities of distributions by introducing the
concept of the wavefront set, a central notion of microlocal analysis. Recall that a distribution
T ∈D′(E) is called regular if it lies in the image of the map (A.1), or equivalently, if it can
(globally) be identified with a smooth section. For a general distribution T ∈D′(E), we define
the singular support as the set of points around which the distribution fails to be regular, i.e.,
sing supp(T) := M\{Ω⊂M open | T|Ωregular } ⊂supp(T) .
In general, the product of two distributions is not well-defined, since already the concept
of a “value at a point” lacks meaning in full generality. Nevertheless, motivated by physical
applications, one may still ask whether the multiplication of distributions can be defined in a
consistent way, at least under suitable restrictions. To start with, if we consider two distributions
T, S ∈D′(U) for U ⊂Rd open such that sing supp(T) ∩sing supp(S) = ∅, there is an obvious
way to define their product, e.g. by choosing an open cover (Ui)i∈I of U such that for every i ∈I,
we have either T|Ui ∈C∞(Ui) or S|Ui ∈C∞(Ui), and by defining T · S ∈D′(U) via
(T · S)ψ :=
(
T(Sψ)
if S|Ui ∈C∞(Ui)
S(Tψ)
if T|Ui ∈C∞(Ui)
for all ψ ∈C∞
c (Ui). It is easy to see that this is well-defined, since on overlaps, on which both
S and T are smooth, both definition agree with the pointwise multiplication. More generally,
if we consider two distributions whose singular supports are not disjoint, there are still some
situations in which a product can meaningfully be defined. For instance, we can always consider
the pointwise product of continuous functions, even though their singular supports (which only
detects the failure of being smooth) might not be disjoint. Furthermore, if we consider the
Heaviside function θ with sing supp(θ) = {0}, it intuitively makes sense to set θ2 = θ.
By the previous discussion, it is clear that we need a more refined description of the sin-
gularities of a distribution, which leads us to the notion of the wavefront set. The wavefront
set of a distribution was originally introduced by Sato43 [394, 395] and Hörmander [249, 250]
in the early 1970s. We refer to [66] for a gentle introduction and [252] for a detailed discus-
sion. The central idea is to analyse distributions in Fourier space. As a consequence of the
Paley–Wiener–Schwartz theorem, a distribution u ∈E′(Rd) is regular, that is, u ∈C∞
c (Rd), if
and only if for every m ∈N there exists a constant Cm > 0 such that
|F(u)(ξ)| ≤Cm(1 + |ξ|)−m
for all ξ ∈Rd, where the left-hand side is well-defined, since F(u) ∈C∞(Rd) for all u ∈E′(Rd).
Definition A.8. (Wavefront Set)
Let T ∈D′(U).
We call a pair (p, ξ) ∈U × (Rd\{0}) regular directed, if there exists
χ ∈C∞
c (U) with χ(p) = 1 and a closed conic neighbourhooda V of ξ such that for all
m ∈N there exists a Cm > 0 with
|F(χT)(ξ)| ≤Cm(1 + |ξ|)−m .
The wavefront set WF(T) is the set of all (p, ξ) ∈U×(Rd\{0}) that are not regular directed.
a i.e. for all λ > 0 it holds that λξ ∈V.
43 More precisely, the definition of Sato is equivalent to what is nowadays known as the analytic wavefront set.


## Page 218


198
Appendix
By definition, the projection of WF(T) onto the first factor is exactly sing supp(T).
In
other words, WF(T) can be understood as a refinement of sing supp(T) that does not only
include informations on where a distribution is singular, but also informations on the singular
direction in Fourier space. Furthermore, to come back to the central motivation, there is a
well-defined notion of a product of two distributions T, S ∈D′(U) if their wavefront sets satisfy
WF(T) ∩(−WF(S)) = ∅. In this case, the product ST is defined as the pullback of their tensor
product along the diagonal map δU : U →U × U (see e.g. [252, 8.2.10] for details).
Example A.9. Consider the δ-distribution δ ∈S′(Rd) defined by δ(φ) := φ(0). Clearly, it
holds that sing supp(δ) = {0}. Now, as a distribution, we have F(δ) = 1. Hence, for arbitrary
χ ∈C∞(U) with χ(0) = 1, it holds that
|F(χδ)(ξ)| = 1
for all ξ ∈V. This shows WF(δ) = {(0, ξ) | ξ ∈Rd\{0}}.
▲
To define the wavefront set on manifolds, we first observe that it behaves covariantly under
the change of coordinates. More precisely, if φ: U →V is a diffeomorphism between two sets
U, V ⊂Rd, then WF(φ∗T) = {(x, (dφx)tξ) | (φ(x), ξ) ∈WF(T)} for all T ∈D′(V), where φ∗T
is defined by duality. In particular, on a smooth manifold M, there is a unique way of defining
WF(T) ⊂T∗M\{0}
for all T ∈D′(M), where 0 denotes the zero-section. More generally, if E is any smooth and
finite-rank vector bundle over M, we define WF(T) ⊂T∗M\{0} for T ∈D′(M, E) as the union
of the wavefront set of its components in a fixed (but arbitrary) local trivialisation.

similar arguments as in Example A.9, one finds
WF(δ) = {((x, ξ), (x, −ξ)) | (x, ξ) ∈Rd × Rd\{0}} .
Now, due to the antisymmetry in this expression, it is customary to define the primed wavefront
set for all T ∈D′(M × M) by
WF′(T) := {((x, ξ), (y, η)) ∈T∗(M × M)\{0} | ((x, ξ), (y, −η)) ∈WF(T)} .
With this definition, we obtain WF′(δ) = ∆\{0}, where ∆:= {((x, ξ), (x, ξ)) | (x, ξ) ∈T∗M}} ⊂

A.2
Microlocal Analysis II: Pseudodifferential Calculus
We ended the discussion of the previous chapter with one of the central notions of microlocal
analysis, namely the wavefront set of distributions. In this chapter, we continue the discussion
of microlocal analysis with the theory of pseudodifferential operators (short ΨDOs). The theory
of ΨDOs and more generally, of Fourier integral operators (FIOs), has been developed in the
1960s by Bokobza-Unterberger [63], Kohn-Nirenberg [288], Hörmander [247,248,250] and others,


## Page 219


A.2. Microlocal Analysis II: Pseudodifferential Calculus
199
generalising earlier work on singular integral operators (see e.g. [72, 73, 325, 326]), as a tool to
study (elliptic) partial differential equations. Since then, pseudodifferential calculus has become
an indispensable tool for studying linear PDEs. For instance, already the simplest operations
one might want to perform on elliptic differential operators (e.g. taking the inverse, square
root, etc.) typically yield operators outside the class of differential operators. ΨDOs provide a
generalisation of differential operators, and under some reasonable assumptions on the class of
symbols, the operations mentioned above can be performed within this class. Furthermore, the
correspondence between operators and their symbols allows many computations and construc-
tions to be carried out purely at the algebraic level of symbols. We refer to [217,240,253,417]
for a detailed discussion of that subject.

The goal of pseudodifferential calculus is to make the above considerations more precise.
One of the main advantages of this approach is that many calculations can be reduced to purely
algebraic considerations on the underlying symbols.

i.e. the 1-form dΦ ∈Ω1(U × Rd
∗) defined by

Hence, condition (iii) means that at every point (x, ξ) ∈U × Rd
∗at least one element of
{∂xiΦ, ∂ξjΦ} is non-zero. To sum up, a phase function is a real-valued smooth function on
U × Rd
∗, which is positively homogeneous of degree one in ξ and which does not have critical
points.
Example A.17. An important example is provided by Φ(x, ξ) := x · ξ, where “·” denotes the
standard inner product on Rd, as usual.

A.2.3
Pseudodifferential and Fourier Integral Operators
In the following, let U ⊂Rd be open. After having discussed suitable classes of symbols and
phase function, we are now in the position to define Fourier integral operators, which are linear
operators whose Schwartz kernel is given by an oscillatory integral.
Definition A.23. (Fourier Integral and Pseudodifferential Operators)
Let a ∈Sm
ρ,σ(U × U) with (ρ, σ) ∈(0, 1] × [0, 1) and Φ ∈P(U × U). A linear and continuous
operator A: C∞
c (U) →D′(U) whose Schwartz kernel is given by kA := IΦ,a ∈D′(U × U) is

ρ,σ(U).

Remark A.24. Note that neither a Fourier integral operator nor a pseudodifferential operator
does in general uniquely define its phase function Φ and symbol a. In other words, different
pairs (Φ, a) can give rise to the same operator. Furthermore, even for a fixed phase function Φ,
the corresponding operator does in general not determine its symbol uniquely.

however, it might be that at some point all the derivatives with respect to x (or y) vanish. If

actually semiregular and in fact, even biregular:

Corollary A.27. (Properties of Pseudodifferential Operators)
Let A ∈Ψm
ρ,σ(U) be a pseudodifferential operator.
(i) A is continuous as an operator A: C∞

Choose a test function φ ∈C∞
c (V) such that φ = 1 on V′. Then, ψ = φψ + (1 −φ)ψ where
ψ1 := φψ ∈C∞
c (U) and ψ2 := (1 −φ)ψ ∈E′(U) vanishes on V′. Since kA is biregular, we have
Aψ1 ∈C∞
c (U). Moreover, Aψ2 ∈D′(U) is regular in V′. Hence, Aψ is regular in V′.

Remark A.28. (Kohn-Nirenberg and Weyl Quantisation)

## Page 228

This quantisation map was first introduced by Weyl in his work on group theory and quantum
mechanics [446,447]. At the same time, Wigner [451] defined what is now known as the Wigner
function in his analysis of quantum corrections to statistical mechanics. Although not originally
framed as such, the Wigner function effectively serves as an inverse to Weyl quantization. The
connection between Weyl’s quantisation and Wigner’s transformation was only fully recognised
in the late 1940s through the work of Groenewold [219] and Moyal [336]. From the point of
view of pseudodifferential calculus, the Weyl quantisation for a general class of symbols has been
extensively studied by Hörmander [251] (see also [253, Sec. 18.5]) and Dencker [111], following
earlier works of Grossmann-Loupias-Stein [221], Berezin-Shubin [52] and Voros [439].
More generally, one can also consider an operator Opτ in which (x −y)/2 is replaced by
1
2(xτ + (1 −τ)y) for τ ∈[0, 1]. The Weyl quantisation has some advantages which makes it
preferable in some applications: first of all, it is invariant under symplectic transformations.
Furthermore, OpW(a) is formally self-adjoint if and only if a is real-valued. More generally, it
holds that Opτ(a)∗= Op1−τ(a). We refer to [417, Chap. IV, Sec. 23] for more details.
▲

Proposition A.30. (Properties of Properly-Supported Pseudodifferential Operators)
Let A: C∞
c (U) →C∞(U) be a properly-supported pseudodifferential operator.
(i) A is linear and continuous as an operator of the form

Proof. Claim (i) is a direct consequence of Proposition A.7(iii) and Proposition A.26. For (ii),
let a ∈Sm
ρ,σ(U × U) be a symbol of A. We choose a test function χ ∈C∞(U × U) such that
χ = 1 in a neighbourhood of supp(kA) and such that supp(χ) is proper.45 Then, substituting
a′(x, y, ξ) := χ(x, y)a(x, yχ) in kA does not change kA and a′ is proper.
■
In other words, the kernel of a properly supported pseudodifferential operator is supported
“sufficiently” close to the diagonal ∆:= {(u, u) ∈U × U}, which is the region containing the
singular support of its Schwartz kernel.
Example A.31. Any linear partial differential operator A = P
α∈Nd, |α|≤k aαDα is a properly
supported ΨDO, since supp(kA) = ∆in this case.
▲
If A, B ∈Ψm
ρ,σ(U) are two properly supported pseudodifferential operators, then the com-
positions A ◦B and B ◦A are well-defined, linear and continuous as operators from C∞
c (U) to
C∞
c (U). As we will see below, they are actually again pseudodifferential operators.
Being properly supported is not a very restrictive property from the microlocal point of
view, since every pseudodifferential operator is properly supported up to a smoothing operator:
Proposition A.32. Let A ∈Ψm
ρ,σ(U). Then, there exist a properly-supported Apr ∈Ψm
ρ,σ(U)
such that A = Apr + S for some S ∈Ψ−∞(U).
Proof. Let χ ∈C∞(U × U) be such that χ(x, y) = 1 in a neighbourhood of the diagonal
∆and such that supp(χ) is proper.
Then, we decompose the symbol a ∈Sm
ρ,σ(U × U) of
A as a = a′ + s, where a′(x, ξ) := χ(x, ξ)a(x, ξ) and s(x, ξ) := (1 −χ(x, ξ))a(x, ξ).
The
pseudodifferential operator Apr with symbol a′ is properly supported and the pseudo-differential
operator S with symbol s is contained in Ψ−∞(U), since s = (1 −χ)a vanishes outside the
diagonal. By construction A = Apr + S.
■
As discussed above, the symbol is not uniquely determined by the pseudodifferential operator
in question. In the special case of differential operators, however, there always exists a preferred

which mimic the behaviour of the symbols of differential operators.

calculus on manifolds.
Remark A.45. (Global Pseudodifferential Calculus)
As discussed above, the quantisation map in Rd establishes a canonical linear isomorphism be-
tween symbol classes and the corresponding pseudodifferential operators (mod S−∞). Moreover,
there are explicit formulas both for constructing an operator from its symbol and for recovering
the symbol of a given operator. A central feature of this calculus is its covariance under coor-
dinate changes. This local covariance, in turn, allows one to define symbol classes on manifolds
with values in vector bundles. Furthermore, one can define pseudodifferential operators on man-
ifolds acting on the sections of vector bundles by patching together local constructions using a
partition of unity, as mentioned above. While sufficient for many applications, this approach de-
pends, at least up to a smoothing operator, on non-canonical choices, such as coordinate systems
and trivialisations, and thus lacks a fully intrinsic character. In fact, only the principal symbol


## Page 235


A.2. Microlocal Analysis II: Pseudodifferential Calculus
215
of a given pseudodifferential operator behaves tensorially and is globally well defined. Conse-
quently, there is strong motivation to develop a coordinate-independent, geometrically natural
theory of symbols and quantisation on manifolds. Several such approaches have been explored
in the literature. Early work concentrated on the Kohn–Nirenberg quantisation for scalar-valued
operators, notably by Bokobza-Haggiag [61,62], Widom [449,450], and Drager [123]. More re-
cent developments have extended these ideas to vector bundle-valued settings, as explored by
Pflaum [359] and Sharafutdinov [413,414]. An invariant τ-quantization on closed Riemannian
manifolds for operators acting in 1-forms has been developed in [82]. Further advancements
in Weyl and τ-quantisations within the scalar framework have been contributed by Dereziński-
Latosiński-Siemssen [113], Underhill [433], Unterberger [434], Liu-Qian [310], Pflaum [358],
Safarov [320,381,382], Fulling [178] (see also [177,179]) and, more recently, by Levy [306] and
Andersson-Moser-Oancea-Paganini-Schmid [21] in the vector-bundle valued case.
More generally, a line of research concerns the definition of global Fourier integral operators
and the global construction of propagators for hyperbolic equations. In particular, Laptev-
Safarov-Vassiliev [297] (see also [384]) demonstrated that the propagator for a broad class of
hyperbolic operators on compact manifolds can be represented as a global Fourier integral op-
erator with complex-valued phase function. Building upon this work and some applications
discussed in [264], a global symbol calculus for Fourier integral operators on closed manifolds
has been developed by Safarov [383]. Furthermore, a geometric approach to global parametrices
for the scalar wave operator on closed Riemannian manifolds, including a global invariant defi-
nition of the full symbol of the wave propagator and an algorithm for explicitly calculating its
homogeneous components, was recently proposed by Capoferri-Levitin-Vassiliev [78]. This result
has been generalised to the Lorentzian setting by Capoferri-Dappiaggi-Drago [77] for the wave
operator on globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifolds with compact Cauchy surfaces. Generali-
sations to the propagator for the Dirac operator have been obtained by Capoferri-Vassiliev [81]
in the Riemannian and by Capoferri-Murro [79] in the Lorentzian setting.
▲
A.2.4
Pseudodifferential Calculus on Manifolds of Bounded Geometry
Let M be a smooth manifold. As discussed above, two properly supported pseudodifferen-
tial operators can be composed. In particular, the space of properly supported ΨDOs forms
a well-defined algebra of operators.
However, for more advanced applications, this calculus
turns out to be too small. For instance, consider a complete Riemannian manifold (M, g). Its
Laplace–Beltrami operator ∆is a classical and properly supported ΨDO of order two, but its
resolvent (−∆+ i)−1 is not properly supported. Now, if we work with compact manifolds,
then every ΨDO is properly supported, yielding a well-defined calculus, namely, the uniform
pseudodifferential calculus on compact manifolds. More generally, however, we need to identify
a class of ΨDOs that is small enough to allow for well-defined composition (and hence to form
an algebra), but large enough to be closed under other operations of interest. Such a class
is provided by the bounded pseudodifferential operators on Riemannian manifolds of bounded
geometry, which we shall discuss now.
Throughout the following, let (M, g) be a smooth and connected Riemannian manifold with
Levi-Civita connection ∇. We recall the following basic terminology of Riemannian geometry:
(i) By standard ODE theory, there exists a geodesic γp,v such that γ(0) = p and ˙γ(0) = v
for every p ∈M and v ∈TpM. Furthermore, all such geodesics agree on their common
domain and we shall denote the maximal possible open domain by Ip,v ⊂R. Now, let
DpM be the set of vectors v ∈TpM such that [0, 1] ⊂Iv. Then, the exponential map
expp : DpM →M at p ∈M is the map defined by expp(v) := γp,v(1). In the case Ip,v = R
and hence DpM = TpM, the manifold (M, g) is called (geodesically) complete and by the
the celebrated Theorem of Hopf-Rinow, this is equivalent to require that M is complete


## Page 236


216
Appendix
as a metric space with respect to the metric induced by Riemannian distance.
(ii) The exponential map expp is not a diffeomorphism in general, even in the case in which
(M, g) is complete, however, by the inverse function theorem, it is always a local diffeo-
morphism. The largest radius R > 0 for which expp : Bgp
R (0) →M is a diffeomorphism
onto its image, is called the injectivity radius at p and denoted by rg(p), where Bgp
R (0)
denotes the open ball of radius R around the origin of the inner product space (TpM, gp).
Accordingly, the injectivity radius of M is defined by
rg := inf
p∈M rg(p) = inf
p∈M



The injectivity radius of the Euclidean space (Rd, δ) is rδ = ∞and the injectivity radius
of an arbitrary Riemannian manifold can be viewed as the largest radius of a ball around
each point within which geodesics behave similarly to that of Euclidean space.
(iii) For a given p ∈M, let us denote by ∥· ∥p the obvious norm on Tr,s
p M = TpM⊗r ⊗T∗
pM⊗s
defined by gp and g−1
p . With this notation, we call a rank (r, s)-tensor field T ∈Γ∞(Tr,sM)
uniformly bounded, if ∥T∥:= supp∈M ∥Tp∥p < ∞.
Definition A.46. A Riemannian manifold (M, g) is said to have bounded geometry, if
rg > 0 and if ∇kRiem(g) ∈Γ∞(T1,3+kM) is uniformly bounded for all k ∈N0, where
Riem(g) ∈Γ∞(T1,3M) denotes the Riemannian curvature tensor of (M, g), as usual.
In general, every Riemannian manifold with rg > 0 can easily be seen to be complete
and hence, every Riemannian manifold of bounded geometry is complete as well.
Besides
(Rd, δ), every compact Riemannian manifold has bounded geometry and every Lie group G
equipped with a bi-invariant Riemannian metric has bounded geometry as well. We also note
that “bounded geometry” is a purely geometric concept, since on every manifold there exists
a Riemannian metric of bounded geometry, see [215]. In other words, there are no topological
obstructions to the existence of Riemannian metrics of bounded geometry.
In what follows, we introduce several definitions and constructions on Riemannian manifolds
of bounded geometry, which will be used to develop the pseudodifferential calculus in this setting.
The following exposition follows the discussion in [195] and [191, Chap. 10] (see also [416,
App. 1]). To start with, we provide an equivalent characterisation of bounded geometry, which
will be very useful in applications.
Let U ⊂Rd be open and let δ denote the Euclidean

A.2.5
Propagation of Singularities
In the last section of this exposition on microlocal analysis, we briefly discuss the propagation
of singularities theorem, which, roughly speaking, states that the singularities of solutions to
partial and pseudodifferential equations governed by so-called “real principal type operators”
travel along the bicharacteristic flow determined by the associated Hamiltonian vector field.
To start with, we recall some terminology from Hamiltonian systems, which clarifies the
geometric picture underlying the forthcoming discussion. We refer to [377] for details.
(i) Let (M, ω) be a symplectic manifold, i.e. a smooth n-manifold with non-degenerate and
closed ω ∈Ω2(M). Since every skew-symmetric matrix in odd dimensions is necessarily
degenerate, we have n = 2d for some d ∈N. Now, for a given function f ∈C∞(M),
we call Xf := −(df)♯, where the isomorphism ♯: T∗M →TM is defined by ω in analogy
to Riemannian geometry, the Hamiltonian vector field associated to f. In other words,
Xf⌟ω = −df. Now, it is well known that around every point p ∈M, we can find a local
coordinate chart (x1, . . . , xd, ξ1, . . . , ξd) such that ω = dξi ∧dxi (cf. Darbeaux Theorem).
With respect to this local coordinate system, the vector field Xf is given by
Xf = (∂ξif)∂xi −(∂xif)∂ξi .
The flow Φf : I × M →M of the vector field Xf, where I ⊂R is some open time interval,
is called the Hamiltonian flow of f. By definition,
Φf

(ii) The cotangent bundle T∗M of any smooth d-manifold M can naturally be understood as
a 2d-dimensional symplectic manifold whose symplectic form is given as follows: consider
a local chart (U, φ = (x1, . . . , xd)) of M around some point p ∈M and let π: T∗M →M
denote the bundle projection. Then, (π−1(U), ψ := (xi, ξi)) defined by

Prop. 11.9]). The 1-form θ ∈Ω1(T∗M) defined by θξ(X) := ξ(dπξ(X)) for ξ ∈T∗M and

form, and ω := dθ = dξi ∧dxi defines the canonical symplectic form on T∗M.
Now, consider a pseudodifferential operator A ∈Ψm(M) of order m and denote its principal
symbol by a(x, ξ) := σA(x, ξ). By definition, a ∈C∞(T∗M), and we denote by Ha ∈X(T∗M)
the Hamiltonian vector field associated to a. In local coordinates (xi, ξi) of T∗M, we have
Ha = (∂ξia)∂xi −(∂xia)∂ξi .
Now, we call the integral curves of the Hamiltonian vector field associated to the pseudodif-
ferential operator A the bicharacteristics of A. If a bicharacterstic γ(t) = (xi(t), ξi(t)) satisfies
a(xi(t), ξi(t)) = 0 for all t ∈I, i.e. if it is contained in char(A), it is called a null bicharacteristic.

for a simple class of operators. More precisely, we consider the Cauchy problem

The following theorem is due to Egorov [132], see also [240, Sec. 7.2] or [427, Sec. 7.8].
Theorem A.49. (Egorov Theorem)
Let P0 ∈Ψm(Rd) with symbol p0 ∈Sm(Rd) and define P(t) := U(t, 0) ◦P0 ◦U(0, t). Then,
there exists a (time-dependent) smoothing operator R such that P(t) −R(t) ∈Ψm(Rd)
Moreover, the principal symbol of P(t) is given by σP(t)(Φt(x0, ξ0)) = p0(x0, ξ0).
Proof (sketch). To start with, let us take the time derivative of the defining relation P(t) :=
U(t, 0)◦P0◦U(0, t), which yields ∂tP(t) = i[A(t), P(t)]. The idea of the proof is then to construct
an approximate solution Q(t) of this equation, i.e. an operator Q(t) such that
∂tQ(t) = [A(t), Q(t)] + R(t)

■
Now, let us briefly discuss the general situation. Let M be a smooth manifold. We say
that a properly supported pseudodifferential operator P ∈Ψm(M) is of real principal type, if its
principal symbol p(x, ξ) := σP(x, ξ) is real-valued and (positively) homogeneous of degree m,
i.e. p(x, λξ) = λmp(x, ξ) for any λ > 0 and ξ ̸= 0, and if dp ̸= 0 on char(P). In particular, if
Hp ∈X(T∗M) is the corresponding Hamiltonian vector field, then Hp ̸= 0 on char(P).
Remark A.51. The equation Pu = f for operators of real principal type is locally solvable,
i.e. for every f ∈C∞(M) there exists a local solution u ∈D′(M) on some compact set K ⊂M (for
differential operators, this result is due to Hörmander [246]). However, for complex coefficients,
this is not true in general and a famous and surprisingly simple counterexample was provided
by Lewy [307]. It was conjectured that a necessary and sufficient condition for local solvability
is the so-called “condition (Ψ)” (cf. Nirenberg-Treves conjecture [345,346]). The conjecture was
proven to be true by Dencker [112].
▲
Theorem A.52. (Propagation of Singularities for Operators of Real Principal Type)
Let P ∈Ψm(M) be a properly-supported pseudodifferential operator of real principal type
and u ∈D′(M). Then WF(u)\WF(Pu) ⊂char(P) and WF(u)\WF(Pu) is invariant under
the Hamiltonian flow.
This theorem is due to Duistermaat-Hörmander [126, Thm. 6.1.1] (see also [254, Thm. 26.1.1]).
The idea of the proof is to use Fourier integral operators to conjugate the operator P in a mi-
crolocal sense to the operator −i∂t for which the claim is obvious. An alternative proof, based
on positive commutator arguments, can, for instance, be found in [240, Chap. 8].
Example A.53. As an example, consider the Beltrami d’Alembertian □= gαβ∇α∇β on a
Lorentzian manifold (M, g). Its principal symbol is given by σ□(x, ξ) = gαβ
x ξαξβ. The corre-
sponding characteristic set is therefore the dual light cone, i.e.
char(□) = {(x, ξ) ∈T∗M\{0} | gαβ
x ξαξβ = 0} .
The operator □is clearly of real principal type. The Hamiltonian equations associated with the
principal symbol σ□reduce to the geodesic equations ¨xγ + Γγ
αβ ˙xα ˙xβ = 0 with velocity vector
ξα = ˙xα. Hence, the wavefront set of any solution to the wave equation is a union of cotangent
vectors along lightlike geodesics.
▲

A.3. Unbounded Operators and Spectral Theory

A.3
Unbounded Operators and Spectral Theory
While the previous two parts of the appendix focused on microlocal analysis, we now turn to
a different topic. More precisely, we provide brief definitions of some important concepts from
functional and spectral analysis that were used in our discussion of the Hodge decomposition
in Section 3.2. This section does not aim to be exhaustive, rather its purpose is to present the
definitions employed, thereby ensuring that the thesis remains self-contained.
Throughout this section, let (H1, ⟨·, ·⟩H1) and (H2, ⟨·, ·⟩H2) be two (complex) Hilbert spaces.
A (linear, densely-defined) operator is a C-linear map A: dom(A) →H2 whose domain dom(A) ⊂
H1 is a dense linear subspace. We recall the following definitions and basic facts:
(i) For two densely-defined operators A: dom(A) →H2 and B: dom(B) →H2, we write A ⊂B
if dom(A) ⊂dom(B) and B|dom(A) = A. Furthermore, if not explicitly stated otherwise,
we equip the operators A + B and AB with the domains dom(A + B) = dom(A) ∩dom(B)
and dom(AB) = {ψ ∈dom(B) | Bψ ∈dom(A)}. We stress that neither dom(A + B) nor
dom(AB) is dense in general and they might as well be empty.
(ii) The adjoint of a densely-defined operator A is the operator A† : dom(A†) →H1 with
dom(A†) := {ψ ∈H2 | ∃η ∈H1 : ⟨ψ, Aφ⟩H2 = ⟨η, φ⟩H1 for all φ ∈dom(A)} ⊂H2 ,
in which case we set A†ψ := η. Note that dom(A†) is not necessarily dense and might in
fact even be trivial. If H := H1 = H2, we call A symmetric if ⟨Aψ, φ⟩H = ⟨ψ, Aφ⟩H for all
ψ, φ ∈dom(A). In this case, A ⊂A∗. If A = A†, i.e. if in addition dom(A†) = dom(A), we
call A self-adjoint.
(iii) For a densely-defined operator A, we denote by G(A) := {(ψ, Aψ) | ψ ∈dom(A)} ⊂H1⊕H2
its graph.
If there exists an operators whose graph is given by the closure G(A), we
call A closable. In this case, there exists exactly one operator A: dom(A) →H2 with
G(A) = G(A), which is called the closure of A. The domain dom(A) can be characterised as
the completion of dom(A) with respect to the graph norm ∥ψ∥G(A) := (∥ψ∥2
H1 +∥Aψ∥2
H2)
1
2
defined for ψ ∈dom(A).
One can show that A is closable if and only if its adjoint
is densely-defined in which case it holds that A = (A†)†.
There is yet another useful
characterisation of the closure: A is closable if and only if for every ω ∈H1 and every
pair of sequences (ψn)n∈N and (φn)n∈N in dom(A) with ψn →ω and φn →ω as n →∞
and with the property that (Aψn)n∈N and (Aφn)n∈N are convergent in H2 it holds that
limn→∞Aψn = limn→∞Aφn. As a consequence, dom(A) can also be characterised as
dom(A) = {ψ ∈H1 | ∃(ψn)n∈N ∈dom(A)N with
lim
n→∞ψn = ψ : (Aψn)n∈N is convergent}
and the closure is given by Aψ := limn→∞Aψn for all ψ ∈dom(A) with (ψn)n∈N as above.
(iv) A densely-defined and closable operator A is called essentially self-adjoint, if its closure A
is self-adjoint. It is not too hard to see that the adjoint A† of any densely-defined operator
A is closed. Furthermore, it holds that
A
† = ((A†)†)† = A† = A† .
Hence, essentially self-adjointness is equivalent to the condition A† = A. If A is essentially
self-adjoint, one can show that A is the unique self-adjoint extension of A.
Having introduced the most important terminology for (not necessarily bounded) operators,
let us briefly discuss the spectral theorem. Let A: dom(A) →H with dom(A) ⊂H be a densely-
defined operator on some C-Hilbert space (H, ⟨·, ·⟩H). We denote the resolvent set of A by
ρ(A) := {λ ∈C | (A −λ id): dom(A) →H is bijective with bounded inverse } .


## Page 242


222
Appendix
The spectrum of A is then defined to be the complement in C, i.e. σ(A) := C\ρ(A). In the
finite-dimensional case, in which A can be identified with a Hermitian matrix, σ(A) is exactly
the set of all eigenvalues of A. More generally, σ(A) has both a discrete and continuous part.
Moreover, we recall that σ(A) is always a closed subset of C. If A is bounded, then σ(A) is
in fact even a compact set. Last but not least, it is well known that a closed and symmetric
densely-defined operator A is self-adjoint if and only if σ(A) ⊂R.
Remark A.54. If A is not closed, then it is easy to show that σ(A) = C. Hence, it is only
useful to study the spectrum for closed operators. Now, if A is closed, the closed graph theorem
implies that the inverse of (A −λ id) (if it exists) is automatically bounded and hence, the
addition “with bounded inverse” is redundant in the above definition of ρ(A) in this case.
▲
Now, let X be any topology space and denote its Borel σ-algebra by B(X). A projection-
valued measure (PVM), or spectral measure, is a map µ: B(X) →P(H), where P(H) denotes
the subset of L(H) := {T: H →H | linear and bounded} consisting of orthogonal projectors,
with the following two properties:
(i)
µ(∅) = 0
and

A.4. Linear Maps between Quotient Spaces

The assignment f 7→f(A) is usually called the spectral calculus of A.
For f, g Borel-
measurable, it has the following properties:
(i) f(A) is bounded if and only if f ∈L∞(σ(A), µA). In this case, ∥f(A)∥op = ∥f∥∞.
(ii) f(A)∗= f(A). In particular, f(A) is self-adjoint if and only if f is real-valued.
(iii) (f + g)(A) = f(A) + g(A) and dom(f(A) + g(A)) = dom(f(A)) ∩dom(g(A)).
(iv) (fg)(A) = f(A)g(A) and dom(f(A)g(A)) = dom(g(A)) ∩dom((fg)(A)).
(v) If f ̸= 0 (almost everywhere), then f(A) is invertible and f(A)−1 = (1/f)(A).
(vi) If f(λ) ≥0 (almost everywhere), then f(A) ≥0.
(vii) If f is continuous, then σ(f(A)) = f(σ(A)) where f(σ(A)) := {f(λ) | λ ∈σ(A)}.
If 1: 1 7→1 denotes the constant 1-function, then 1(A) = idH. Furthermore, idσ(A)(A) = A
and for a polynomial p(λ) = Pn
i=0 αnλn, we obtain p(A) = Pn
i=0 αnAn. As a consequence of
the above properties, we observe that the assignment ΦA : C0(σ(A), C) →L(H), f 7→f(A) is
a continuous unital ∗-algebra homomorphism. For more details on the spectral theorem and
proofs of the aforementioned statements, we refer to [332,400,444]. Last but not least, we note
that the spectral theorem can be generalised to (unbounded) normal operators.
A.4
Linear Maps between Quotient Spaces
In this section, we characterise linear maps and isomorphisms between quotient spaces of
vector spaces„ which is particularly useful for establishing isomorphisms between the various
phase spaces introduced in Section 2.1.
• Throughout the following, let V1, V2 be vector spaces over the same field F and W1 ⊂
V1, W2 ⊂V2 be arbitrary linear subspaces thereof.
Now, proving whether a linear map f : V1 →V2 induces a well-defined isomorphism between
the quotient spaces V1/W1 and V2/W2 amounts to check the following conditions.
Proposition A.56. Let f : V1 →V2 be a F-linear map. If f(W1) ⊂W2, then f induces a
well-defined F-linear map
[f] : V1
W1
→V2
W2
defined by [f]([v]) := [f(v)] for all v ∈V1. Furthermore, the following holds:
(i) The map [f] is injective if and only if f−1(W2) ⊂W1.
(ii) The map [f] is surjective if and only if V2 = f(V1) + W2.
Proof. We first show that [f] is well-defined. If v1 and v2 are two representatives of the same
equivalence class in V1/W1, then v1 −v2 ∈W1. By assumption, f(W1) ⊂W2, which implies
that W2 ∋f(v1 −v2) = f(v1) −f(v2), i.e. [f(v1)] = [f(v2)]. We now turn to injectivity and
surjectivity.
(i) By definition, [f] is injective if and only if [f(v)] = 0 implies [v] = 0 for all v ∈V1. This
is equivalent to saying that f(v) ∈W2 implies v ∈W1. This, in turn, is clearly equivalent
to the inclusion f−1(W2) ⊂W1, recalling that f−1(W2) := {v ∈V1 | f(v) ∈W2}.


## Page 244


224
Appendix
(ii) The map [f] is surjective if and only if for every v2 ∈V2, there exists a v1 ∈V1 such that
[f(v1)] = [v2]. In other words, surjectivity is equivalent to saying that for every v2 ∈V2,
there exists a v1 ∈V1 and a w ∈W2 such that v2 −f(v1) = w. Hence, [f] is surjective if
and only if we can write every v2 ∈V2 as v2 = f(v1) + w for some v1 ∈V1 and w ∈W2.
■
Remark A.57. Note that f(W1) ⊂W2 implies W1 ⊂f−1(W2). Hence, if the quotient map [f]
is injective, we in fact have f−1(W2) = W1.
▲
